# Parameter graph specification

Revision 0.6, 2026-10-11. This document is the contract for the `params` module of `exogen-core`. Every node the user can see or set is listed here with its unit, kind, default or derivation, and the source of the relation. The formula appendix at the end gives the relations by number. Values marked *fit* are calibrated against the reference bodies during the phase that implements the stage; the derivation form is fixed here, the constants are not.

## Implementation status

Phase 1 implements the graph mechanism and the groups star, orbit, body, rotation, interior, atmosphere, volatiles, climate (zero-dimensional subset), cratering (scaling subset), detail, output, seeds and multipliers, plus `tectonics.dichotomy_mode`, `tectonics.dichotomy_amplitude` and `tectonics.initial_crust_thickness`. Phase 2 adds the cratering population nodes (`cratering.loop_threshold`, `cratering.min_diameter`, `cratering.retention_age`, `cratering.empirical_saturation`, `cratering.secondary_fraction`, `cratering.oblique_fraction`), `surface.space_weathering_timescale`, `surface.solidification_time`, the flux history nodes `cratering.early_flux_ratio` and `cratering.early_flux_decay`, and the material controls `materials.bedrock`, `materials.regolith_mix`, `materials.deep_bedrock`, `materials.deep_bedrock_depth` and `materials.colour_space`. The impact flux is written in time since formation, so the crater record begins at the solidification time whatever the body's age; craters carry an impact angle; and each preset carries its own master seed. The executable table is `crates/exogen-core/src/params/registry.rs`; every constant lives in `crates/exogen-core/src/constants.rs` with its source, and constants marked provisional there are calibrated in the phase named. Phase 3 adds the thermal history curves (`interior.heat_flux_history`, `interior.mantle_temperature_history`, `interior.lithosphere_history`, `interior.relief_limit_history`, `interior.contraction_history`, `orbit.eccentricity_history`), `volcanism.rate`, `volcanism.resurfacing_rate` and `volcanism.history`, the contraction nodes `tectonics.contraction_strain` and `tectonics.scarp_total_shortening`, the plume nodes `tectonics.plume_rise_count`, `tectonics.plume_rise_diameter`, `tectonics.plume_rise_height` and `tectonics.rift_extension`, the episodic-lid nodes `tectonics.resurfacing_event_age` and `tectonics.tessera_fraction`, the ice-shell nodes `interior.love_number_h2`, `tectonics.tidal_stress`, `tectonics.ice_crack_density` and `tectonics.nsr_period` (Phase 4), the Io-regime node `tectonics.mountain_count`, the volcanism counts `volcanism.shield_count`, `volcanism.corona_count`, `volcanism.dome_count` and `volcanism.paterae_count`, `volcanism.province_count`, and `materials.lava`. Phase 4 adds the orbital-history nodes `orbit.resonance_age`, `orbit.resonance_eccentricity` and `orbit.resonance_duration` with `interior.tidal_flux_history`, the partial-resurfacing nodes `tectonics.polar_thinning`, `tectonics.ice_resurfaced_fraction`, `tectonics.ice_resurfacing_age` and `tectonics.ice_resurfacing_duration`, `tectonics.chaos_fraction`, `volcanism.cryodome_count`, `volcanism.plume_fallout_rate`, `materials.salt` and `surface.trailing_irradiation`. The fourth revision of Phase 4 derives `atmosphere.bond_albedo` from the surface (F52) with lagged dependencies, marks the composition, inventory and rotation nodes as inputs, so that no reference preset pins a derived node, and adds the focusing-episode nodes `tectonics.focusing_period` and `tectonics.focusing_episode_count` (F53). Phase 4F, the fidelity of the base, adds the sub-grid crater roughness (F54) and the Gaussian pyramid under the renewed terrains, the structure of the focused heating and the local polar folds (F43), variation within units (F55) and Io's accumulated deposits (F56). The remaining tectonics, volcanism, weather, surface-process and material nodes are added by the phases that implement those stages. A test asserts that every derivation reads only the nodes it declares, and `exogen validate` checks the derived values of the sixteen reference presets against measured values. The sixty-ninth milestone adds the thermal-orbital nodes `orbit.resonance_heating`, `orbit.resonance_forced` and `interior.ocean_age` (F138, F140) and the reference preset Mimas.

## Conventions

- Node ids are `group.name`, snake case.
- Units are SI unless the unit column says otherwise. Display units may differ (AU, bar, Gyr, km) and are converted at the UI boundary only.
- **Kinds**: `root` (an input with a default, no derivation), `derived` (computed from upstream nodes; shown dimmed; can be set, which turns it into an override), `expert` (derived or root, hidden unless expert mode is on), `multiplier` (an artistic scale factor, default 1, always visible, never derived), `curve` (a piecewise-linear history over simulated time; its default is a constant or a derived curve), `list` (a user-editable table of records).
- Every derived node records the ids it was derived from, so the UI can show them on hover.
- Setting a derived node pins it. Downstream nodes keep deriving from the pinned value. "Reset to derived" clears the pin. A derived node marked *input* (what the body is made of, its volatile inventory, its rotation state) is a default a preset is expected to set: setting it gives an input, not a pinned outcome, and the reference presets set nothing else. A node may read *lagged* dependencies from the previous recompute pass, so that a feedback (the surface's reflectance sets the temperature that sets the surface) stays acyclic; a recompute repeats its pass until nothing changes, four passes at most.
- Ranges are soft: values outside them raise a plausibility warning, never an error.
- References point to PLAN.md section 12 by author and year.

## star

| id | unit | kind | default or derivation | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| star.mass | M_sun | root | 1.0; range 0.08 to 3 | |
| star.luminosity | L_sun | derived | F1 mass–luminosity relation, then F3 age brightening | Eker et al. 2018; Gough 1981 |
| star.radius | R_sun | derived | F2 | Eker et al. 2018 |
| star.teff | K | derived | Stefan–Boltzmann from luminosity and radius | |
| star.spectral_type | text | derived | Lookup from teff; display only | |
| star.age | Gyr | root | 4.57; warn if above star.main_sequence_lifetime | |
| star.main_sequence_lifetime | Gyr | derived | 10 x mass / luminosity_zams | |
| star.luminosity_history | L_sun | curve | Up to 1.4 solar masses the BHAC15 tracks' share of the present luminosity (F260), a heavier star F3; LUMINOSITY_HISTORY_POINTS + 1 times crowded toward the body's start | Baraffe et al. 2015; Gough 1981 |
| star.xuv_relative | ratio | derived | F4, F261: the star's light times its XUV share by its mass and age, relative to the present Sun at 1 AU, scaled by 1/a² at the body | Ribas et al. 2005; Jackson et al. 2012; Wright et al. 2018; Pass et al. 2024 |
| star.xuv_history | ratio | curve | F261: star.xuv_relative at each time of star.luminosity_history | as above |

## orbit

| id | unit | kind | default or derivation | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| orbit.primary | enum star, planet | root | star | |
| orbit.semi_major_axis | m | root | 1 AU; display AU for a star primary, parent radii for a planet primary | |
| orbit.eccentricity | 1 | root; curve allowed | 0.0167 | |
| orbit.resonance_age | Gyr | root | 0: how long before the present an orbital resonance held the eccentricity up (F50); Ganymede's preset 2.2, placed so that its lanes, trailing the resonance, are two gigayears old (F138); Miranda's 1.0; Mimas's 0.0417, its 3:1 resonance with Dione from 83 Myr ago | Showman and Malhotra 1997; Zahnle et al. 2003; Meyer and Wisdom 2008 |
| orbit.resonance_eccentricity | 1 | root | 0: the eccentricity the resonance held (F50); for an icy moon the resonance supplies what that eccentricity gives a warm moon, as the dynamics that found it assumed of the moon (F138); Ganymede's preset 0.0185, Miranda's 0.0165 | Showman and Malhotra 1997; Ćuk et al. 2020 |
| orbit.resonance_duration | Gyr | root | 0: the episode's full width at its base (F50); a free icy moon escapes no later than its own dissipation lets its eccentricity decay to the present value (F138); Ganymede's preset 1.0 | Showman and Malhotra 1997 |
| orbit.resonance_heating | W/m² | root | 0: the heat a past resonance supplied an icy moon's eccentricity, which rises until the moon dissipates as much (F138); 0 to take what orbit.resonance_eccentricity gives a warm moon; Mimas's preset 0.0328, Meyer and Wisdom 2008's equilibrium heating for the Mimas-Dione 3:1 resonance scaled to Saturn's dissipation as Lainey et al. 2017 measure it | Meyer and Wisdom 2007, 2008; Lainey et al. 2017 |
| orbit.resonance_forced | bool | root | false: whether a resonance holds the present eccentricity (F138); the presets of Io, Europa, Ganymede (the Laplace resonance) and Enceladus (its 2:1 with Dione) true; a free eccentricity decays by the moon's own dissipation | Meyer and Wisdom 2008 |
| orbit.cycle_period | Gyr | root | 0: the period of a resonance's thermal-orbital cycle (F77); Io's and Europa's presets 0.12 | Bagheri et al. 2026 |
| orbit.cycle_onset | Gyr | root | 0: how long before the present the cycle began (F77); Io's and Europa's presets 1.6 | Bagheri et al. 2026 |
| orbit.cycle_amplitude | 1 | root | 0: the eccentricity's swing through the cycle about its mean, as a share of the mean (F77); Io's preset 0.18, Europa's 0.25 | Bagheri et al. 2026 |
| orbit.cycle_phase | 1 | root | 0: the share of a period since the eccentricity last peaked (F77); Io's and Europa's presets 0.2 | Bagheri et al. 2026 |
| orbit.eccentricity_history | 1 | curve | an icy moon locked to its planet: evolved with its interior (F138), held by a resonance that holds it now, pumped by a past resonance's heat until the moon dissipates as much (capped at 0.2), otherwise free and decaying by the moon's own dissipation, in steps of 0.5 Myr, runs of one value kept by their ends; any other body: the mean about which the present eccentricity stands, with the pulse of the resonance episode (F50), rising over its first fifth, holding, falling over its last, and from its onset the resonance's cycle, 1 + A cos 2π(t − t_peak)/P, grown to its full swing over its first period, at 16 points a period (F77); constant without either | Phase 3, Phase 4 |
| orbit.period | s | derived | F5 Kepler, primary mass plus body mass | |
| orbit.mean_motion | rad/s | derived | 2π / period | |
| orbit.insolation | W/m² | derived | L / (4π d²) where d is the distance to the star (the parent's orbit for a moon) | |
| orbit.year | s | derived | F234: the orbit about the star, the body's own or its parent's for a moon | |
| orbit.star_eccentricity | 1 | derived | F234: the eccentricity of the orbit about the star, the body's own or its parent's | |
| orbit.perihelion_season | deg | root | 282.9: the star's longitude seen from the body at perihelion, 0 at the northern spring equinox (F234); Mars 251, Saturn's moons 280 | |
| parent.mass | kg | root (planet primary) | 1 M_jup | |
| parent.radius | m | root (planet primary) | 1 R_jup | |
| parent.semi_major_axis | m | root (planet primary) | 5.2 AU | |
| parent.eccentricity | 1 | root (planet primary) | 0.05 | |

| rotation.primordial_period | s | derived | F238: the spin the body was born with, 0.35 of its breakup rate for a held spin, the period a free spin's history runs back to | Kokubo and Ida 2007 |
| rotation.period_history | curve | derived | F238: the sidereal period through the body's life | |

| rotation.regime | enum rapid, rhines, slow | derived | F11 from the equatorial Rossby deformation radius against the radius | Haqq-Misra et al. 2018 |

| interior.love_number_h2 | 1 | derived | 1 + k2 for a fluid-like rocky interior; for an ice shell over an ocean 1 / (1 − ξ2 + Λ), ξ2 = 3 ρ_ocean / (5 ρ̄), Λ = 8 (1 + ν) / (5 + ν) μ d / (ρ_ocean g R²) (F75: Europa 1.16, Ganymede 1.34, Enceladus 0.046); for ice frozen to the rock (5/2) / (1 + 19 μ / (2 ρ̄ g R)) | Beuthe 2018 |
| interior.dissipation_q | 1 | expert derived | 100 rock, 30 ice, 12 with surface ocean | |

| interior.shell_tidal_flux | W/m² | derived | The tides an icy moon's shell dissipates at the thickness its thermal-orbital history leaves it (F75, F138): Europa 13 mW/m², Ganymede 0.1, Enceladus 0.8, Mimas 13 | Ojakangas and Stevenson 1989; Nimmo et al. 2007 |
| interior.core_porosity | 1 | derived | mean porosity of the rocky interior under the H2O layer, 0.4 x exp(-P / 60 MPa) at each depth by volume (F68); Enceladus 0.23, the large moons none | Sclater and Christie 1980 |
| interior.core_tidal_power | W | derived | F13 on the interior's radius with an imaginary Love number 1.6 x (porosity - 0.05) (F68, F75), for an icy moon only while its pores hold water (F138); Enceladus 28.5 GW, Mimas and Miranda none | Choblet et al. 2017 |
| interior.tidal_flux | W/m² | derived | tidal_power / (4π R²) | |
| interior.secular_cooling_fraction | 1 | expert derived | 1.0 for R above 3000 km falling to 0.2 at 500 km, times radiogenic flux | fit |
| interior.surface_heat_flux | W/m² | derived | radiogenic plus tidal plus secular; an icy moon locked to its planet, what its shell conducts while it floats, more than is made in it while it freezes (F138: Mimas 27 against 13 made) | |
| interior.heat_flux_history | W/m² | curve | F12 and F13 (an icy moon's shell by F75) evaluated over body age with the eccentricity curve, plus secular cooling; 96 samples, or as finely as the eccentricity curve changes, up to 2,048 (F77) | Phase 3 |
| interior.tidal_flux_history | W/m² | curve | Along the eccentricity history: a rocky body's present tidal flux times (e / e_now)²; an icy moon's porous interior so, and its shell's tides recomputed at each time for the shell the heat of the time leaves (F75) | Phase 4 |
| interior.mantle_temperature_history | K | curve | Thermal evolution over the body's life, ending at interior.mantle_temperature | Phase 3 |
| interior.lithosphere_history | m | curve | F14 along the heat-flux history | Phase 3 |
| interior.relief_limit_history | m | curve | F15 scaled by the elastic thickness relative to today's, floored at 0.15 | Phase 3 |
| interior.contraction_history | m | curve | F16 accumulated over the body's life | Phase 3 |
| interior.mantle_temperature | K | expert derived | Thermal evolution, calibrated so Earth is 1600 K now | fit |
| interior.lithosphere_thickness | m | derived | F14 conductive thickness | |
| interior.elastic_thickness | m | derived | Depth to the 923 K (650 °C) isotherm for rock, 170 K for ice, from the conductive profile (F144) | |
| interior.crust_strength | Pa | derived | 150 MPa rock, weakened linearly to 50 MPa as surface temperature approaches 750 K; 5 MPa ice | fit |
| interior.max_relief | m | derived | F15 | |
| interior.contraction_total | m | derived | F16 at the present | Byrne et al. 2014 |
| interior.magma_production_rate | m³/s | derived | F17 | fit |
| interior.ice_shell_thickness | m | derived | F18 for rock_ice and ice_dominated bodies; an icy moon locked to its planet, the thickness its thermal-orbital history leaves (F138) | |
| interior.has_subsurface_ocean | bool | derived | Ice layer thicker than the conductive shell thickness | |
| interior.ocean_degree | 1 | derived | How far an ice shell floats on its ocean (F231): a smoothstep of the ocean's share of the ice layer from 0, the shell reaching the rock, to 1 at 0.3 of it (OCEAN_SHARE_WIDTH). The cryodomes and the cryolava floods go as far as it is 0, and the deep ice from the crust's mixture to clean ice by it. The ocean bodies 1, Triton's ocean the least at 0.38 of its layer; the frozen bodies 0 | stated width |
| interior.ocean_age | Gyr | expert derived | How long the shell has floated on an ocean without freezing through, up to the present (F138, F140): an icy moon's from its history, Mimas 0.033; any other body's its age when it has an ocean now, else 0 | |
| interior.tectonic_regime | enum mobile_lid, episodic_lid, stagnant_lid, ice_shell_ocean, ice_shell_rock | derived | F19; episodic where its mantle has broken a dry rocky lid, however long ago (F121, F230) | |
| interior.episodic_degree | 1 | derived | How far a lid that has broken is episodic now (F230): a smoothstep of the time since its last overturn from 1 within 0.4 of the body's age to 0 past 0.6; 0 on a lid that never broke. Sets the melt efficiency geometrically between the stagnant lid's and the episodic lid's, the rises' width and the domes. Venus 1 | stated width |

| tectonics.plate_speed_scale | m/s | derived | 5 cm/yr x (surface_heat_flux / 0.087)^0.5 | heuristic |
| tectonics.continental_fraction_initial | 1 | derived | 0.1 mobile lid; grows in the loop | |
| tectonics.hotspot_count | 1 | derived | 10 x (area / Earth area) | heuristic |
| tectonics.reorganisation_interval | Myr | expert | 150 | |
| tectonics.contraction_strain | 1 | derived | 2 x contraction_total / radius; the scarps take this fraction of the area up as shortening, the longest of them scaling with its square (F35) | Phase 3 |
| tectonics.scarp_total_shortening | m | derived | contraction_strain x 2π R | Phase 3 |
| tectonics.plume_rise_count | 1 | derived | Stagnant or episodic lid at least 3,000 km in radius: floor(surface_heat_flux / 0.02 W/m²), at most 3; else 0. Mars 1, Venus 3 | Phase 3, heuristic |
| tectonics.plume_rise_diameter | m | derived | 1.5 R on a stagnant lid (Tharsis), 0.4 R on an episodic lid (Beta Regio), geometrically between them by the episodic degree (F230) | Phase 3, fit |
| tectonics.plume_rise_height | m | derived | 0.28 x max_relief (Tharsis 7 km, Beta Regio about 3 km) | Phase 3, fit |
| tectonics.rift_extension | m | derived | 4 x plume_rise_height when there is a rise: the extension across a rise's rift zone (Valles Marineris some 30 km on Tharsis's 8 km; Devana Chasma 10 to 20 km on Beta Regio's 4 km), taken up by a population of troughs from chasmata to graben drawn until it is spent (F38) | Phase 3, Andrews-Hanna 2012 |
| tectonics.resurfacing_event_age | Gyr | derived | The time since a broken lid's last overturn, which resurfaced it (F121, F230): 0.66 on Venus; 0 on a lid that never broke | Phase 3 |
| tectonics.tessera_fraction | 1 | derived | 0.08 on a lid that has broken (F230): the highest-standing share of the surface, measured without the plume rises' domes, that survives the event; it stands as crustal plateaus 2 km above the plains | Phase 3 |
| tectonics.mountain_count | 1 | derived | Io regime: 2 per TW of tidal power, some 150 on Io | Phase 3, heuristic |
| tectonics.tidal_stress | Pa | derived | The largest tension the diurnal tide puts on a locked moon's uniform shell through an orbit (F41); Europa 82 kPa, Enceladus 20 kPa (Beuthe 2018: about 15 kPa in a uniform 23 km shell), Ganymede 5 kPa; a hard shell thinned is stressed the more (F76) | Phase 4 |
| tectonics.ice_crack_density | per m | derived | On a shell over an ocean whose tidal tension where it is thinnest (F76) exceeds 40 kPa: 12 x (tidal_stress / 1 MPa) / shell thickness, the cracks a transect crosses per metre (Europa one per 31 km); else 0 | Phase 4, fit |
| tectonics.nsr_period | Gyr | derived | Period of a cracking shell's rotation against the tidal axis: 2 x cratering.retention_age, so the lineae turn half a turn over the surface's age and the zones of tension sweep every longitude; 0 without cracks | Phase 4, heuristic |
| tectonics.polar_thinning | 1 | derived | Share of the shell's thickness lost under one pole where the tidal dissipation focuses (F43): 0.8 x min(tidal_flux / 0.02 W/m², 1) x (1 − exp(−(d / R) / 0.03)) on a shell over an ocean, else 0, by the share of what the shell conducts that is made in it and grown over its conductive time d² / κ since its ocean formed (F140); Enceladus about 0.75, Europa 0.2, Ganymede 0, Mimas 0.21. Which pole is drawn from seed.tectonics | Phase 4, fit |
| tectonics.focusing_period | Gyr | derived | 40 x d² / κ (d the shell thickness, κ 1.2e-6 m²/s) where the ocean reaches the surface through the thinned shell (tectonics.crack_reach at least 1, F228), else 0: how often the focused heating returns (F53); Enceladus 0.53 Gyr | O'Neill and Nimmo 2010; fit |
| tectonics.crack_reach | 1 | derived | How far the cracks the tides open from above at the thinnest shell reach toward the water a crack from the ocean holds, which stands below the surface by the thinned shell x (1 − ρ_ice / ρ_water): the tidal stress there over ρ_ice g, against that depth (F228); ocean water reaches the surface past 1, where the episodes and plumes begin. Enceladus 1.74, Mimas 0.86, Europa 0.04; 0 off a shell over an ocean | Crawford and Stevenson 1988 |
| tectonics.focusing_episode_count | 1 | derived | floor((body.age − 0.01 Gyr) / focusing_period): the past episodes, each 10 Myr long, one every period before the present one (F53); Enceladus 8 | O'Neill and Nimmo 2010 |
| tectonics.ice_resurfaced_fraction | 1 | derived | Share of a shell over an ocean renewed as lanes of grooved terrain (F44, F140): min(1, ∫ (min(tidal, heat flux) − 0.005 W/m²)⁺ dt / (0.0045 W/m² x 0.56 Gyr)) x (1 − polar_thinning / 0.8), clipped at 0.95: the tidal heat the lid carried above the floor over the shell's life, against the heat calibrated on Ganymede, whose resonance episode renews 0.69; Callisto none, Enceladus's steady 37 mW/m² (a renewal saturated at one, then the focusing's share) 0.05, Mimas's young ocean 0.054, its lid yielding in compression through most of it (F225); times one less the shell's thin degree, a thin shell taking chaos instead (F227) | Phase 4, fit |
| tectonics.ice_resurfacing_age | Gyr | derived | body.age less the mean time of the tidal flux history above the 0.005 W/m² floor, weighted by the excess (the history's samples are uniform in time): the peak of Ganymede's pulse, 2.0 Gyr; the middle of the age for a steady flux; 0 without lanes | Phase 4 |
| tectonics.ice_resurfacing_duration | Gyr | derived | sqrt(12) times the excess-weighted standard deviation of that time, the width of the uniform episode with the same spread, over which the lanes' times are drawn about the resurfacing age so that younger lanes cut older ones: Ganymede 0.65 Gyr, a steady flux the whole age | Phase 4 |
| tectonics.chaos_fraction | 1 | derived | Share of a shell over an ocean disrupted into chaos (F45, F80): 0.3 x clipped (tidal_flux / 0.012 W/m²) x the shell's thin degree, a smoothstep from 1 at 0.02 R to 0 at 0.03 R (F227); Europa 0.3, thick shells 0 | Phase 4, fit |
| tectonics.focused_heating_pole | superseded | | Replaced by tectonics.polar_thinning; which pole is a coin tossed from seed.tectonics | Phase 4 |
| tectonics.nonsynchronous_rotation | 1 | expert | 0 | |

## volcanism

| id | unit | kind | default or derivation | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| volcanism.rate | m³/s | derived | interior.magma_production_rate | Phase 3 |
| volcanism.resurfacing_rate | m/Myr | derived | rate / area | Phase 3 |
| volcanism.style | enum none, silicate, sulfur_silicate, cryo_water_ammonia, cryo_brine | derived | By composition class and heat flux; sulfur_silicate above 1 W/m² on rock | |
| volcanism.province_count | 1 | derived | Magmatic provinces the eruptions favour: the plume rises first, then one random region per 2,000 km of radius, Gaussian 40° wide, the rest of the body at 3% weight; Moon 1 (the Procellarum terrane), Mercury 1, Mars 2, Venus 3 | Phase 3, heuristic |
| volcanism.cryodome_count | 1 | derived | Domes of brine-rich ice a shell over rock has built over its life (F46), as far as no ocean floats it (F231): 0.7 of the ice magma budget (volcanism.rate x age x (1 - interior.ocean_degree)) in domes of 1e-6 to 3e-5 R³ log-uniform; Ceres about 20, Pluto about 15, most relaxed away. Informative; the finish draws them from the budget per time bin | Phase 4, Sori et al. 2018 |
| volcanism.plume_fallout_rate | m/Gyr | derived | Fresh ice a polar plume lays down at its pole (F47): 1e5 x clipped (tidal_flux / 0.02 W/m²) x clipped ((crack_reach − 1) / 0.5) where the ocean reaches the surface and the shell cracks (F228); Enceladus 1e5 (0.1 mm/yr near the stripes, Kempf et al. 2010); else 0 | Phase 4 |
| volcanism.shield_count | 1 | derived | 5 on the main plume rise, half as many on each further rise; hotspot_count for a mobile lid in Phase 6 | Phase 3, heuristic |
| volcanism.corona_count | 1 | derived | A rock lid's diapirs that melt under it and break it in an annulus (F145): some 750 on Venus, none on Mars or the Moon; an ice mantle's upwellings (F133) | F145 |
| volcanism.dome_count | 1 | derived | Episodic lid: 3.3e-13 per m² x the episodic degree (F230), some 150 on Venus | Phase 3, heuristic |
| volcanism.arc_volcano_density | per m of arc | derived | 1 per 70 km of convergent boundary | |
| volcanism.flood_episode_count | 1 | derived | From the heat flux history: one per 10 Myr of rate above threshold, capped | fit |
| volcanism.paterae_count | 1 | derived | Io regime: 5.4 per TW of tidal power, some 400 on Io | Phase 3, heuristic |
| volcanism.fresh_lava_albedo | 1 | derived | 0.07 basalt, 0.4 cryolava, 0.1 sulfur-coated | |
| volcanism.active_vent_count | 1 | derived | Vents younger than 1 Myr at the output epoch | |
| volcanism.history | m³/s | curve | F17 along the thermal history; the extrusive share erupts as floods (F36) | Phase 3 |

## cratering

| id | unit | kind | default or derivation | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| cratering.snow_line_position | 1 | derived | The body's distance from its star over the distance where a blackbody is 170 K, 2.69 AU for the Sun, from its insolation: the Moon 0.37, Mars 0.57, Ceres 1.03, Jupiter 1.94, Saturn 3.56, Uranus 7.1 (F135) | |
| cratering.late_flux_multiplier | x | root | 1: the steady flux after the early bombardment against the Solar System's at the same place against the snow line, for the belts and giant planets the engine does not model; the early bombardment is kept (F135) | stated choice |
| cratering.production_function | enum neukum_lunar, neukum_mars | derived | The rocky leftovers' craters: lunar inside 0.447 of the snow line (1.2 AU for the Sun), Mars-type beyond (F135) | Neukum et al. 2001; Ivanov 2001 |
| cratering.flux_relative_to_moon | 1 | derived | The rocky leftovers' flux per unit area: the Moon's inside 0.40 of the snow line, twice it from 0.57 (Mars), fading out from 1.2 to 1.6, times the late flux multiplier (F135) | Ivanov 2001; Zahnle et al. 2003 |
| cratering.icy_flux | /km²/Gyr | derived | The outer system's comets of 1 km or more today: 4.7e-7 unfocused inside 3.56 of the snow line, rising as its square beyond, times the planet's or the body's focusing, from the snow line out; Enceladus 1.1e-5, Ganymede 4.5e-6 (F135) | Nesvorný et al. 2023 |
| cratering.icy_crater_1km | km | derived | The crater a comet of 1 km makes: pi-scaling with the strength of cold ice (35 MPa) or rock (7.6 MPa), then the cavity's collapse (F136). Mimas 18, Enceladus 17, Ganymede 19, Miranda 12, Pluto 5 | Holsapple and Housen 2007; Holsapple 2022; Bottke et al. 2024 |
| cratering.debris_velocity | m/s | derived | The speed a moon's debris strikes it at: 0.3 times its orbital speed about its planet (F136) | stated choice |
| cratering.debris_fragments | /km² | derived | Fragments of 2 km or more striking a moon at once after the last impact it could not hold: 3.5e-4 where the comets strike at 25 km/s, as the square of the impact speed elsewhere; none where the record starts with the crust (F136) | Bottke et al. 2024; fitted on Mimas, stated choice |
| cratering.icy_early_total | Gyr of steady flux | expert | The comets' early bombardment in all: 2475 around the Sun, times M/L, over the late flux multiplier (F135) | Wong et al. 2023; Nesvorný et al. 2023 |
| cratering.icy_early_timescale | Gyr | expert | Of the stretched exponential exp(-(t/τ)^0.317): 1.6 Myr around the Sun, times (L/L☉)^0.75 (M/M☉)^-0.5 (F135) | Wong et al. 2023 |
| cratering.impact_velocity | m/s | derived | F29, F135 | Zahnle et al. 2003; Nesvorný et al. 2023 |
| cratering.impactor_density | kg/m³ | derived | Each population's by its share of today's craters of 1 km: 2700 rocky, 600 comets (F135) | |
| cratering.target_density | kg/m³ | derived | 2700 rock crust, 920 ice | |
| cratering.target_ice | 1 | derived | How far a crater's target is ice rather than rock (F229): 0 off ice; on ice a smoothstep of its bedrock's ice share by volume from 0.3 to 0.5, a body's dirty ice its crust's (1 − crust_impurity), clean ice 0.98. Sets the transition's ice factor, the target's strength and the largest crater geometrically between rock's and ice's, and the pit against the rings. Every icy preset 1, rocky 0 | Durham et al. 1992; stated width |
| cratering.simple_complex_transition | m | derived | F30, its ice factor by the target's ice degree (F229) | Pike 1980; Schenk 2002 |
| cratering.peak_ring_onset | m | derived | 8.5 x simple_complex_transition: a peak ring on rock, a central pit on ice (F72) | Alzate and Barlow 2011 |
| cratering.multiring_onset | m | derived | 20 x simple_complex_transition: a multi-ring basin on rock, a dome in the pit on ice (F72) | Schenk 1993 |
| cratering.morphometry_coefficients | table | expert | Pike lunar set by default; per-class overrides | Pike 1977, 1980 |
| cratering.min_diameter | m | derived | Larger of atmosphere.crater_cutoff_diameter and two export pixels | |
| cratering.early_flux_ratio | x steady | expert | The rocky leftovers' rate at formation over the steady rate: a b e^(4.5 b) / c of the chronology, about 1.57e4 (F31); around another star times M/L over (L/L☉)^0.75 (M/M☉)^-0.5, over the late flux multiplier (F135) | Neukum et al. 2001 |
| cratering.early_flux_decay | Gyr | expert | 1 / b of the chronology, 0.144 Gyr (F31), times (L/L☉)^0.75 (M/M☉)^-0.5 (F135) | Neukum et al. 2001 |
| cratering.record_start | Gyr after formation | derived | When the crust solidifies, or the median time of the last impact too large for the body to hold (a crater as wide as an icy body's radius, as a rocky body's hemisphere, F168, between them by the target's ice degree, F229), whichever is later (F135) | Movshovitz et al. 2015 |
| cratering.flux_history | 1 | curve | Production of craters of 1 km over the body's life relative to the present, every population's: zero before cratering.record_start, then the early bombardment decaying into the steady flux (F31, F135) | Neukum et al. 2001; Wong et al. 2023 |
| cratering.giant_basins | list of diameter, lat, lon, age | root | empty: sampled from the production function | |
| cratering.diffusivity | m²/Myr | derived | Gardening diffusivity at the present flux: 5.5 x (gravity / 1.62)^0.5 x the body's present production of craters of 1 km over the Moon's (F135), x 10 with an atmosphere above 1 kPa; the flux history scales it back in time, so a crater's degradation follows the impacts that came after it | Fassett and Thomson 2014; fit |
| cratering.ray_fading_timescale | Gyr | derived | 1.0 on the Moon over the body's maturing rate against the Moon's (F214), at most 1.5, airless; 0.05 with an atmosphere | fit |
| cratering.rampart_ejecta | bool | derived | Regolith volatile content above threshold | |
| cratering.secondary_fraction | 1 | expert | 0.2 | |
| cratering.oblique_fraction | | retired | retired by F167: a crater's threshold angle for elongation follows its cratering efficiency. Impact angles follow sin 2θ; ejecta and rays thicken downrange below 45°, open an uprange zone of avoidance below 30° and grow side lobes below 15° | Bottke et al. 2000; Gault and Wedekind 1978 |

## materials

Composition class sets only the defaults of this group. Surface colour comes from the per-cell mixture of end-members that the processes fill: the bedrock and regolith mix as the base, volatile ices from the inventory wherever the climate makes them stable (nitrogen and methane ice over water-ice bedrock on a cold body, for example), and dust, tholin, sulfur, salt and weathering deposits from their stages. Every derived node here can be pinned, so a surface can be composed by hand as well as derived.

| id | unit | kind | default or derivation | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| materials.bedrock | enum basalt, basalt_iron_rich, basalt_iron_poor, anorthosite, norite, felsic, graphite_bearing_crust, graphite_rich_crust, impact_melt, sulfur_deposit, carbonaceous, water_ice, water_ice_dirty, ammonia_water_ice | input (derived default) | What the crust is made of, by the processes that made it: sulfur where a tidally heated body's plumes coat it, felsic where plates recycle it with water, anorthosite where the magma ocean floated plagioclase, a graphite-bearing crust where an iron-poor ocean floated graphite, otherwise the body's basalt (F125); an icy crust's dirty ice, the rock it accreted in it, or clean ice where the shell renews itself everywhere (F126). Pin to choose | F125, F126 |
| materials.regolith_mix | map end-member to fraction | input (derived default) | Baseline surface mixture before deposits: on a rocky crust its flotation crust and its basalt by their shares (F125); on an airless icy crust the crust and its lag, the carbonaceous rock its warm latitudes leave as their ice sublimes, which impact ejecta spread over the old crust, with the share of the renewed ice that impacts carry onto it (F126); otherwise the bedrock. Pin to set the surface composition by hand | F125, F126 |
| materials.deep_bedrock | enum as bedrock | input (derived default) | What large craters excavate from below the surface layer: the noritic lower crust under a flotation crust, the graphite flotation crust under a reduced body's lavas, otherwise the basalt (F125); the frozen ocean's clean ice under a shell that floats on one, the crust's own mix under one frozen to its rock, between them by interior.ocean_degree (F231), which is also the ice that flows when a crater relaxes (F126). Pin to choose | F125, F126; provisional |
| materials.lava | enum as bedrock | input (derived default) | What floods and flows leave: a rocky body's basalt, its iron from its mantle (F125); cryolava the ice its liquid freezes to, clean water ice or ammonia-water ice (F126). Pin to choose | F125, F126 |
| materials.lag_depth_share | 1 | derived | The share of the surface layer an airless icy crust's lag has taken over its age, the lag's depth by latitude against the deep bedrock's depth; a body's dirty ice shows the lag's colour fresh by this share (F126): Ceres 0.92, Callisto 0.02 | F126 |
| materials.deep_bedrock_depth | m | derived | 25 km rocky, 10 km iron-rich, at the initial crust's thickness and as much thinner as the crust under a crater is (F215); 0.5 km ice shells. A crater excavating a tenth of its transient cavity's diameter (Melosh 1989) brings up the deep bedrock in its ejecta by the share of its paraboloid cavity below this depth, landing nearest its rim, and shows it in its floor by the share of its transient cavity, 0.3 of the diameter deep, below it (F216) | Phase 2, provisional |
| materials.salt | enum hydrated_salt, carbonate_salt | input (derived default) | What an ocean's brines leave on the surface: sulfate and chloride hydrates irradiated brown-red under a Jupiter-class magnetosphere (parent over 1e27 kg); sodium carbonates white elsewhere (Ceres's faculae, Enceladus's stripes) | Phase 4 |
| materials.end_members | list | derived | The end-members active for this world: the bedrock, the regolith mix, the volatile ices present in the inventory, and the deposits the processes produce | USGS Spectral Library v7; RELAB |
| materials.custom_end_members | list | root | User-defined materials with a name, a reflectance spectrum or an RGB, roughness and specular; usable in the regolith mix | Phase 2 |
| materials.colour_space | enum linear_srgb, acescg | root | linear_srgb | |
| materials.wetness_darkening | bool | expert | true | |
| materials.vegetation_enabled | bool | derived | Phase 8: liquid water, temperature band, atmosphere | |
| materials.vegetation_pigment | linear RGB | root | chlorophyll reflectance | Phase 8 |

## Plausibility rules

Each rule is a predicate with a severity (note, warning) and an explanation that names the quantities. Rules never block.

| Rule | Severity | Explanation template |
|---|---|---|
| Liquid requested but not stable | warning | "{species} liquid requested. Surface temperature {T} K is outside its liquid range at {P} bar ({T_melt} to {T_boil} K)." |
| Atmosphere retention | warning | "Jeans parameter below 36 at the exobase temperature {T} K for {species}. These species are unlikely to survive {age} Gyr." |
| Pressure below triple point | note | "Surface pressure {P} is below the {species} triple point; no liquid can exist." |
| Locking inconsistency | warning | "Locking timescale {t} Gyr is shorter than body age {age} Gyr but rotation is pinned free." |
| Relief above strength limit | warning | "Requested relief {h} km exceeds the crustal strength limit {h_max} km." |
| Body too small for spheroid | note | "Radius {R} km is below 400 km; the body is unlikely to be spheroidal." |
| Density outside physical range | warning | "Density {ρ} is outside 900 to 8000 kg/m³." |
| Star age beyond main sequence | warning | "Star age {age} Gyr exceeds the main-sequence lifetime {t_ms} Gyr." |
| Thick atmosphere on low gravity | note | "Column mass {m} kg/m² on gravity {g} m/s² has no solar-system analogue." |
| Io regime with surviving craters pinned | note | "Resurfacing rate {r} m/Myr erases craters faster than they form; the pinned crater density will be mostly buried." |

## Formula appendix

Symbols: G gravitational constant, σ Stefan–Boltzmann, k_B Boltzmann, m_u atomic mass unit. Solar units where stated.

- **F1 Mass–luminosity.** Six-piece fit of Eker et al. 2018, log L = a log M + b (solar units), with (a, b) = (2.028, −0.976) below 0.45 M_sun, (4.572, −0.102) to 0.72, (5.743, −0.007) to 1.05, (4.329, 0.010) to 2.4, (3.967, 0.093) to 7, (2.865, 1.105) to 31; normalised so that one solar mass gives one solar luminosity. This is the reference value at the age fraction of F3.
- **F2 Mass–radius (stars).** R = 0.438 M² + 0.479 M + 0.075 for 0.18 to 1.5 M_sun (Eker et al. 2018); above 1.5 the radius continues as R(1.5) (M / 1.5)^0.555.
- **F3 Luminosity history.** L(t) = L_zams / (1 − 0.286 (t / t_ref)) with t_ref = 0.457 x main-sequence lifetime, which reproduces the Sun at 71% of present luminosity at zero age (Gough 1981 form, rescaled by lifetime).
- **F4 XUV history.** F_xuv ∝ t^−1.23 for t above 0.1 Gyr, saturated before (Ribas et al. 2005), normalised to the present Sun.
- **F5 Kepler.** P = 2π sqrt(a³ / (G (M_primary + m))).
- **F6 Gravity.** g = G m / R².
- **F7 Escape velocity.** v_esc = sqrt(2 G m / R).
- **F8 Bulk composition.** Given (m, R), find the (core, rock, ice) mass fractions whose three-layer model reproduces R, using mass–radius curves for iron, silicate and water-ice end members (Zeng et al. 2016 fits). Two unknowns from one constraint: assume a chondritic core-to-rock ratio of 0.45 unless density exceeds pure rock, in which case ice is zero and the core fraction is solved for. As built, the fractions mix end members by the uncompressed density, the chondritic ratio Earth's 0.48; since F122 a body formed too warm for water ice to condense carries none, its density's deficit below the chondritic mix being iron it lacks.
- **F9 Oblateness.** f = (5/4) Ω² R³ / (G m) x correction for the moment of inertia (Darwin–Radau).
- **F10 Locking timescale.** t_lock = ω a⁶ I Q / (3 G M_p² k₂ R⁵), I = α m R² (Gladman et al. 1996).
- **F11 Rotation regime.** L_Ro = sqrt(sqrt(g H) / (2 Ω / R))... computed as the equatorial Rossby deformation radius L = sqrt(c R / (2 Ω)) with c = sqrt(g H), H the scale height. rapid if L below R/3; slow if L above R; rhines between (Haqq-Misra et al. 2018, simplified).
- **F12 Radiogenic heating.** H(τ) = H_0 Σ_i f_i exp(λ_i τ) for τ = time before present, with H_0 = 5e−12 W per kg of silicate now and (f_i, half-life Gyr): U238 (0.37, 4.47), U235 (0.02, 0.70), Th232 (0.43, 14.0), K40 (0.18, 1.25) (Turcotte and Schubert).
- **F13 Tidal heating.** Ė = (21/2) (k₂/Q) G M_p² R⁵ n e² / a⁶ (Peale, Cassen and Reynolds 1979).
- **F14 Lithosphere thickness.** z = k (T_mantle − T_surface) / q, with k = 3 W/m/K for rock and k = 651 / T W/m/K for ice.
- **F15 Maximum relief.** h_max = c Δσ / (ρ_crust g), c calibrated so Earth gives 9 km, Mars 22 km and Venus 11 km with the strength law of interior.crust_strength. For ice, h_max is the smaller of the strength value and the relaxation-limited value for the body's age.
- **F16 Global contraction.** ΔR = (α_v / 3) ΔT R with α_v = 3e−5 per K and ΔT = 50 K x min(1, (R / R_Mars)²) of mantle cooling plus 250 K times the squared iron index from the uncompressed density (0 at 3.3 g/cm³, 1 at 5.15), accumulating as 1 − exp(−(t − 0.7 Gyr) / 2 Gyr) after a 0.7 Gyr onset during which early heating offsets the cooling. Mercury 5.7 km, Mars 1.7 km, the Moon 0.2 km (Byrne et al. 2014; Watters et al. 2010; Tosi et al. 2013).
- **F17 Magma production.** Rate = max(0, q − q_conductive) x 4π R² / (ρ L_fusion + ρ c_p ΔT) x efficiency, calibrated so Earth gives about 20 km³/yr and Io about 1 cm/yr of resurfacing.
- **F18 Ice shell thickness.** d = k_ice (T_melt − T_surface) / q with k_ice = 651 / T evaluated at the mean shell temperature; T_melt lowered by ammonia if present.
- **F19 Tectonic regime.** ice_shell_ocean if has_subsurface_ocean; ice_shell_rock if composition is ice_dominated or rock_ice without an ocean; mobile_lid if surface liquid water exists and surface_heat_flux is above 0.05 W/m² and radius above 4000 km; episodic_lid if rock with surface_heat_flux above 0.04 and no surface water and radius above 5000 km; stagnant_lid otherwise. Overridable.
- **F20 Scale height.** H = k_B T / (μ m_u g).
- **F21 Greenhouse optical depth.** τ = Σ_species a_s u_s^b_s with u_s the species column mass, a_s and b_s fit to Earth (+33 K), Mars (+5 K), Venus (+500 K) and Titan (+3 K net); water column derived from relative humidity and temperature by Clausius–Clapeyron; N2–N2 and CH4–N2 collision-induced terms scale as column squared. Surface temperature follows T_s⁴ = T_eq⁴ (1 + 3τ/4) in the zero-dimensional estimate.
- **F22 Equilibrium temperature.** T_eq = (L (1 − A_B) / (16 π σ d²))^(1/4); substellar temperature without redistribution is √2 T_eq.
- **F23 Jeans parameter.** λ = G m μ_s m_u / (k_B T R) per species, evaluated at the exobase temperature T_exo = T_s + 700 K x xuv^0.3 x (1 − 0.8 f_CO2) (provisional). A species is flagged below λ = 36, which is the rule of thumb that a gas survives when the escape speed exceeds six times its most probable thermal speed.
- **F24 Crater cut-off.** D_min = c (P/g)^0.6, c such that Earth gives 150 m; this yields about 2.4 km for Venus and 13 m for Mars, to be re-fit against Venus and Titan counts.
- **F25 Vapour pressure.** ln(p/p_t) = (L / R_s)(1/T_t − 1/T) per species about its triple point (T_t, p_t) with latent heat L of vaporisation above T_t and sublimation below.
- **F26 Sea level.** Find z such that ∫ max(0, z − h) dA over the surface equals the liquid inventory volume, by bisection on the hypsometric curve.
- **F27 Plate count.** N = round(7 (A / A_Earth)^0.5 (q / 0.087)^0.5), minimum 2.
- **F28 Impact flux.** Relative to the Moon: inner system by heliocentric distance and gravitational focusing per Ivanov 2001 (Mars about 2); outer system per Zahnle et al. 2003 by planet and satellite distance. Replaced by the bombardment of any star (F135): the rocky leftovers by the place against the snow line, the outer system's own comets beyond it.
- **F29 Impact velocity.** v² = v_∞² + v_esc², with v_∞ by location: 0.4 to 1.0 x the local orbital speed for heliocentric impactors, plus parent-planet focusing for moons. Since F135 v_∞'s share falls from 0.5 at the Earth's place against the snow line to 0.4, and a moon adds three times the square of its orbital speed about its planet (Zahnle et al. 2003).
- **F30 Simple-to-complex transition.** D_sc = 16.5 km x (1.62 / g) x f_material, f = 1 rock, 0.15 ice.
- **F31 Flux history.** The Neukum chronology N(≥1 km) = 5.44e−14 (e^(6.93 T) − 1) + 8.38e−4 T per km², T in Gyr before present, rewritten in time since formation τ with the present 4.5 Gyr after the Moon formed: rate = c [1 + ρ e^(−τ/τ_d)] with ρ = a b e^(4.5 b) / c ≈ 1.57e4 and τ_d = 1 / b = 0.144 Gyr, zero before the surface solidifies. A body of any age gets its heavy bombardment at formation and the steady flux ever after; the production function is the Neukum 2001 polynomial.
- **F32 Hadley edge.** φ_H = sqrt(5 g H Δ_h / (3 Ω² R²)) in radians, capped at π/2, with H the tropopause height (taken as 2 scale heights, which gives Earth 32°) and Δ_h the fractional equator-to-pole radiative-equilibrium temperature contrast (Held and Hou 1980).
- **F33 Heat transport coefficient.** D = 0.58 W/m²/K x (P / 1 bar)(c_p / c_p,air)(μ_air / μ)(Ω_Earth / Ω)², capped at 100 x the Earth value (Williams and Kasting 1997 scaling as used in exoplanet energy-balance models).
- **F35 Scarp population.** Lengths from N(>L) ∝ L^−1.5 between 10 km and L_max = min(0.4 R, 1,000 km x (ε / 0.005)²) for areal strain ε; height h = 0.03 L_km^0.667 km (Enterprise Rupes), shortening h / tan 25° per unit length, the population drawn until the shortening area ε x 4π R² is spent; a face of 12° central slope and a back slope 20 h long.
- **F36 Flood volcanism.** Eruptive fraction of F17 = 0.1 / (1 + exp((z_lith − 400 km) / 30 km)) x exp(−(ε / 0.0012)²); eruptions of 100 to 10,000 km³ (log-uniform) from vents weighted by exp(−(crust − min) / 10 km) exp(−(h − median) / 2 km) x (1 + 20 x rise height fraction) x the magmatic province weight (volcanism.province_count); each fills a lake from the nearest low, taking cells in height order where its lava stands at least 50 m deep, a full lake spilling the rest over its lowest pass into the next low (F107), its flows running no further than 1,500 km from the vent, the lava subsiding by 5% of its thickness. The finish's fine pass filled the flood's own footprint level wherever the base still said its lava and no finer pass had written there, reading what stood above the level in those cells as the resampling's ramp toward a higher neighbour, until F120 withdrew that levelling, which cut terraces into real slopes; what stands above the level, a rim, an older floor or a slope, the flood ends against. A high the flow drapes (less than 50 m above the level) carries a veneer of the sheet's thickness less the height it stands, so the ghost craters of the maria show in relief, not in albedo; under a younger crater the ground is the crater's but the surface is the lava's until the crater's own pass covers it. The fine outline is the coarse footprint's indicator, each cell half its own and a sixteenth each of its neighbours', sampled bilinearly and kept above 0.45, moved by three octaves of lobes two loop cells long by up to 0.3, so flow fronts round the loop's cells rather than step along them.
- **F41 Tidal stress of a thin shell.** The permanent bulge H = h₂ (M_p / M)(R / a)³ R towards the planet; membrane strains ε_θθ = (L P₂'' + H P₂) / R and ε_φφ = (L cot θ P₂' + H P₂) / R about the bulge axis with L = 0.25 H, plane stress with E = 9 GPa and ν = 0.33. The diurnal field at mean anomaly M is the field of a bulge (1 + 3e cos M) times the permanent one about an axis librated by 2e sin M, less the permanent field; non-synchronous rotation adds the field of the axis advanced by 1° less the permanent field. Cracks open perpendicular to the most tensile principal stress where it exceeds 40 kPa.
- **F42 Lineae.** The population carries π density / 2 of crack length per unit area, drawn per time bin within the retention age, lengths log-uniform 0.064 to 0.96 radii (Europa 100 to 1,500 km); a crack nucleates in proportion to the tension above the threshold, runs along the field of its time where the slow field dominates, and where the diurnal tension is more than 60% of the whole it propagates at 0.8 m/s only while the tension lasts and turns with the field through the orbit, arcing between cusps; 15% of the ridges pull apart into bands 0.0064 to 0.019 radii wide (Europa 10 to 30 km), each a lens whose width goes as (4 s (1 − s))^0.35 along its length, lineated inside at 1.5 km (or three cells) by 40 m. Under a plume the fissures of the thinned shell are vents: troughs 450 m deep and 6 km wide with flanks 40% of the depth. The ocean's salts in a crack fade under frost over 0.03 Gyr. Cracks answer one another (Pollard and Aydin 1988): 0.35 of new cracks start at the tip of an older one, where its stress concentrates, and run one way from it at the field's heading of their own time, so that sets branch and splay; a propagating crack within half a shell thickness of an open fissure (a vent) feels its stress shadow and turns toward meeting it at a right angle, stopping within half a width of it as at a free surface, while healed ridges and bands are crossed. Every crack's relief and width rise and fall along it by 0.35 rms over 15 km segments, so ridges and troughs pinch and swell rather than run uniform.
- **F43 Focused heating.** The shell under one pole is thinner than the mean by the polar thinning times a cosine taper that reaches the mean 70° from the pole, the edge wobbling by 15% with a two-cycle field, and the thinning structured across the zone by the shell's convection, up and down by 0.3 in cells two shell thicknesses wide, so that the zone's outline, its depression and its renewed terrain are lobate rather than circular; the replayed craters' survival and the crack density follow the local thickness (the density as 1 / d); the loop's relaxation keeps the mean shell's rates, a soft zone given its own band factors beside a stiff one sinking many times its offset. The renewed terrain, once the thinning passes 0.3, is the taper's core (above 0.75) and the ground round the fissures that opened where the taper stands above a half: within 30 km of them in the core, narrowing to nothing where the taper falls to a half, so that it follows the fissures out as tongues and its outline is theirs; a fissure leaving the core meets the thicker, stiffer shell and refracts along the contrast, turning between the 0.75 and 0.5 profiles to run round the margin, and stops in the thick shell where the taper falls below 0.3, so the fissures run on into the cratered terrain beyond the renewed one; it floats 0.6 of its isostatic depression low from the start of its episode (F53), which the relaxation treats as the shell's equilibrium surface rather than a load; at every step it keeps only the wavelengths longer than the renewal length (0.2 R, 50 km on Enceladus: in the thinned, hot zone every wavelength up to the zone's scale relaxes in a geological instant, so no ghost of an older crater survives there), so that it carries no craters (F60 has how the renewal is weighted and how the finish takes the older fine relief at the terrain's turn), no secondary lands whose parent is older than the renewed ice it would land on, and its cracks are drawn within the last 2 Myr; and the finish lays folds of 250 m at 2.5 km as a sum of oriented segments: one kernel per cell of a lattice 7 km across, each a wave windowed over 5 km along the ridges and 2.5 km across, laid along the fissures' direction averaged as doubled angles over 20 km about its centre (so that neighbouring kernels agree and no two cross in a moiré) and in phase with the nearest fissure's line within 40 km, so that the ridges parallel each fissure, run on past its ends and relay from one fissure's set to the next without a seam or a loop (a phase measured from a distance, however softened, closes in loops round short cracks), each kernel's spacing following the lid it buckles (the local shell thickness over the thickness at the edge of the core, a fold's wavelength going as the layer that folds: 0.6 of the nominal at the centre of Enceladus's terrain, 1.4 in the tongues and the halo, never below three cells), varying by 0.5 with the set field and by a factor of 0.76 to 1.32 at random, its strength from 0.5 to 1 with the set field and by a depth of its own (F63: a fold's heavy tail to the power 0.58, a kernel being a packet of about three folds, capped at 1.6 times the middling fold, the deep folds being F65's), a kernel in the patches an earlier pulse left standing (F63) keeping that pulse's strike at 0.7 of the relief, fading over the outer half of the fissure range so that each fissure carries its own belt and the plain beyond carries its roughness alone, a share of the kernels absent so that the segments die out, the kernels within two lattice cells summed and the sum normalized above a root weight of 1 so that the amplitude holds where kernels overlap and fades where they thin, in belts, over a tectonic roughness of 120 m rms, self-affine from 20 km down to the grid, so that the plains are rough at every scale as the cratered terrain is, all fading in over 10 km from the edge, a 400 m margin ridge 8 km wide along that edge and a belt of folds parallel to the edge decaying inward over 15 km, 300 m at 12 km, in segments 25 km long that shift against their neighbours by up to 0.4 of a wavelength, en echelon, where the spreading is taken up; ridge and belt stand in arcs, where a 25 km segment field is high, and are absent between them, where the fabric's fractures run out into the old ground instead, as the breaks in the belt about Enceladus's south pole do (Porco et al. 2006); the terrain meets the old surface by degrees (F60); a vent narrows to 0.35 of its width and 0.4 of its depth at its tips. A past episode's ridge, belt and folds are spread and lowered by the gardening since, each by (w² / (w² + 4 κ t))^1/2 for its width w, as the craters of its time are. A shell thinner than 0.025 R renews itself everywhere within the retention age instead; a thicker one keeps its old craters.
- **F44 Grooved terrain.** Lanes of new ice are swaths about centre lines traced along the tidal field at their time where the shell cracks and the tension at their start opens it, so that they curve with the field as Enceladus's sulci do, and along great circles where nothing steers them, as Ganymede's; widths log-uniform 4 to 24 thicknesses of the elastic lid where and when the lane opens (F61; a lane is a swath, a rift zone a few to a few tens of times the layer that breaks, which does not scale with the body: 44 to 266 km on Ganymede's 11 km lithosphere of the episode), lengths 6 to 40 widths, log-uniform, at most 1.5 rad (Ganymede's lanes hundreds to a couple of thousand kilometres) their edges and ends as F61 has them, their midpoints gathered by rejection into belts of extension drawn by a 1.2-cycle field squared; 0.85 of them are laid beside one of the 24 lanes laid lately, in its generation (its time within 5% of the episode), turned by up to 3°, their centre lines 0.4 to 0.85 of the sum of their half widths apart so that the two always overlap and a set is one swath and between half and all of its length, so that they come in parallel sets; the rest run along the strike of a smooth field of extension (1.2 cells per radian about the body, read as an angle from east) that drifts by a cell through the episode, so that generations cross-cut instead of every lane taking a heading of its own; drawn until they cover the resurfaced fraction, at most 12,000; a fifth of them are smooth bright terrain. The amount comes from the peak tidal flux of the body's history (the pulse of a resonance episode, F50, or the steady flux), and each lane forms at its own time, uniform over tectonics.ice_resurfacing_duration about tectonics.ice_resurfacing_age, the spread and the mean of the time the flux spent above the floor; at that time the relief gives way, by the lane's cover of each cell (F60), to the relief smoothed over 100 km (or R / 20), and drops 200 m, the drop spread over the lithosphere's flexural parameter at the time ((4 D / rho g)^1/4, about 90 km on Ganymede today and 55 km during its episode, 130 km on Enceladus) so that a lane narrower than it sinks little and no lane ends on a step, the surface age resets and the unit becomes grooved terrain, a younger lane overprinting an older, clean ice whatever the regolith around it; the finish redraws the lanes at its own resolution, completes the renewal out to their resolved edges (F60) and lays their fabric (F61). Craters formed since cut the grooves; craters formed before are gone.
- **F49 Thermal segregation on slopes.** A slope rising towards the pole faces the equator and a slope rising towards the equator faces the pole, so each cell of an icy surface sits at the mean temperature of the latitude its slope behaves as, |lat| − atan(poleward slope), for its frost and its lag; frost lies on pole-facing walls and leaves equator-facing ones, and the cap's edge follows the ground.
- **F45 Chaos** (its patches replaced by F80's generations). Patches of diameter log-uniform 5 to 300 km, their number the chaos fraction of the surface over the mean patch area, drawn per time bin within the retention age at places accepted by a two-cycle heat field whose troughs take half as many as its crests; inside an outline wobbling by 35% in about six waves round the patch the matrix drops 150 m and breaks into hummocks of 60 m at 1.5 km around rafts of the old surface (45% of the patch at a 15 km scale) that keep what was on them; the matrix takes the surface age, the chaos unit and the ocean's salts, which fade like a crack's.
- **F46 Cryovolcanism.** On a shell over rock 0.7 of the ice magma budget extrudes as domes, per time bin while the budget allows, of volumes log-uniform 1e-6 to 3e-5 R³, each a bell of height 0.24 of its diameter (V = (2π/3) x 0.24 r³) placed at random, relaxing afterwards like any load of its wavelength by F39; its substrate is half clean ice and carries salts that darken over 0.15 Gyr. A crater deeper than 3 km brings brines up: salts in patches at 4 km over the inner 35% of its floor, darkening over 0.15 Gyr.
- **F47 Plume fallout.** A polar plume lays fresh ice down at the rate volcanism.plume_fallout_rate at its pole, falling off as exp(−angle / 25°) to 1% of it far away; it buries a millimetre of lag or salt in that depth over the rate, which caps the exposure the lag maturity sees and the time the salts show, and keeps a full frost cover wherever it exceeds 1e3 m/Gyr.
- **F48 Hemispheric asymmetry.** The sub-parent point lies on +x and the orbit runs prograde about +z, so a locked moon's trailing apex faces +y; the parent's plasma darkens and reddens the ice there by the tint (0.76, 0.69, 0.62) at the apex, falling off as the squared cosine of the angle from it, scaled by surface.trailing_irradiation.
- **F50 Resonance episode.** An orbital resonance holds the eccentricity up for a while: e(t) is the present value with a pulse to orbit.resonance_eccentricity centred orbit.resonance_age before the present and orbit.resonance_duration wide at its base, rising over the first fifth of the episode after the capture, holding at the forced value, and falling over the last fifth after the escape (Showman and Malhotra 1997: the Galilean moons' passage into the Laplace resonance pumped Ganymede's eccentricity to 0.01 to 0.07 for 10 Myr to 1 Gyr; Bland, Showman and Tobie 2009 on the heating pulse that followed). The tidal flux follows e² (F13), so the pulse is a pulse of heating in every history downstream: the shell thins with it and the relaxation of the craters of the time follows (F39), and the lanes of grooved terrain take their amount from its peak and their times from its spread (F44; since F140 their amount from the heat carried over the episode). Ganymede's preset carries the episode, 0.008 for a gigayear two gigayears ago, which thins its shell to about 40 km, enough to relax the craters over 150 km to palimpsests and not the 70 km ones (a stronger pulse flattens those too, which the dark terrain does not show), and pins nothing.
- **F51 Ejecta cover and mixed exposure.** A blanket's deposit thins as r⁻³ (McGetchin et al. 1973) and covers the ground it falls on by 1 − 2^−(edge / r)³: complete out to 1.3 radii (a raw cover above 0.92 counts as complete, the cover being scaled by it), half at the continuous edge (two radii, wandering by 15% in eleven to sixty-one lobes round the crater, the lobes developing outward, a lobe narrower than four cells along the edge left undrawn since at the grid's scale it would be a tooth from a continuous rim zone), a fifth at three radii, tapering to nothing over the outer quarter of the footprint, where the deposit is too thin to leave a record. The covered cell keeps one age, the one whose exposure gives the mixture's maturity under a law that saturates on the surface's maturity timescale τ: a = t_event + τ ln(f + (1 − f) e^−(t_event − a_old)/τ), exact for an exponential law at every later time; τ is surface.space_weathering_timescale on rock and, on ice, the exposure at which the lag at the body's mean temperature reaches 1 − 1/e. Where the cover is under a half the ground keeps its unit, its melt and its salts or lava. The ray field brightens the blanket by the same cover and runs its rays out from the rim through it, so a fresh crater's disc grades into its rays and an old crater's into the ground, with no circle at either.
- **F52 The surface's own albedo.** An airless body (under 10 Pa) has the Bond albedo its surface gives it: the luminance of the regolith mixture at maturity, raised to the fresh luminance over the share of the body a plume's fallout keeps under frost (the frost cover of F47 integrated over the sphere). The surface depends on the temperature the albedo sets, through the shell, the regime and the materials, so the albedo reads the regolith mixture and the fallout rate as lagged dependencies and the graph settles in a pass or two. Against the measured Bond albedos: Moon 0.14 (0.11), Mercury 0.085 (0.088), Ceres 0.086 (0.09), Europa 0.66 (0.67), Enceladus 0.83 (0.81), Triton 0.66 (0.76), Ganymede 0.37 (0.43, the bright lanes not counted), Io 0.37 (0.52), Callisto 0.13 (0.22, its bright craters not counted); Pluto 0.25 (0.5) until its nitrogen and methane ices are among the materials. Under an atmosphere clouds, haze and dust set the planetary albedo, which the climate stage will compute; until then the pressure class stands in. No preset pins the albedo.
- **F53 Episodes of focusing.** The focused heating comes and goes (O'Neill and Nimmo 2010: the shell overturns every 0.1 to 1 Gyr, each overturn active about 10 Myr): episodes recur every tectonics.focusing_period, forty conductive times of the shell (a fit in their range, so that Enceladus's come 0.52 Gyr apart; ten while its shell stood at 44 km, refitted when the porous core of F68 brought it to its measured 22 km), each 10 Myr long. Each starts where the overturn happens to (a direction from the tectonic seed), and the body reorients to put it at the pole (Nimmo and Pappalardo 2006), so in the present frame the terrain it renewed lies about that direction with its fissures turned by the same rotation. Through an episode the zone floats at its isostatic depression and renews itself every step as the present one does, its fissures opening in the polar field of its time, a share of all the thinned zone's cracks falling to each episode alike; when it ends the shell floats back up and the terrain takes craters from then on. An episode renews its terrain once in the loop, at the step its end falls in, whatever the step's length (F60). The finish redraws each fossil terrain's outline from its carried fissures and lays its folds and margin at its time, in sequence with everything else; its fissures vented until it ended, and stand on the last surface it renewed. Enceladus carries eight of them, which with the present terrain and the lanes renew 0.40 of the surface (the sidecar's renewed_fraction); the rest stays cratered plains, 60% against the 43% Kirchoff and Schenk 2009 mapped, each past terrain being the size of the present one.
- **F54 Sub-grid crater roughness.** The craters smaller than the finish stamps (below cratering.min_diameter or two cells) are a population too, and each cell's surface age says how many it has taken: at the smallest resolved diameter D the production function and the flux history give the density since the surface formed, against the equilibrium 0.05 of geometric saturation, and the share reached, s = 1 − e^−(N / N_eq). Their relief goes under everything as a self-affine field with octaves from D down to two cells, each 2^−H as strong (H 0.8, the Hurst exponent of cratered terrain, Rosenburg et al. 2011), scaled so that a saturated surface's rms is 0.35 of the depth of a crater of diameter D, times √s. An old surface is rough at every scale, a renewed one smooth, and their contact is a gradient of roughness; the strength follows the surface age smoothed over two cells (twelve passes of the one-cell kernel), so the edge of a young deposit on old ground is a short ramp and never a staircase of cells. The renewed terrains' floors come from a Gaussian pyramid (blur, halve, expand level by level, blur) that leaves no block of the loop's cells, and a renewed cell always drops the finest band.
- **F55 Variation within units.** A body's lavas drift in composition over its eruptive history: a flow unit's reflectance departs from its material's by up to 18%, by a smooth function of the eruption's time with a period of 1 Gyr, which the eruption writes into the surface's composition layer (the loop's and the finish's alike), so units close in time are alike, units an age apart differ, and a crater's ejecta of the same basalt keeps its unit's tone; no mixed age is ever read as a tone (the maria's titanium, albedos 0.06 to 0.11). A surface age itself resamples by its nearest coarse cell, never a blend of two events' times. Steep ground sheds its regolith: from 20°, the regolith's stable slope, the maturity of every airless surface falls, by 0.3 of the way at the angle of repose, 30°, from the ground's own slope (optical maturity maps: the walls of young craters bright, those of old craters near the stable slope only a little less mature than their surroundings), on rock and ice alike. A lane of renewed ice is clean by a share drawn uniformly in 0.55 to 1, so the lanes differ in brightness as Ganymede's do.
- **F56 Io's flows accumulate.** Every patera carries three generations of flow fields, the older with lobes from 150 years to 300,000 years old, six to fourteen lobes each traced over the ground (F59), so the plains are a patchwork of half-buried flows. A flow's coverage varies by 0.4 over 15 km, the overlapping flow units within a lobe. Until F84 a flow faded on a fixed 20,000 years and each patera carried three generations of plume rings faded by their ages; since F84 a flow is coated by what falls on it and the plumes are an eruption history.
- **F59 Flows follow the ground.** A lobe of a flow field is a path traced from the patera's rim at a random azimuth, 0.3 to 1 of the field's reach long: at each step of 2 km or a 64th of its length the heading turns toward the fall line of the regional ground (the base smoothed over 40 km, since a front rides over bumps shorter than its width on its own pile) by 2 tanh(slope / 0.005), sideways along a contour and onward down a slope but never back on itself, and wanders by up to 1 rad over 40 km of path, so a flow runs downhill where the ground slopes and meanders where it is flat; it stops where a mountain stands or where going on would climb more than 150 m above the lowest ground it has reached, as a flow ponds in a basin and crosses a bump no higher than its own pile. The lobe is a tube about that line, its half width 0.05 to 0.15 of the reach (at least 3 km), 0.4 of that at the vent widening to the full at the toe and wandering by 0.25, its margin lobate by 0.3 at the width's scale and soft over 0.15 of the width or a cell and a half; the strongest lobe's coverage and the freshest lava win where lobes cross. The finish lays the regime's surface afresh before it stamps anything, as every loop step does, so the loop's coarse stamps leave no halo of unit or coverage round a feature. A mountain block ends in a fault scarp on its upthrown side (its rim falling as 1 − x¹²) and slopes under the plains on the downthrown (1 − x²), so it is no longer an oval with a steep edge all round (a thrust block since F87). Since F85 a path starts at any point along a heading and turns toward the fall line at a rate per distance, 2 tanh(slope / 0.005) over 20 km of path at full slope (per step before, so that short lobes swung onto the regional slope within a few steps).
- **F57 The tiger-stripe cascade.** Under a plume the fissures of the thinned zone form in a cascade (Hemingway, Rudolph and Manga 2020): the first opens through the thinnest shell, within half a spacing of the pole, and runs along the tidal field while the thin shell lets it, refracting round the margin as a polar crack does; the ice its eruptions pile along its flanks (two Gaussian ridges, each 0.4 of the trough's depth over an eighth of the vent's width) loads a plate broken at the fissure, whose surface tension peaks a quarter flexural parameter away, x = α π / 4 with α⁴ = 4 D / (ρ g) and D = E te³ / (12 (1 − ν²)), the moment there 0.3225 V α and the stress 6 M / te²; the next fissure opens there on either side, and so on outward while the site is still in the thinned core (profile above 0.75) and the bending and the tidal tension together exceed the strength, up to eight a side. The elastic thickness at a site is the body's (interior.elastic_thickness) times the shell's local thinning factor, so the spacing follows from the parameters rather than from the 35 km of the real set (which the paper reproduces with 5.2 km of elastic ice). A fissure wanders by 0.3 rad over 50 km, more than a lesser crack, and ends in a fork: from each tip a branch leaves at 60° and bends back to the field over 15 km, running a quarter of the fissure's length at 0.6 of its width, the hooked, bifurcating terminations of the real stripes. The zone's other cracks are its lesser fractures, drawn as before but no longer vents. Each past episode's cascade is drawn in the pole's frame and carried to its fossil terrain.
- **F58 Plasma under the fallout.** The trailing hemisphere's plasma darkening (F48) processes the optical surface, the top millimetre, in 10⁴ years in a Jupiter-class magnetosphere and proportionally longer in a weaker one (Cooper et al. 2001: micrometres on Europa in about ten years, centimetres in under a million); where a plume's fallout buries that millimetre first, the darkening shows by t_bury / (t_bury + t_dark), so Enceladus's trailing side stays as bright as the rest under its frost while Europa's bullseye keeps its full tint.
- **F60 Renewed terrains keep their history.** A lane, a polar terrain or a past episode's terrain renews the ground once, at its time, and everything since stands on what it left. In the loop a terrain's renewal is a weight on each cell. For a polar terrain: 1 inside and a 15 km ramp across its edge (F43); the ground beyond relaxes under the episode's thinned shell instead (F66). For a lane: its cover, a ramp across its border zone (F61). A cell gives up its relief shorter than the renewal length (a polar terrain's is the larger of the lanes' smoothing length and 0.2 R; a lane's its smoothing length; since F141 a polar terrain's is its zone's radius and a lane's its own width, and the cell keeps the base relief below that length, toned down below the terrain's fabric) by its weight and keeps the rest with its neighbours, so a renewed cell never stands apart from them. A past episode fires once, at the step its end falls in (a 10 Myr episode is far shorter than a step, and a rule that waits for a step to end inside it fires one time in five); the present terrain renews at every step while its episode lasts and alone floats at its depression. The finish lays no floor of its own on a polar terrain: the loop's relief stands, craters since included. It draws each outline at its resolution (a polar terrain's as the signed great-circle distance to its edge), composes the terrains in the order they formed (each one's clean ice laid over what was there by its cover; the latest to take more than half a cell holds its age and unit), and at each terrain's turn in the sequence takes the fine relief stamped before it by the same weight, back to the relief the loop left: a small crater inside is gone, one across the edge is softened by degrees and not cut, and the unresolved craters' roughness (F54) is taken in the same measure. A crater older than a terrain is therefore stamped whole in the finish and taken by the terrain after; the loop, which lays a step's lanes before the step's craters, still keeps such a crater off them. The lanes' renewal is completed on the resolved grid before anything is stamped: where the lane's resolved cover c exceeds what the loop renewed of the cell q (the loop's weight, resampled and smoothed over its cell), the relief shorter than the smoothing length gives way by (c - q) / max(1 - q, 0.25) to the mean of the ground about it (in full where the loop left more than a quarter of the relief; where it left less, the little that remains is not worth a seam along the edge of a younger crater's ground), the craters younger than the lane left out of the mean and left standing. No floor is carried from a lane's time to the finish: a floor recorded at one time and laid at another put a notch hundreds of metres deep along every lane margin whose ground had moved between.
- **F61 A lane is a rift.** One mechanism on every body: a lane is the rift a lid of thickness H makes under extension, and its width, its grooves, its border and its damage zone all scale with H, the elastic lid where and when the lane opens: the lid of the time, thinner by the shell's own thinning where a focusing episode, a past one or the present, has the shell thinned (11 km on Ganymede; 10 to 13 km where Enceladus's lanes open in the thinned zones of its episodes, 23 km outside them). Width: 4 to 24 H (F44). A rift is long: a lane that could not be six times as long as it is wide within the longest a lane may be, 1.5 rad, is not a lane and is not formed, so that no lane is wider than a quarter of the radius, and a draw on a lid too thick for that is a rift that never opened. A lane's time, the place it opens and its width in lid thicknesses are drawn together, and drawn again, up to 4,000 times, until the lid there and then lets a lane that wide be one; the first draw uses the streams it always did, so a body whose lid lets every lane form is not changed by the rule (Ganymede's maps were identical under it). A lane does not open in ground a focusing episode is renewing about it, which keeps no trace. On Enceladus the lid of the time lets no lane form, 4 H being 91 km against the 63 km a lane may be: its lanes open only in the zones its episodes thinned, during those episodes, as sets of belts 44 to 61 km wide (4.3 to 5.9 lid thicknesses) along the margins of its old terrains, and the later terrains overprint the older sets. A body between gets both kinds (principle 15): an Enceladus heated less (0.8 of its eccentricity) makes lanes only in its episodes, and made a fifth larger makes its first few on the lid of the time, half again as large some two dozen there; a fully heated one half again as large makes 86 on the lid of the time and 5 in its episodes (with F75's tides; the counts before were a fifth larger, a few, and half again as large, four fifths and the rest in its episodes). Grooves: the necking of the lid, 3.5 thicknesses of the layer that necks (Fletcher and Hallet 1983 find 2.5 to 6 for the Basin and Range; Dombard and McKinnon 2001 apply the instability to Ganymede's ice), that layer in a rift, hot as a rift is, being 0.18 H: the product, 0.63 H, is one calibration, on Ganymede's grooves at 7 km, which puts Enceladus's at 6.4 to 8.2 km on the thinned lid its lanes break. Their relief is 0.036 of their spacing (250 m at 7 km, Squyres 1981; Enceladus's ridged plains, 100 to 400 m at 3 to 4 km, fall in the same ratio, Bland et al. 2007), whatever spacing a grid draws them at, and no grid draws them closer than three cells. On the necks rides the fine set, the tilted fault blocks (Pappalardo et al. 1998 and Patel et al. 1999: necks about 8 km apart and fault blocks 1 to 2 km apart on Ganymede): six to a groove spacing, 0.4 of the relief, each a long back slope and a short scarp facing the lane's axis, with a wander and segments of their own, drawn where they span three cells (1.1 to 1.4 km on Enceladus, at the edge of what its 387 m cells draw; Ganymede's 1.2 km only on a grid finer than 400 m). Every length of the fabric is in groove spacings: sets change over 40, with the spacing varying by 0.3 and the strength from 0.5 to 1; the grooves are a wave across the lane that sways by 6 rad of phase over 15 spacings and wanders by 2 rad over 3.5, so that they fan and bend; each groove lives in segments about 9 spacings long, with its own run of presence along itself, so that a groove dies out while its neighbours carry on; under them a tectonic roughness of 0.3 of the relief, self-affine from 3 spacings down to the grid. A smooth lane (a fifth of them) carries the same fabric at 0.3 of the relief (Ganymede's smooth bright terrain is finely grooved at Galileo's resolution; Pappalardo et al. 2004). The border: a lane meets the old ground across a zone one H wide, its bounding faults cutting the whole lid, a physical width, the same fault on any grid; a grid's own ramp of 2.5 cells is only the floor where it cannot draw the zone narrower. The edges are the bounding faults' traces: each wanders by 0.08 of the half width rms, self-affine with a Hurst exponent of 0.8 below 6 widths, and is made of segments 12 spacings long; where one relays to the next the trace steps by 0.5 to 1.5 spacings across a relay ramp 3 spacings long, so that it runs straight and bends, the offsets reverting to the mean trace. The fault throws 1.2 times the grooves' relief where its segments stand (0.6 of the trace on average): the lane's side down, decaying over a spacing, and the flank up, decaying over two. Beyond it the damage zone reaches 3 spacings: the grooves go on as open fractures 0.6 of the relief deep, each in fewer and shorter segments the farther out, so that the lane's strain thins out through discrete breaks instead of fading. The ends: every end stops at a cut (since F142 only an end inside an older lane; the rest taper). Where a lane enters an older lane that crosses it at more than 30°, the older lane takes up its extension with probability 0.7, once the lane has run 1.5 widths: the cut runs along the older lane's strike, a cell and a quarter of that lane's half width inside its edge. A lane of a set ends on its parent's cut, so a set ends on one front. An end that has run its length looks on to 3 times it (no farther than the longest a lane may be) for either; finding none, it dies out in the old ground at a fault of its own across the lane, 55° to 90° to it as transfer faults are, whose trace wanders as the edges do and carries the same throw and damage zone.
