On Ganymede two fifths of the ends stop against an older lane and the rest share some 250 fault fronts, a set to a front; running on to a junction makes its lanes 1,800 km long at the median and 4,500 km at the longest, where its named sulci run from Tiamat's 1,330 km to Mysia's 4,221 km. The tone follows the structure: a lane's ice is clean by 0.8 to 1 (its age does the rest through the lag, F40), sets differ by up to 0.15, troughs gather the dark lag and are darker by up to 0.3 (Prockter et al. 1998), and a fracture's walls show ice 0.6 as clean as the lane's in the old ground. At a lane's turn in the finish the fine relief inside its border gives way by its cover to its floor with its own grooves, so a younger lane cuts an older one's grooves and never lies under them. What only one body reaches: no number in this entry is set for one body. The bound of a quarter of the radius turns lanes away on Enceladus alone among the reference bodies, which is the statement that its lid is thick against its size. A switch does remain upstream, F44's rule that a shell thinner than 0.025 R (0.02 until F75) makes no lanes at all: an Enceladus twice as large loses its lanes in one step there, and that rule is on the list for the audit against principle 15.
- **F62 A crack is a chain of segments.** A ridge or a cycloid is drawn as segments about 12 widths long, their ends jittered by 0.3 of a segment. Each opens as an ellipse along its length, as a crack in an elastic plate does (Pollard and Segall 1987), so that its relief falls to 0.3 where one segment hands over to the next; each stands aside from the last by 0.25 to 0.75 of the width, to the one side all along a crack, en echelon; and the whole crack closes toward its own tips as the fourth root of the same ellipse. A band and a vent are single openings and keep their own profiles. A crack that opens under loose cover shows as pits instead (F64).
- **F63 A rift works in pulses, and its grooves differ in depth.** The first pulse lays a set of grooves along the whole lane (F61). Each of two later pulses reworks part of it with a set of its own: struck along the field the lane follows as that field stands later in the lane's working life (the strike field of extension as it drifts, or the tidal field as it turns; the life is a set's spread of the episode, 5% of it, or what is left of the focusing episode that thinned the lid, if less), which turns Ganymede's later sets by 8 degrees at the median and more than 21 for one in ten, and leaves Enceladus's within half a degree of the first; spaced, swayed and segmented on its own; and standing in patches about 20 groove spacings across, where a field of its own is high, two fifths of the lane, the rest keeping what it had. Where a later groove stands, across its own width and along its own segment, it cuts what stood there before, so the sets overlap groove by groove and never add as two waves would. The fine set follows whichever set stands at a cell. A groove's depth has a heavy tail: fault throws along a line across an array follow a power law with a cumulative exponent of 0.5 to 1 (Marrett and Allmendinger 1992), a necked lid spaces and sizes its grooves more evenly than faults at random, and the share of grooves more than x times as deep as the middling one is taken to fall as x to the power -2, a choice between the two and not a measurement, bounded softly at 2.5 times the middling depth, about the deepest measured (700 m crest to trough against a middling 350 m on Ganymede; Squyres 1981). Half the grooves are shallower than the middling one by up to three tenths and one in eight is more than 1.7 times as deep; what a groove has beyond the middling depth goes into its trough, a master trough, and not onto its crest. The folds of a polar terrain take the same tail to the power 0.58, a kernel being a packet of about three folds whose mean varies less than one fold does; and where a field three kernel cells across stands high a kernel may belong to an earlier pulse, and then keeps the strike of the stress field as it stood then, turned by up to 25 degrees by a field that stands for the unresolved turn of the stress through the episode as the body reorients, at 0.7 of the relief and in phase with its own neighbours.
- **F64 Loose cover and pit chains.** The plumes' fallout (F47) is a loose cover: each episode of focusing (F53) had a plume at its centre while it lasted, venting as the present one does, and the cover on a surface is the sum of every plume's fallout there since the surface formed. On Enceladus that is 400 to 1,900 m on ground as old as the body, the same order as the 250 to 700 m its pits imply and two to three times over (Martin et al. 2017), nine plumes having run where the real body's history is not known. An open fracture under loose cover drains the cover into itself and shows at the surface as a row of pits (Wyrick et al. 2004 for Mars). A fracture drains no more than it is deep: a fracture of a lane's damage zone its own depth (F61), a crack the depth to which its tension holds it open against the weight of the ice, the stress that cracks the shell over the ice's density and the gravity, 380 m on Enceladus. A pit is a cone at the cover's angle of repose, 2.9 drained thicknesses across (2 / tan 35 degrees) and as deep as what drained, so 1.1 km across and 380 m deep on Enceladus's old plains (the real plains' 250 to 700 m of cover would make pits 0.7 to 2 km across by the same rule); pits stand one every pit and a half along the fracture, each up to 0.3 of a step off its place and 0.7 to 1.3 of the size, and only where the fracture's own segment stands, so the chain breaks as the fracture does; a grid draws them where they span a cell and a half. In a lane's damage zone the cover fills a fracture by its depth over the fracture's, the open trough showing by what is left and the pits standing for what is filled. A ridge or a cycloid that opened under cover deeper than its own relief built no ridges at the surface and shows as pits alone, with no salts and no unit of its own; one that opened on bare ground keeps its ridges under whatever fell since. So the same crack runs as a ridge across a young terrain and breaks into pits where it enters the old snow-covered plains. A body with no plume has no loose cover any grid could draw pits in.
- **F65 Deep folds grow and link.** A renewed plain's fold fabric (F43) rides on a first-order train of deep folds three times its wavelength (7.5 km at the edge of Enceladus's terrain's core, going with the lid as the fabric's spacing does; the highest known ridge on Enceladus, at Samarkand Sulcus, is 10 km wide, Giese et al. 2017, its ridge-and-trough terrain is spaced 3 to 4 km, Bland et al. 2007, and its funiscular folds 1.1 km, Barr and Preuss 2010: the order is a choice in that range). Its hinges nucleate one to a wavelength, on a jittered lattice wherever the fabric stands, each with a depth of its own from the heavy tail of F63; the fabric's packets keep the body of their tail. A hinge comes up when its fold has amplified enough, growing exponentially from its initial depth, so the deeper come up sooner: 1.2 units of the growth's time for each factor e by which its depth falls short of the deepest a lid holds (2.5 times the middling fold). One that comes up within 0.8 of a wavelength of a fold already grown is part of it. It grows from its nucleus both ways along the folds' direction (the fissures' direction as the fabric reads it), a quarter wavelength at a step, at a rate that goes with its depth, every hinge at once in the order each step is reached, turning no more than 15 degrees in a step and deflected by up to 12 degrees, in patches four wavelengths across, by the lid's unresolved heterogeneity. A tip that comes within 0.55 of a wavelength of another fold's hinge links with it unless it is running away from it (Grasemann and Schmalholz 2012: fold segments link when their hinges lie slightly more than half a wavelength apart or less, and overlap en echelon when farther apart). Where the two run within 50 degrees of each other it bends into the other where its own heading meets it, or, running alongside, no more steeply than 10 degrees, along a cubic that leaves the tip along its heading and arrives along the other, and beyond the junction the two are one fold: a Y where the other runs on; where the other ends there, into its end, which then neither plunges nor, if it was coming the other way, grows on. A tip of the other coming the other way past the junction stops too. Across a wider angle the tip runs straight on into the fold ahead. A tip passes a neighbour it is running away from, one that ends short of where it would join it or lies behind it, and one it could join only across a third fold. No hinge crosses another: a growth step that meets another fold ends there. A tip stops free where the fabric's strength (the terrain's, times the fade away from the fissures) falls under a quarter, half a wavelength short of one of its terrain's own vents, or when the shortening ends: a fold of twice the middling depth, unhindered, reaches about four and a half wavelengths each way, nine in all, as Samarkand's ridge is ten times as long as it is wide (100 km by 10 km and 1.75 km high, Giese et al. 2017). A hinge nucleates no nearer its terrain's vents than a wavelength; a younger crack across the terrain cuts its folds and does not bound them. So the deep hinges grow long first and the shallow ones fill the gaps and link into them: where the train converges its hinges merge, and where they run apart they end en echelon. A fold stands highest where it nucleated and lower, by up to 0.3, where it reached last, so that a linkage leaves a saddle as linked folds do; a free end plunges over a wavelength. Its profile is a narrow crest, a Ricker profile an eighth of the wavelength wide with a trough each side that takes up what the crest stands above, between the train's broad troughs, as the folds of a thin-skinned belt are (ejective folds). One of the middling depth is as steep as the fabric's folds, 430 m high on Enceladus, a deeper one taller in proportion, the deepest about a kilometre. The folds are one surface: the profile is taken about the distance to the nearest hinge, softened over a quarter of a crest's width where two are nearly as near, with the hinges' heights and wavelengths in the same measure, so that two hinges merge where they meet, the one that runs on unbroken, and a trough between two folds is shared. The fabric's folds give way to a deep fold by a half where it stands, the rest riding on its limbs. A past episode's deep folds are spread and lowered by the gardening since over their crests' width.
- **F66 The ground beside a renewed terrain relaxes under the thinned shell.** A renewed terrain takes the old relief inside its outline and across the ramp at its edge (F60), and no farther: no halo is drawn from the outline. Beside it the old ground relaxes by the law of F39 under the thinner shell of the episode that renewed the terrain, for as long as the episode lasted, the thinning read from the episode's own map: in the loop each wavelength band shorter than the renewal length keeps its survival under the episode's shell over its survival under the shell as it was; in the finish each replayed crater relaxes likewise under every past episode it lived through. On Enceladus, whose 44 km shell thinned to 0.45 of itself still keeps a 25 km crater whole through a 10 Myr episode and loses a 100 km load, the cratered plains run on crisp to the margin's ramp; under a thinner shell, as on an Enceladus half again as large, the craters beside a terrain relax by size, the largest first, as Enceladus's relaxed craters follow its heat flow (Bland et al. 2012). The halo it replaces took up to half of every crater's relief out to one mean shell thickness from the outline, whatever its size, which the relaxation law does not support on Enceladus, and copied the outline, tongues and all, into the old ground as a smooth ramp.
- **F67 A complex crater's wall is made by its collapse.** The transient cavity's rim slumps inward on inward-facing normal faults in rings of blocks (Settle and Head 1979): terrace after terrace from the rim inward, the outermost 0.09 D^0.87 km wide on the Moon (Pearce and Melosh 1986, a fit to lunar craters that a slump model with a constant yield strength reproduces), each inward three quarters of the one outside it, the widths growing outward toward the rim as theirs do, until one would be narrower than 0.35 of the outermost or the terraces would fill more than 0.85 of the wall; below them the wall is a plain slope of debris to the floor. A terrace's width depends on the crater's size and on the strength length Y / (rho g) of the collapsing target, so it goes as that length to the power the fit leaves, 0.13; the simple-to-complex transition diameter measures the same length, so a body's terraces scale with its transition against the Moon's, with no constant of their own: Mercury's come out a tenth narrower than the Moon's at a given size, as Leith and McKinnon (1991) measured them narrower, Ganymede's a fifth narrower and Enceladus's a tenth wider. A terrace is a ring of slump blocks, each about five of its widths long and longer or shorter by up to a factor of two, its scarp standing off its neighbours' by up to 0.35 of the width as the throw of the faults varies around a crater, and bowed out toward the rim by a quarter of the width in its middle as a slump's headscarp is; a fifth of the blocks slumped no terrace. A bench keeps 0.35 of the wall's slope; its scarp is a fifth of its width wide, half as wide again or half as narrow from block to block, wanders along its trace by 0.15 of the width at the scale of a few widths, and widens with the crater's degradation; a slumped block is broken, its bench hummocky by a quarter of a terrace's drop at half the outermost terrace's width. The outermost blocks scallop the rim crest, out by 0.4 of the outermost terrace's width in each one's middle and in at the cusps between, in place of the crenulation a simple crater keeps; the innermost blocks' toes bulge the floor's edge in by half of the innermost terrace's width. So the wall's width, its terraces and the floor's edge change from block to block around a crater, and none of them is a copy of the outline. The loop draws the plain wall and the scalloped rim; the finish draws the terraces, for the craters it stamps and in a loop crater's restored detail.
- **F68 A porous core dissipates tides.** The rocky interior under an H2O layer is a pile of grains whose porosity falls with the pressure in it as sediments compact: 0.4 at no pressure, falling by a factor e for each 60 MPa of pressure (Athy's law for sandstone, porosity 0.49 falling by e over 3.7 km of burial, Sclater and Christie 1980), each depth's pressure the H2O layer's weight and the interior's own above it; interior.core_porosity is its mean by volume. Above 0.05, where a rock is consolidated, the pores dissipate tides by the friction of grains and the flow of water through them: the eccentricity tides of F13 on the interior's radius with an imaginary Love number 0.84 times the porosity above 0.05, set so that Enceladus's core of 0.23 dissipates 15 GW, within the 10 to 30 GW Choblet et al. (2017) model for a core of 20 to 30 percent porosity. interior.tidal_power adds it to the body's own. So Enceladus's tides come to 30 GW, its heat flux to 38 mW/m², and its shell to 22 km and its elastic lid to 12 km, where they had been 15 GW, 44 km and 23 km: the shell gravity, shape and libration measure is 18 to 25 km (Čadek et al. 2016; Beuthe et al. 2016), and validate now checks it. The large moons' interiors, at gigapascals, are consolidated and dissipate nothing more; a small moon with a porous core and an eccentric orbit is heated from within, as Enceladus is. With the thinner shell, the craters relax more beside its terrains and across its plains, its lid lets lanes open on the lid of the time as well as in its episodes (F61), and its episodes, forty conductive times of the shell apart (F53), still come every 0.52 Gyr.
- **F69 A basin's collapse breaks a thin lithosphere into rings.** A large crater collapses by drawing the weak substrate under the lithosphere into its cavity, and the inflow drags the lithosphere toward the basin until it fails in concentric normal faults (ring tectonics: Melosh and McKinnon 1978; McKinnon and Melosh 1980). The lithosphere through the collapse is the ice colder than 240 K, 0.88 of its melting point (McKinnon and Melosh 1980; Johnson et al. 2013), on the shell's conductive profile at the impact's time and place: Callisto's was 19 km four billion years ago, where McKinnon and Melosh read 15 to 20 km from Valhalla's rings. Melosh's (1982) measure of the drag against the strength, gamma = sqrt(3) S D_tr / (4 Y z), with the drag S = 0.1 rho g H, H half the transient diameter, and the strength of a brittle lithosphere Y z = (1/2)(1 - 1/k) rho g z² for the friction of ice, 0.69 (Beeman et al. 1988), as Singer et al. (2023) evaluate it for Europa's ring graben, comes to 0.06 (D_tr / z)², the transient diameter from McKinnon and Schenk's (1995) scaling. Above 2 the lithosphere fails, and above 5 it breaks into an annulus of graben (Melosh 1982); between, the annulus reaches out by degrees. The drag needs a substrate that flows in: the share of the basin's own wavelength it relaxes within 1 Myr (F39), whole under an ice shell and nothing under a rocky body, whose mantle this model does not relax, so the Moon's basins keep their own rings and no more. The graben run from 3 to 8 transient radii (Tyre 3.6 to 7.7, Callanish 2.8 to 6.9, Valhalla 4.2 to 8.4 with its last ring at 10.2, Asgard 2.5 to 5.9, Ganymede's furrows 1.8 to about 10.4). Each is two normal faults dipping 62 degrees that meet at the base of the lithosphere, so 1.05 times the lithosphere wide (20 km at Valhalla, where they are 15 to 20), the next five widths on (100 km at Valhalla, 75 to 100 measured), spaced wider and narrower outward as the square root of the distance; a graben is as deep as a strain of 2 percent at the annulus's inner edge times its spacing, the strain falling as the square of the distance, its flanks raised by a quarter of its depth and falling away over 0.3 of its width, its faults' footwalls rebounding. Inside the graben, from 1.5 transient radii, the blocks' raised edges stand as ridges 0.6 of the graben's spacing apart, half their width wide and as high as the strain times their spacing (Valhalla's sinuous ridge zone, 55 km apart). Each ring breaks into arcs of 2 to 8 spacings, a quarter of its length in gaps, wandering off it and standing off it en echelon. The arcs are events of the finish at the basin's time, so younger craters overprint them and later lanes renew them, and each keeps the share of its relief its own wavelength keeps to the present (F39).
- **F70 A crater relaxes by wavelength.** An ice shell relaxes each wavelength of a crater's relief on its own timescale (F39): the bowl, its longest, goes first and the floor domes up, while the rim and the wall's terraces, short loads the elastic lid holds, stand (Parmentier and Head 1981; Dombard and McKinnon 2006; Bland et al. 2012 on Enceladus). The radial profile is split into bands between Gaussian blurs a factor of two apart, from the profile table's step to half the crater's radius; each band carries the wavelength at which the difference of its two blurs peaks, 6.54 times the finer blur, and keeps the share of its relief the shell lets that wavelength keep over the crater's life (F39, F66), and the smooth remainder keeps the longest band's share. The terraces, ridge rings and central peak keep the share of a wavelength a quarter of the diameter. The finish relaxes the craters it replays so, and a loop crater's restored detail; until now a replayed crater kept one share of its whole relief, its rim and terraces with its bowl, and a loop crater's restored detail none of its relaxation.
- **F71 A complex crater's relief scales with the strength length.** A complex crater collapses until its slopes stand on the target's strength against its gravity, the length the simple-to-complex transition measures. Its depth, rim height and central peak are those of the lunar crater at the same multiple of the transition (Pike's laws), scaled by the body's transition against the Moon's, and the basin caps scale the same way. So Ganymede's complex craters come out 60 to 70 percent shallower than the Moon's of the same size (Croft 1981): its 40 km craters 0.9 km deep and its 60 km craters 1.0 km, where its central pit craters measure 0.5 to 1.7 km (Bray et al. 2012). Mimas's Herschel, 139 km across, comes out 11 km deep, where it is 10 to 12. The Moon's craters keep Pike's laws, and a body of higher gravity, Mercury, gets complex craters shallower than the Moon's of the same size, as Pike (1988) measured them, where the lunar laws had given every body the Moon's depths.
- **F72 On ice the central uplift collapses into a pit.** A complex crater's central uplift in ice collapses into a central pit, by the drainage of its abundant melt, ice melting under a fraction of the shock pressure rock needs, or by the failure of a weak uplift (Schenk 1993; Alzate and Barlow 2011; Bray et al. 2012), where rock's overshoots and falls outward into a peak ring. From 8.5 transitions a crater on ice has a central pit (Ganymede's peaks give way to pits near 30 km) and from 20 a dome rises in the pit (near 60 km; central dome craters span 60 to 180 km on Ganymede and Callisto); neither has the lunar interior rings or a basin's outer ring, which on ice the lithosphere's own failure makes (F69). The pit is 0.19 of the crater across at its onset, widening as the crater over the onset to the power 0.4, up to 0.4 (the median of 471 pits on Ganymede, Alzate and Barlow 2011; Bray et al.'s 0.2 at 40 km and 0.32 at 100 km); its raised rim spans 0.34 of the diameter as a central peak would, stands as high as a peak would, about 200 m below 50 km, and breaks up around its circuit (Schenk 1993); it is half the crater's depth deep. A dome is 0.6 of its pit across and rises to 0.3 of the crater's depth above the floor (domes up to 1.5 km high in craters 0.8 to 1.6 km deep, Schenk et al. 2004). The pit's outline, its rim and its dome wander each by a shape of its own.
- **F73 Only the rim carries the rim's outline, and the rim's height varies.** A crater's radius was stretched everywhere by its rim's scalloped outline, so its floor's edge, terraces, rings and peak repeated the rim's shape in every contour. Now the stretch is whole at the rim and beyond and falls to nothing across the outer 0.3 of the wall, where the outermost slump blocks that scallop the rim stand (F67), so the inner terraces, the floor and the central features keep shapes of their own: where no terraces shape them, the floor's edge and the bowl take a crenulation of their own as irregular as the rim's, and each ring keeps its own outline, 0.035 of the radius. The rim stands higher or lower around the crater by 0.15 root mean square in low orders (16 to 74 percent around Ganymede's craters, Bray et al. 2012), fading out over its flanks.
- **F74 The plumes' fallout mantles the ground.** Snow laid over billions of years creeps down the slopes of what it covers, filling the hollows and thinning on the crests, so relief smaller than its thickness is buried and what stands larger is rounded by no more than it: Enceladus's old craters are muted (Kirchoff and Schenk 2009). The loose top of the deposit, what drains into fractures, is a few hundred metres (Martin et al. 2023); the whole deposit, sintered below, mantles. The finish draws the ground toward its own low-pass over twice the deposit's thickness since each surface formed (F64), by no more than that thickness either way; the deposit's mean, which would only raise the ground, is left out. The mantle buries only what is older than it: the craters too small to resolve (F54) are laid after it, and only those dug since the cover above them grew thinner than they are deep pit its surface, so a plain under thick fallout keeps the small craters younger than its last few hundred metres of snow.
- **F75 An icy moon's shell dissipates its own tides.** The provisional tidal model gave every body a Love number k₂ and a dissipation factor Q by its composition class, so that an ice shell's tides went as the eccentricity squared whatever the shell's state: Europa's came to 76 mW/m² and its shell to 7.7 km, where Juno's microwave radiometer measures a conductive shell of 29 ± 10 km (Levin et al. 2025), and a pulse of eccentricity would have thinned it without limit. A shell over an ocean now dissipates as its rheology has it (Ojakangas and Stevenson 1989; the formula of Nimmo et al. 2007 as Lemasquerier et al. 2023 give it): a Maxwell solid strained at the tidal frequency ω turns 2 μ ⟨ė²⟩ / ω · ωτ / (1 + (ωτ)²) of work per unit volume into heat, τ = η / μ its Maxwell time; the cold top is elastic, and the warm base, where the viscosity passes μ / ω, takes nearly all of it ("nearly all of the tidal dissipation is concentrated in the lowermost few kilometers of the shell"); across a conductive shell the viscosity falls by a factor e in each R T_m² / (E ΔT) of its thickness, so the shell dissipates H = 2 μ ⟨ė²⟩ / ω · arctan(μ / (η_m ω)) · R T_m² d / (E ΔT) per unit area, with the ice of F39 (η_m 1e14 Pa s, E 60 kJ/mol) and of F41 (μ 3.4 GPa). The strain rate is the diurnal tide's (F41): ⟨ė²⟩ = κ (e ω h₂ (M / m)(R / a)³)², κ = 1.92 being the orbit- and surface-averaged square of the breathing bulge's deviatoric strain; Europa's is 2.4e-20 s⁻², against Ojakangas and Stevenson's 3.3e-20. h₂ is the shell's own (Beuthe 2018): over an ocean, the core taken as rigid, the shell is a membrane spring of stiffness Λ = 8 (1 + ν) / (5 + ν) · μ d / (ρ g R²) against the ocean's buoyancy, and h₂ = 1 / (1 − 3 ρ / (5 ρ̄) + Λ); ice frozen to the rock deforms as an elastic sphere, h₂ = (5/2) / (1 + 19 μ / (2 ρ̄ g R)) (Love 1909). A large moon's shell is soft and follows its ocean (Europa 1.16, Ganymede 1.34, where the class rule had given 1.14 and 0.77); a small moon's is hard, and its own stiffness sets its tide (Enceladus 0.046, against Beuthe's 0.043 for 23 km, where the rule had given 0.43). The shell's thickness is where its conduction carries the heat from below, the rock's and its porous interior's (F68), with its own: a soft shell dissipates in proportion to its thickness, so the two meet at one thickness; a hard shell's h₂, and with it its heating, falls as it thickens. Europa's shell comes to 30 km, dissipating 0.43 TW (Lemasquerier et al.: 0.56 TW in a 25 km shell), and a fivefold eccentricity would thin it to 7 km (Kihoulou et al. 2025 model 35 km thinning to 5). Enceladus's hard shell dissipates 0.6 GW, a small share of its heat, as Souček et al. (2019) and Beuthe (2019) find; its porous core, refitted to 28.5 GW within Choblet et al.'s 10 to 30 (F68), holds its shell at 22.7 km. Ganymede's and Callisto's shells barely change (83 and 97 km); Titan's thickens from 83 to 92 km. The thermal history solves the shell at every time with the eccentricity and the heat of that time, so Ganymede's resonance pulse (F50) now takes an eccentricity of 0.0185, inside Showman and Malhotra's 0.01 to 0.07 where 0.008 had sat below it, to heat its shell to 8 mW/m² and renew two thirds of its surface. A rocky body keeps F13 with its class k₂ and Q (Io's k₂/Q 0.012 against the measured 0.015, Lainey et al. 2009). With Europa's shell at its measured thickness, the fits keyed to its old flux are refitted against the same observations: the chaos flux (F45) to 12 mW/m², the crack density (F41) to twelve, and the thin-shell share (S4) to 0.025 of the radius, between Europa's 0.019 and Ganymede's 0.031.
- **F76 A hard shell is stressed where it is thin.** A shell's tidal stresses follow its deformation, and its deformation its local Love number: a hard shell's stresses go nearly as the inverse of its local thickness (Beuthe 2018: "surface stresses are approximately inversely proportional to the local shell thickness"), a soft shell's barely change. The tidal field (F41) is scaled at each point by h₂ of the local thickness over h₂ of the mean, the local thickness being the focused thinning's (F43). Enceladus's uniform shell is stressed by 20 kPa (Beuthe: about 15 kPa in a uniform 23 km shell), under the 40 kPa at which ice cracks, and its thinned south polar shell by up to 78 kPa: its cracks open where its shell is thin, as Beuthe finds "stress amplification due to crustal thinning is absolutely necessary" for them; its old plains, which the class rule's tenfold h₂ had cracked, the diurnal tide alone no longer cracks. Europa's soft shell is stressed alike everywhere, by 82 kPa.
- **F77 A resonance cycles.** Moons in a mean-motion resonance have their eccentricities forced by it, and the heat the forced eccentricity raises can feed back on it: where an interior's dissipation rises as it warms, the heat damps the eccentricity, the interior cools and stiffens, and the resonance raises the eccentricity again, a thermal-orbital cycle (Ojakangas and Stevenson 1986; Hussmann and Spohn 2004). The Laplace resonance of Io, Europa and Ganymede is in one: the moons' measured migration rates, heat flows and tidal response select a cycle that repeats every 95 to 150 Myr, begun 0.8 to 2.0 Gyr ago (1.6 at the median), driven by Io's melt-sensitive upper mantle, with the moons now just past a peak, their eccentricity falling and Io migrating inward (Bagheri et al. 2026). Four orbital inputs carry it, as the resonance episode's three carry Ganymede's passage (F50): the cycle's period, its onset, its amplitude (the eccentricity's swing about its mean as a share of the mean) and its phase (the share of a period since the last peak). From the onset the eccentricity swings about its mean as 1 + A cos 2π(t − t_peak)/P, growing to its full swing over its first period, the mean being whatever puts the present eccentricity at the present phase; the curve is laid at 16 points a period, and the thermal history samples it as finely, up to 2,048 samples over a body's life. Io and Europa take Bagheri et al.'s representative history: a period of 0.12 Gyr from 1.6 Gyr ago at a fifth of a period past a peak, Io swinging by a fifth (0.0035 to 0.005) and Europa by a quarter (0.007 to 0.0115). Europa's shell breathes with it, between 26 and 40 km (F75; Bagheri et al. find its total shell swinging by 3 to 15 km); Io's tides, which its class k₂ and Q fix at its present temperature (S10), swing as its eccentricity squared. The coarse loop's 90 steps of 50 Myr see a cycle of 120 Myr only at their midpoints, and the processes that read the present shell (Europa's chaos, its cracks, its wholesale renewal) do not yet read its history; the Europa milestone is to.
- **F78 Europa's lineae age through stages.** Geissler et al. (1998) find that Europa's lineae age through four stages: incipient cracks under 1.6 km wide and colourless; ridges 3 to 6 km wide; triple bands, a dim medial stripe with broad diffuse margins 8 to 12 km wide tinted by a red-brown non-ice contaminant; and ancient bands, only slightly darker than the plains. The drawn lineae are these, the ones a map shows; double ridges narrower than about 2 km make the ridged plains' fabric and are not drawn one by one. A ridge's full width is drawn from a heavy tail between 2 and 7 km, the number per unit width falling as its square (a stated choice), and its height is 0.05 of its width, so 300 m at 6 km (crests are usually no higher than 300 m, Haslebacher et al. 2024). As it forms, a crack lays a stain across its margins, in a new surface layer, `stain`, 0 to 1. The stain is a Gaussian reaching 1.1 ridge widths from the axis, about 9 km across a 3 km ridge, dimmer by half over the medial stripe. It is laid over the first million years, so the youngest cracks are colourless (a stated choice), and it fades with the salts over 30 Myr (SALT_FADE_GYR) to 0.15, the ancient band. A band stains its floor. The stain follows the crack's trace, a deposit spread over the whole lineament, while the relief follows its segments (F62). A younger crack cuts away the older stain under its own width, and its margins combine with older ones by the greater of the two; a surface renewed later carries none. The stain takes the salt's colour, laid under the frost. A ridge narrower than two cells is drawn by the area of its cross-section, wider and lower at the grid's width, and makes no surface of its own; a ridge widened by gardening keeps its area the same way. A ridge or band writes its unit and age only where the grid resolves it, so no crack is drawn as a line of whole cells. Before, every 2 km crack was widened to two cells at full height and wrote whole cells of salt, which the coarse grid drew as hairlines broken in steps.
- **F79 A crack opens by the stress across its own plane and kinks by the field's shear.** A crack's trace once took the field's heading, perpendicular to the most tensile stress, at every step, and a cycloid ran whenever the most tensile stress passed the cracking stress, whatever its direction. On the equator the tidal field is symmetric about the equatorial plane, so its principal directions lie east and north, and where the two principal stresses cross, or as the orbit turns them, the most tensile direction swaps by a right angle: the traces turned at right angles there, and ran as stairs. Now a crack runs on while the normal stress across its own plane exceeds the cracking stress, so where the field swaps through the orbit a cycloid waits for the next orbit instead of turning; and it turns toward the heading it seeks by the maximum tangential stress criterion (Erdogan and Sih 1963), by 2 atan((K_I − (K_I² + 8 K_II²)^1/2) / (4 K_II)), the normal stress and shear on its own plane standing for K_I and K_II, never past the field's heading, 70.5 degrees for a crack held closed. Under near-equal tension in all directions the shear vanishes and it runs on straight; misaligned in a strongly anisotropic field it kinks sharply, as at a cycloid's cusp. Its slow wander turns the heading it seeks, not the one it has; misaligned by more than 80 degrees, where the nearer of the field's two headings is ambiguous, it keeps kinking the way it last kinked; and its heading is laid in the tangent plane at each step. The sharpest turn of a crack traced from Europa's equator is now under 45 degrees, where it was 90; the cycloids keep arcs of steady curvature broken by cusps of 10 to 20 degrees. A plume's fissures (F57) are held open by more than the tide: by the bending of their neighbours' flanking ridges, which places them, and by their own venting. They run while their zone lets them, whatever the tide across their own plane; the rule had cut half of Enceladus's present stripes short and its forks to stubs, which the twenty-third milestone found. The present episode's fissures vent to the present, their troughs renewed by their own eruptions until now, so they are laid last and no lesser crack of the zone crosses them (a lesser crack younger than a fissure had cut white gaps into its sintered trough).
- **F80 Chaos from the shell's warmth.** F45 drew chaos as patches: discs of log-uniform size placed by a two-cycle heat field, each a wobbling outline round a fixed profile of matrix and rafts, which read on the map as lace discs. Chaos now forms where the warmth the ocean brings up under the ice stands high (Schmidt et al. 2011; Soderlund et al. 2014), in generations through the surface's age: four in each retention age, evenly spaced (a stated choice; Riley et al. 2000 find that chaos and the tectonics took turns, newer chaos disrupting older), each with a warmth field of its own. The field is structured at every scale from 1,300 km, the largest contiguous chaos Riley et al. map, down to the rafts, which no finer structure can show, each finer octave keeping three quarters of the last's amplitude (a stated choice, so that a chaos breaks into patches of every size rather than a few pools), and the equator stands 0.6 of the field's spread above the poles (a stated choice for the low-latitude heat of Soderlund et al., who find chaos most common within 40 degrees of the equator). Each generation disrupts the share of the surface that leaves the chaos fraction disrupted by all of them together, 8.5% of Europa each for 30%, wherever its warmth stands above the matching threshold. There the brittle lid over a melt lens 3 km deep (Schmidt et al.: Thera and Conamara) breaks into rafts two lens depths across, the cells of a jittered lattice, which group into plates two rafts across, the cells of a coarser one (stated choices: Greenberg et al. 1999 find rafts up to several km across that carry the old terrain, the largest a few percent of their chaos's area). The disruption grows with the warmth's excess over the threshold, whole at one spread: a plate founders into the matrix with a chance of its disruption, and in a foundered plate each raft stands as a block with a chance of the rest of its own, so a chaos is a mosaic of plates at its edge and matrix with blocks in its middle. A plate or a block that stands drifts by up to 0.3 of its size and turns by up to 20 degrees as far as its disruption goes (stated choices; Spaun et al. 1998 reconstruct Conamara's rafts as moved and rotated), carrying the old surface with its ridges, ages and stains. Leads of matrix 12% of the size of what they part open between plates, round a block, and round the chaos, where the lid breaks from the shell that stays whole; a lead narrower than a cell covers its share of the cells it crosses. A raft is whole or not, so a chaos's outline follows its rafts' edges. The matrix settles at the old surface's level smoothed over four rafts (a stated choice), so that no old relief shows through it, 150 m below it; its 60 m hummocks at 1.5 km keep the share of their relief that one cell's mean holds; and the ocean's material stains it as it stains a crack's margins (F78), from the start, fading with the salts. Each generation reads the whole shell's warmth, so it is one event over the body, after the cracks and craters older than it and before the younger. Europa's chaos covers 30% of the surface, as Riley et al. map at 200 m (Hoppa et al. 2001 correct the share for lighting and resolution to 40% or more), half of it matrix, as Spaun et al. map 59% of Conamara, and is denser within 40 degrees of the equator than beyond. The export's chaos count is now the number of generations.
- **F81 The ridged plains' fabric renews the surface.** Europa's plains kept the coarse loop's first crust: the wholesale renewal of a thin shell (S6) set the retention age of the crater record and renewed no surface, so 84% of the moon carried a surface age of 4.5 Gyr, and the sub-grid crater roughness (F54) roughened it as a saturated ancient surface. What renews the plains is the fabric of narrow ridges that makes them, the ridged plains (Doggett et al. 2009). The cracks narrower than a map draws are the tail of the same population as the lineae (F78), down to 500 m (Leonard et al. 2018: double ridges 500 to 1,200 m wide in Galileo's highest-resolution mosaic; Galileo's captions put the most prominent ridges at about a kilometre), with the same heavy tail of widths and the same lengths, placed by the same tension, traced along the same field at the times they opened (in steps of four cells) and built into ridges of the same shape. None is resolved on the standard grid: each renews the share of the cells it crosses that its width covers, the cell's surface taking its time by that share; adds the area of its cross-section to their relief; and stains them by its margins as they stand at the present, the dark material it brought up going as its cross-section over margins as wide as it is, so its stain goes as its width, full from 2 km (a stated choice). The drawn ridges narrower than two cells now renew their share of the cells they cross in the same way. A strip narrower than two cells is drawn two cells wide by its share, as its relief already was, and margins and medial stripes narrower than 1.2 cells are spread over them by theirs, so that no narrow line beads along its length on the grid (the narrowest drawn lineae's margins are a seventh fainter for it). A surface renewed throughout renews one surface's worth in each retention age, its mean age: the fabric renews what the drawn lineae (17% of Europa in its retention age) and the chaos matrix (15%) do not within that age, and all of it before, where what they made has been overprinted into the plains. It is laid in slices an eighth of the retention age long on the body's own timeline, each one event over the body, back to where 5% of the surface is left unrenewed (three retention ages; a stated choice): the renewal keeps no memory, so what it has not reached takes the age it gives on average, the history's start less one retention age. Europa's surface now has a mean age of 97 Myr and a median of 96 Myr against its retention age of 100 Myr, and none of it older than three retention ages; its plains are smooth where they were rough with saturated craters, streaked by the ridges' renewal and faintly lined by the young ridges' margins over the residual stain of the old. The retention age itself is still the regime's provisional constant (S6), and the fabric's rate closes the renewal to it. Under the tidal field alone the young ridges share the field's headings over the time their margins stay dark, so the plains are combed along it; the stresses of a freezing shell, the next milestone, cross them.
- **F82 A freezing shell and a heterogeneous one.** Under the tide alone Europa's cracks followed the field's smooth headings in lanes, where the real moon's undulate and cross each other. Two stresses besides the tide now act on every crack, drawn or narrow (F81). A shell that thickens is stretched: the water that freezes onto its base takes more room as ice, and a membrane over an incompressible ocean is stretched by (rho_water / rho_ice - 1) times the thickening over the radius, which an elastic shell bears as E / (1 - nu) times that strain, some 750 kPa per km of thickening on Europa (Nimmo 2004: a thickening shell's extension passes 10 MPa). The cracks it opens relieve it as soon as it passes the cracking stress, so a thickening shell stands at the cracking stress in tension, and a thinning one, which the ocean draws in, at it in compression (a stated choice: creep and faulting hold it there), answering the thickening of the last million years (a stated choice). The cracking stress, not the several MPa a shell would bear unrelieved, keeps the tide in charge of the cracks: under a static stress of MPa no cycloid would form, and Europa has many. The freezing adds to the tide's tension where a crack starts and across its plane as it runs, and to the tension of which a cycloid's diurnal part must be a share; Europa's shell, breathing with the resonance's cycle (F77), alternates between the two, and its thinning phases open fewer cracks. And the shell's thickness varies, so a lid of varying stiffness carries the field unevenly: the stress departs from the field by a fifth of its magnitude (a stated choice), in a deviatoric pattern whose cells are twice the elastic lid's flexural parameter, the length over which the lid spreads a load and with it the stress about a stiffer or softer patch (100 km on Europa), renewed every five million years as the shell's thickness changes (a stated choice). A crack's tip feels the stress along the crack weighted by the inverse square root of the distance back from the tip, whose mean is a third of the crack's length: the heterogeneity is felt averaged over a third of the length grown so far, so a young crack answers it and a long one follows only what is coarser than itself. Where the tide pulls nearly equally every way, about the points its pattern turns on, the heterogeneity steers the cracks into a network crossing at every angle; where the tide is strongly anisotropic it bends them gently. Europa's cracks traced from the same starts turn 11 degrees per 100 km against 5 in the tide alone; followed at the tip instead, they meandered at 28 degrees per 100 km and closed in loops, and at cells of two shell thicknesses, the convection's, at 42. A plume's fissures (F57) keep to the tide: the bending of their neighbours' flanking ridges steers them along their neighbours far more strongly than the heterogeneity could (some 135 kPa on Enceladus against 16), so they stay parallel. A cycloid's crack stops and starts again with the orbit; one the freezing holds open through half an orbit would follow the field's turn into a loop some 150 km across, and ends there instead. Lanes (F61) still trace the tide alone. A crack closes toward its tips as an elastic crack under a uniform load opens, an ellipse along it: a ridge's or a cycloid's width, its stain and the surface it renews follow (4 s (1 - s))^(1/2) along it, so a crack fades to its tips instead of ending square, and renews pi/4 of its width along its length, which the fabric's budget counts.
- **F83 A band spreads.** Europa's bands were drawn as floors 100 m below the plains, stained like a crack's margins and cut into the surface. Bands stand high: they spread from a V-shaped trough along their axis, roughly symmetric, as mid-ocean ridges do, of thermally or compositionally buoyant ice, often with a ridge along their sharp margins (Prockter et al. 2002), and with a relief of a few hundred metres; and they are dilational, the surface on either side matching across when a band is closed up (Tufts et al. 2000). A band now stands 150 m above the old surface's level where its crack was (a stated choice within that relief), with a V trough along its axis down to that level over a fifth of its half-width and a ridge along each margin 0.3 of its height (stated choices), lineated parallel to its axis where the grid resolves it. Its ice spreads from the axis over 2 Myr (a stated choice), so the axis is youngest; it is grey when young, laying half a margin's stain (a stated choice: Galileo's dark bands are grey wedges), and fades with the salts. The plates it opens between are pushed apart by its half-width at each margin, the push spreading into them over half the band's length, as a crack's opening does over its length: what crossed the crack before it opened is parted by the band's width, and what ran beside it bows away. A cell across a band's edge takes the band by the share of it the band covers; a band's stamp reaches half its length, which its cap bounds.
- **F84 Io's surface is what its plumes have left on it.** Io's plains were one colour by latitude (the day's peak temperature set a frost background and a red-brown below 118 K), each live plume's ring was stamped on them with two older generations faded in a century, and flows and floors faded on fixed times. Now every patera erupts in episodes, followed back five burial times of the background fallout below (500 years; nothing laid earlier still shows): one erupting now if the patera is active and its class's share throws a plume, part way through; before that, memoryless quiet spells and eruptions from a few months to decades long (log-uniform 0.3 to 30 years: Surt's months, Tvashtar's years, Pele and Prometheus since Voyager), at the duty its class keeps, the active share times the share of active centres with a plume. Each eruption has its own speed (log-normal, 0.15) and so its range v²/g, its own mass (log-normal, 0.5), its vent's elongation (a Pele-class deposit oval to 1.3 across the vent, which runs along the patera's long side; a Prometheus-class one to 1.1; Geissler et al. 2004) and harmonics of its range with azimuth (orders 2 to 6, 0.08 rms). Its gas condenses in a ring at the canopy where the plume falls back, ending steeply beyond the range of the fastest ejecta (a Gaussian of 0.1 of the range outside, 0.25 inside); its pyroclasts, sulfur and silicate, fall within (0.15 of the crest for a Pele-class plume, 0.4 for a Prometheus-class one, falling to nothing at the range; the silicate within about a third of the range). A Pele-class ring is red sulfur from its S2 with a quarter frost, 3,000 kg/s (Spencer et al. 2000: S2/SO2 0.08 to 0.3 in some 10⁴ kg/s of SO2, the flux Zhang et al. 2003 need for Pele's column); a Prometheus-class ring is SO2 frost from 10⁵ kg/s (Jessup et al. 2004), its frost share drawn between 0.4 and 0.95 for each eruption, the rest sulfur (Geissler et al. 2004: small plumes' rings white or yellow). The fallout's mass varies by 0.35 on a sixth of the range, unresolved; it hides what lies beneath at 0.03 kg/m², tens of microns (a stated choice: a Pele-class ring shows within a few months, as Galileo saw rings form), and each eruption reaches as far as its ring's tail lays a fall that could show over its life. Every cell's top layer runs through the eruptions that reached it in order, in closed form between their starts and ends: each fall buries what lay on top as it falls; red sulfur reverts to yellow in two years (Surt's and Tvashtar's rings); a frost column sublimes at 1.9 kg/m²/yr at the equator (Ingersoll 1985: about 0.1 cm/yr), elsewhere by the SO2 vapour pressure (4,510 K over T) at the frost's own temperature by day (albedo 0.75, 122 K at the subsolar point) with its slopes towards the pole over 10 km, and over a spread of a third to three times that rate for the shorter slopes, which blends a frost line over the width its ground gives it; the sulfur falling on frost and the radiation products cover it, and fresh frost covers them in turn; and the plasma alters what lies on top to red-brown in ten years where nothing shields it (a stated choice), shielded by the column of the sublimation atmosphere, which the same vapour pressure sets, the equatorial column ten times the column that stops the plasma (a calibration: the red-brown plains begin near 30 degrees, Geissler et al. 1999, Williams et al. 2011; the column falls tenfold to the poles, Dott et al. 2025). A background fallout of 0.01 optical depths a year (a stated choice: a third of a micron) buries everything. A lava flow or a patera floor is black at the time it was laid, over its coverage, and coated by what falls on it after, rough lava thirty times slower than a smooth plain (a stated choice); one laid before the history began has been coated at the history's mean rate. A flow spreading over the plains drives off 100 kg/m² of SO2 from the ground beneath over three years, which condenses round it over 8 km (stated choices; Williams et al. 2011: white diffuse deposits round flow margins; Kieffer et al. 2000: Prometheus's plume rises where its flows advance over SO2-rich plains). The history leaves the dark silicate share in `deep`, the red in `melt`, the altered share in `stain` and the visible frost in `cover`, which the materials stage lights. About six Pele-class plumes erupt at a time (IO_PELE_CLASS_FRACTION from 0.05 to 0.15, a calibration on Geissler et al. 2004's half a dozen large rings), and an active patera's floor is a floor like any other.
- **F85 A flow field is compound.** Io's fields were six to fourteen lobes radiating from a patera's rim at random azimuths, each with its own age: worms of one width. Io's long-lived eruptions build their fields over years to decades from small breakouts, about a metre thick, on top of older flows, fed through insulated paths that carry the lava tens to hundreds of kilometres from the vent, Amirani with more breakouts along its length (Keszthelyi et al. 2001). A field now grows over a span of 3 to 100 years, log-uniform (a stated choice within "years to decades"): its trunk leaves the patera's rim on the downslope side, within 0.6 rad, and runs 0.6 to 1 of the field's reach (now 40 to 500 km, log-uniform: Amirani's and Masubi's fields are over 300 km long, NASA PIA02567), its surface 6 km wide; up to three branches leave it at 20 to 50 degrees; and breakouts, each 5 to 25 percent of the reach long and half to fully as wide, leave the trunk, the branches and the earlier breakouts, 30 percent from their fronts and the rest along them, more towards their ends, within 0.8 rad of their headings, until they cover half the reach squared twice over (at most 250; stated choices), each laid at its own time within the span. A field fans out from its trunk into lobes of many ages, the youngest overprinting the oldest, and each unit is coated by what falls on it after (F84). An active field grows at about 1,500 km² a year, as Amirani's new flows covered 620 km² in five months of 1999 and 2000. Fields are grown in the finish over its own ground from the paterae alive at the epoch, not in the loop, whose keyframes carry only the relief the loop needs: Io's loop takes 3 s instead of 142. With F85, frost no longer settles on lava laid within the last 20 years (a stated choice: Io's flows glow for years to decades), a young flow's halo lands only beside young lava, rough lava hides under 300 optical depths of fallout (about a centimetre of fines in its hollows; 30 before), so old fields stay grey long after they cool, the coverage of a lobe varies by 0.1 (0.4 before; the breakouts' ages carry the variety), and a Prometheus-class plume's pyroclasts are 3 percent of its condensate (40 percent before, which buried an active field within a couple of years).
- **F86 The plains are buried.** Io's plains carried the base terrain's bumps, slopes of 2.5 degrees rms at 3 km, where Io's plains are smooth: the regime renews its surface at a centimetre a year, so whatever relief stands lower than the burial since the surface formed is filled. The base is now buried as the loop's Io regime begins: each band of its Gaussian pyramid is shrunk by the burial depth, the resurfacing rate times the plains' age (11.4 m/kyr over 100 kyr, 1.1 km), and the broadest level kept whole, so the plains are flat apart from the body's broadest undulations and what the regime builds on them, mountains, paterae and flows.
- **F87 Io's mountains are thrust blocks.** Io's mountains were ovals 80 to 300 km across with noisy outlines, a fault scarp on one side (the rim falling as 1 − x¹²) and a slope under the plains on the other (1 − x²), ridged by noise and a quarter bare rock all over: grey blotches over 9 percent of the surface, against Io's 3 (Williams et al. 2011). Each is now a block of crust thrust up along a fault (Schenk and Bulmer 1998), its length log-normal about 130 km with a spread of 0.6, at most 600 km, its height about 5.5 km with a spread of 0.5, at most 18 km (Schenk et al. 2001: 157 km long and 6.3 km high on average, the longest 570 km, the highest 17.5 km), its length 2.5 times its width, log-normal with a spread of 0.4 (a stated choice, which with the lengths covers 3.4 percent of the surface). Its fault strikes 30 or 60 degrees from the tide's most compressive direction at its most compressive phase, as Io's mountains most often do (Seeger and de Kleer 2025); without a tide, at random. Its uplift is the fault's throw, falling as a cosine squared to the fault's tips (the fault 1.4 times the block's exposed length), times its fall across the block: a layered plateau's holds level to an erosional back that wanders along the strike, a tilted block's falls steadily, a massif's rounds (45 percent lineated, 40 layered and the rest massifs, a stated choice after Williams et al. 2011's mountain units: lineated 27 percent, layered 22, mottled 3, the rest undivided). The plains bury it to a fifth of its height, so its back and its tips are where its surface dips under them. The thrust's heave, its throw over a dip of 30 degrees (Anderson's thrust for a friction of 0.6), carries the block out over the plains the farthest where the throw is greatest, so its trace bows out towards them, as a large thrust's trace is convex towards its movement (the bow-and-arrow rule, Burg 2017's notes on thrust systems, ETH Zurich); its arrow is about a twentieth of its length, where Earth's thrust sheets' are about a tenth. Its front is the fault's scarp worn back by landslides to 30 degrees and cut back in arcs 40 km long by up to 0.15 of its width (Schenk and Bulmer 1998: Euboea Montes' landslide; the amounts stated choices), each arc counting only where it cuts; their debris lies below, 0.15 of the crest at the scarp's foot, falling off over a scarp's width. Its surface carries its own relief, unresolved: octaves from half its width down to what the grid resolves, each 0.55 of the one before (a Hurst exponent of 0.86), its spread 0.15 of its height on its dissected margins and 0.025 on its top, and none where the crust was not raised, so outliers stand only beside a block; the noise is read on a plane slanted to its lattice. A lineated block is ridged along its strike, the ridges 10 km apart and 0.06 of its height (stated choices): the noise's zero crossings drawn out eight times along the strike, so its crests wander, branch and end, in patches over the block, buried with its surface. A block takes the plains' coat and shows its rock where its slopes pass 5 to 15 degrees. The loop draws each block smooth, its scarp at least four loop cells wide, for its keyframes and the flows' ground; the finish draws it whole at its own resolution on the buried plains the loop laid it on (F86), which the loop now hands over, so nothing of the coarse block is left to take away.
- **F88 Paterae are collapses and faults.** Io's paterae were polygons of five to eight sides over 60 percent of their outline, noisy by 0.12, 10 to 200 km across, log-uniform and skewed small, their walls 0.08 of their radius. A patera's diameter is now log-normal about 34.5 km with a spread of 0.59, from 2.5 to 210 km (Radebaugh et al. 2001: 417 paterae from 2.5 to 203 km across, mean 41.0 km, median 34.5), its depth 0.6 to 1.8 km (a stated choice), its walls at 70 degrees (Chaac Patera's wall, 2.7 km high at 70 degrees, Radebaugh et al. 2001), drawn at least a cell wide. Its outline is the union of one to three nested collapses along an axis the crust's fabric gives it (F87), each containing its centre, so its margin is scalloped where they meet, as nested calderas' are (elongated, and ending at the mountains it abuts, since F90). 42 percent are cut straight by one to three faults, each along one of the fabric's strikes, their sides ending at sharp corners, and 13 percent sink at the foot of a mountain's scarp, with a straight side along its fault, where magma rises (Radebaugh et al. 2001: 42 percent with straight or irregular margins ending at sharp corners, 13 percent beside mountains). Lava that runs into a patera ponds on its floor and is the floor's, the younger lava setting its age (Williams et al. 2011 map a patera's floor as one unit): patera floors cover 1.9 percent of the surface, against 0.5 while the fields' lobes took the floors they crossed, and Io's 2.5 (Williams et al. 2011). The finish cuts each pit whole at its resolution into the buried plains (F87).
- **F89 The plains' fallout follows the volcanic centres.** Io's plains were buried everywhere at one rate, 0.01 optical depths a year, the far fallout of every plume and of plumes too small to follow (F84), so that away from the rings they were one tone at each latitude. The far fallout now spreads about each patera alive at the epoch over two of its class's ranges (a stated choice: 1,100 km about a Pele-class centre, 280 km about a Prometheus-class one), falling off exponentially, weighted by its plumes' output, the share of the time it throws one times their mass, and scaled so that its mean over the surface is the same 0.01 optical depths a year: the plains are buried fastest among the active centres and slowest far from them, from 0.004 to 0.023 optical depths a year on the default Io before the fall's clumping. The fall is clumped, unresolved, by a factor whose logarithm spreads by 0.5 from 120 km down over four octaves (stated choices). Where the plasma alters the plains, from about 30 degrees, the balance of burial and alteration follows: the red-brown plains reach towards the equator in quiet regions and yield to yellow among the active centres, mottled at 15 to 120 km. Small eruptions, the vents too small to be paterae and the stealth plumes (Veeder et al. 2012: the known volcanoes carry 60 percent of Io's heat; de Pater et al. 2020: SO emission spread widely, mostly away from known volcanoes), erupt ten a year (a stated choice of the order of the surface changes Galileo saw, more than 80 in five years, Geissler et al. 2004), their ranges 10 to 100 km (eruptions at 130 to 420 m/s; a stated choice), as many per factor of range as cover equal areas, each rising too low for a canopy, so that its frost and sulfur fall in a smooth bump about the vent, 2 optical depths at its centre (log-normal with a spread of 0.8; a stated choice), over days to months, at the latitudes the paterae favour: some 5,000 over the history, occasional fresh patches; those under two cells across are left out.
- **F90 Paterae are elongated, and end at the mountains they abut.** F88's paterae were one to three nested discs, more than half of them single circles, and those beside a mountain were cut by a straight fault at the scarp's foot. Seventy percent of Io's paterae are longer than wide by more than 1.25, up to 5.73 (Radebaugh et al. 2001): a patera's elongation is now its logarithm half-normal with a spread of 0.58, at most 5.73, half of it each collapse's own and half the spread of its collapses along its axis, a chain as calderas coalesce along a fault (Radebaugh et al. 2001: Monan's worm shape), each link of its own size and wandering across the axis by a third of its width. Paterae that abut mountains are oddly shaped, with arcuate or scalloped margins, and some wrap round them (Radebaugh et al. 2001: Gish Bar, Monan): such a patera now centres half its width out from the scarp's foot, its long axis along it, and its margin towards the block is where the block first stands above its unit's share of its height, found along 360 rays from its centre, so that it follows the foot's bow and its landslides' arcs; the block's debris inside it sank with the floor, which stays level. A faulted patera's straight sides cut across its outline as far out as it reaches each way. Patera floors cover 2.1 percent of the surface (Io's 2.5, Williams et al. 2011).
- **F91 Rays from the ejecta curtain.** A fresh crater's rays were 8 to 24 straight Gaussian spokes at random azimuths, 6 to 15 radii long and of constant width, about a glow: a lens flare on every airless body (audit T4). An impact throws its ejecta as a curtain whose mass is uneven round the crater, gathered into jets by the target's relief and heterogeneity at the rim (Sabuwala et al. 2018 made rays in a granular bed whose surface undulated, and a uniform blanket where it was smooth). Beyond the continuous blanket the jets' fragments land as secondaries that excavate fresh ground from under the space-weathered skin, densest along each jet's midline (Shoemaker 1965), and a ray is the share of the ground they excavated (Elliott et al. 2018). A crater's curtain is now the exponential of 2.5 times a unit spread of harmonics of orders 6 to 64, their amplitudes falling as the order to the 0.75, each scattered by a factor of 0.5 to 1.5, times the oblique impact's modulation of the ejecta (Gault and Wedekind 1978; Herrick and Forsberg-Taylor 2003). These are stated choices: they give some 13 jets a crater with nine tenths of the mass in a fifth of the azimuths, where Elliott et al. counted 10.7 rays a crater on the Moon, 22 degrees wide at two radii. The share excavated at distance r is 1 - exp(-A), A the jet's mass times a scale times r to the -3.48, the slope of Elliott et al.'s secondary-crater model (a - beta b / (1 - beta) with their fragment density slope a = -4.5, fragment size slope b = -5 and strength exponent beta = 0.17); the scale puts a median jet's half-excavated end at their median lunar ray length, 6.59 R^1.27 km for a crater of radius R km, a lunar calibration that a body's skin depth and fragment population would change. The skin the secondaries dig through is the Moon's 10 cm (their choice; estimates run from a few centimetres to a metre or two), or on an icy surface its sublimation lag where that is thicker, the lag's opaque mass of 2,000 kg/m² being a couple of metres of dust at a bulk density of 1,000 kg/m³ (a stated choice), and in their model the excavated area goes as the skin's depth to the -4.02 (-(2 + b / (1 - beta))): Callisto's rays are short or absent where its lag is thick (Moore et al. 1999: metres of dark lag), so 6 percent of Callisto is brightened by more than a tenth of the fresh contrast, where the Moon's skin gave 31. A ray therefore narrows to its jet's midline, breaks up and fades rather than ending. Downrange ejecta leave faster, by up to 20 percent at a grazing angle (the cosine of the impact angle; Anderson et al. 2003 measured 20 to 40 percent), which stretches a jet's reach by the square. Noise bends each jet's path past the blanket, by 0.03 radians per e-fold of distance at the lowest of its orders, and bunches its fragments in bursts whose logarithm spreads by 0.7, stretched along the path (stated choices, for the azimuthal momentum and the clumping the curtain's heterogeneity gives its fragments). The rays' median lengths come out at 16, 124 and 757 km for craters of 2, 10 and 42 km radius, against Elliott et al.'s 16, 123 and 759. Overlapping craters' rays join as independent shares do. The continuous blanket's edge is lobed by the curtain's harmonics from order 11, the 15 percent raggedness of before now in the jets that start the rays, and the secondaries of the loop's craters land along the curtain's jets, drawn by their mass, on the jets' bent paths, so that they lie in the rays. A ray freshens the ground its secondaries dug, not the melt of an older crater it crosses, which keeps the maturity of its own age: rays had turned every old melt pond they crossed into fresh dark glass. On the Moon 21 percent of the surface is brightened by more than a tenth of the fresh contrast and 4 percent by more than a half; on Mercury, whose rays fade in 0.15 Gyr, 3 and 0.03 percent; on Ganymede 28 and 7; on Europa 1 and 0.4.
- **F92 Terraces come in as a crater collapses.** F67's terraces were complete rings of slump blocks, about four at every size above the simple-to-complex transition, evenly spaced like the rungs of a ladder (audit T2). How much of a crater's wall slumps now follows how far it collapsed: the fall of its depth-to-diameter ratio from the transition's, 1 - (D / D_t)^-0.70 under Pike's complex law, none at the transition, 0.38 at twice it and 0.70 at Copernicus, against 0.6, from which the wall is terraced in full (about 3.7 transitions, 60 km on the Moon; a stated choice, Pike 1980 placing the onset of terraces just past the transition). Below it the terraces come in from the rim, the outermost first, each over the share of its circuit the collapse has reached. Each terrace inward is 0.75 of the one outside it, varied by up to a fifth from terrace to terrace (a stated choice); a block stands off its neighbours by up to 0.6 of its terrace's width (0.35 before), far enough to meet the terrace inside or outside it, so the terraces merge and split around the wall; only the blocks that slumped scallop the rim. A lunar crater of 20 km stands 0.7 terraces at an azimuth on average, one of 30 km 1.9, of 45 km 2.9 and of 93 km 3.4.
- **F93 Unresolved facets grade an icy surface's lag.** A cell of icy surface sat at one temperature, its latitude's and its resolved slope's, and since the lag's growth is exponential in the temperature (the vapour pressure) its thickness fell by orders of magnitude across a few degrees of latitude; F91's rays, whose excavated area goes as the skin's depth to the -4.02, switched on across a line on Callisto, poleward of about 55 degrees. Real icy surfaces segregate at every latitude, the warm slopes growing lag and the cold ones keeping their ice (Spencer 1987's thermal segregation), so a cell is now summed over 24 unresolved facets of equal probability, tilted toward and away from the pole by a normal spread of 35 degrees times the cell's share of saturation by the unresolved craters (F54): Callisto's Hapke mean slope, 35.1 degrees, the roughest of the Galilean satellites, whose roughness grows with their cratering (Domingue and Verbiscer 1997: Ganymede 27 degrees, Europa near none). The maturity and the rays' skin share are the facets' means, at the cell's settled albedo. Callisto's rays now come in over 30 to 50 degrees, patchy at first, its lag's latitude gradient softens with them, and 8 percent of it is rayed (6 before); the equatorial band stays rayless under its lag.
- **F94 Lava plains are compound flow fields.** A flood was drawn at the finishing resolution as the whole of its coarse low below its level, its margin the low's outline bent by noise two loop cells long (audit W1): a mare was a patchwork of blobs whose margins changed with the quality, and since a flood's composition followed only the eruptive time's drift (F55), floods close in time were alike and each mare read as one tone. A flood that can show at the end, among the last four to cover a coarse cell of its low, is now drawn as its flows (the lobes leave about three tenths of their low to their predecessors, so a deeper flood shows through less than one cell in a hundred, and deeper ones keep the coarse outline). From the coarse cell of its low nearest its vent, where its lava came in, a trunk runs across the low toward its middle, 0.6 to 1 of the low's reach long; up to three branches leave it at 20 to 50 degrees; and breakouts leave the lobes already laid, 30 percent from their fronts and the rest along them, more toward their ends, each 0.15 to 0.5 of the reach long (log-uniform), until the lobes cover the low one and a half times over, at most 60. A lobe's half width is 0.08 to 0.2 of its length, at least a cell and a half; it is a path over the base smoothed over 20 km (F59), steered by the fall line over 50 km at full slope, wandering by 0.5 rad over six of its half widths, ponding where it would climb more than a sheet (50 m), stopping where its margin would leave the flood's pass, and drawn as Io's lobes are, lobate at its width's scale. These shares are stated choices. Schaber (1973) traced Imbrium's flows 400 to 1,200 km over slopes of 1:1000, their lobes 10 to over 35 m high; LOLA shows the Phase III lobes near Euler 10 to 12 km wide and 20 to 30 m thick; mare flow units average 30 to 60 m (Hiesinger et al. 2002). The flood's sheet lies where its lobes cover at least half a cell, and where the loop filled the low deeper than one and a half sheets the pond fills it whatever the lobes do; elsewhere in the low the ground keeps the surface it had, and outside the low a lobe lays a sheet on the ground it crosses rather than filling to the low's level. A flood's composition is now its time's drift, its source's and its own batch's, at shares of 0.7, 0.7 and 0.5: the source a smooth field of three cycles about the body, for the unevenness of the mantle under the vents (titanium varies among the lunar mare basalts from place to place as well as with age; Giguere et al. 2000), and the batch a unit normal draw (several basalt types lie side by side at the Apollo 12 and 15 sites), recorded with the flood (stated choices). A mare is a mosaic of flow units of different tones with lobate fronts, and the flows' fronts show faintly in its relief. The flood passes take 47 s of the Moon's finish at 8192 by 4096 (42 before), 43 s of Mercury's (36), 156 s of Mars's (145) and 9 s of Venus's.
- **F95 Gardening blends the lava's units, and the lava fills the hollows it comes to.** Three things made F94's maria look wrong (the user: "the edges are too sharp", "they're just more blended on the Moon and have less contrast, and even more for Mercury"). Only the lava's coverage was mixed sideways by small impacts, so every flow unit kept a hard edge of tone; the mixing was capped at 24 passes of the blur (audit W4); and each flood drew its own tone at a share of 0.5 of the range, so neighbouring flows differed nearly as much as different maria. The coverage and the tinted coverage are now both mixed, the tint read back as their ratio, over each contact's own zone: small impacts move the regolith as a diffusion does, so a zone's square grows with the gardening since the contact formed, the body's diffusivity times the impacts' equivalent time (the law that degrades its craters, F38), from the spread of 4.5 km that Li and Mustard (2000) measure across lunar mare contacts of 3.5 Gyr. A contact is as old as its younger side, so each cell mixes over no more than its own lava's zone, nor more than the youngest lava within reach allows, from a power mean of the lava's gardening weighted to the least gardened (power 2, a stated choice); the eruption's time is kept in a new layer, `lava_age`, under the craters later laid on the lava, which reset the surface's age but not the contact's. The zones are the levels of a Gaussian pyramid, between which a cell takes its share, so no cap is needed. A lunar mare unit of 3.3 Gyr mixes over 3.7 km, one of 3.8 Gyr over 8.3 km; Mercury's plains, older in the model and gardened 1.5 times as fast by its diffusivity, over 5.4 km at 3.5 Gyr to 13.6 km at 3.9 Gyr; Io's flows are not gardened. Each flood's own draw now carries 0.2 of the range (0.5 before): the units of a mare are hard to tell apart by albedo alone (Iqbal et al. 2019, mapping Mare Tranquillitatis, whose boundaries are gradational), while its major units span a factor of 1.4 in reflectance (Denevi et al. 2009), the range LAVA_COMPOSITION_RANGE keeps, and Mercury's smooth plains about the same (high-reflectance red plains up to 20 percent above the global mean, low-reflectance blue plains 15 percent below). Under a crater younger than a flood, each older flood now writes its lava where its lobes reach, in their order, as beside the crater; the first flood to write a cell kept it before, so the oldest flood's coarse tone showed in cell-sized blocks under young craters' ejecta. And a crater finer than the loop's cells (the loop leaves those under 85 km on the Moon to the finish) lies deeper below a flood's level than the ground the loop flooded there: both grounds carry the same ramps between coarse cells, so only the finer relief tells, and in the ring round the low, whose coarse ground stood above the level, a ramp only raises the ground. The lava fills such a hollow to its level from where it comes in over the rim: from the body of the lava lying in the low (its connected stretches at least a coarse cell in size), over the cells its flows covered that stand no higher than they drape, into the hollow, and on past the cells the pass visits for as far as half the loop's threshold. A rim standing above the lava keeps the bowl dry, whatever lobe the smoothed ground let cross it. Before, the lava filled only the cells under its lobes, and a crater in a mare could keep a cliff three kilometres high round a part filled. The flood pass's work on each cell and the smoothing kernel now run in parallel: the event sequence takes 34 s of the Moon's finish at 8192 by 4096 (47 before) and 36 s of Mercury's (43); the mixing itself takes 3.4 s of the Moon's and 3.3 s of Mercury's.
- **F96 The discontinuous ejecta follow the curtain's jets.** Beyond a young crater's continuous blanket the deposit's cover fell off with distance the same way at every azimuth, and both the age the stamp resets and the blanket's brightness in the ray field follow that cover, so every young crater wore a uniform half-fresh disc about two radii wide. On Callisto, where the mature ground is dark, the disc was a solid grey circle over which the rays barely showed, so they seemed to begin at its edge (the user: the star shape and the rays "unlink"). The blanket's lobes came only from the curtain's finest harmonics, from order 11 up, while its mass, and the rays, are in its strongest jets. Beyond the continuous edge the deposit's thickness now goes as the curtain's mass toward the azimuth (F91: the exponential of its harmonics, mean one, at the orders the grid resolves), in full from half again the edge's distance past it (EJECTA_JET_ONSET 0.5, a stated choice of where the continuous blanket gives way): the discontinuous ejecta run out along the jets that go on as rays and leave the ground between them old. The continuous blanket is unchanged. A young crater is a bright blanket whose spikes run straight on into its rays; on the Moon, whose mature ground is closer in tone to fresh ejecta, the change is slight.
- **F97 Later craters strike out the old ring systems.** Callisto carried 77 ring systems, most from its first half billion years, their graben reaching 1,500 to 5,000 km from their basins, so arcs crossed the whole surface (the user: the rings "cover the entire surface, whereas that doesn't appear to be the case for real Callisto"). Their formation (F69, Melosh's gamma on a lithosphere of 18 km) is physical, but nothing removed them: the craters that would have, the early bombardment's, are themselves gone by the equilibrium the crater population keeps, which the replay draws only as its survivors. A ring's graben now survive by the same equilibrium the craters reach: a piece of graben stands by exp(-N/N_eq), N the craters at least as wide as the graben formed since the ring (the flux history's equivalent time, the production function and the body's flux), N_eq EMPIRICAL_SATURATION_FRACTION of geometric saturation at that width; each ring breaks into pieces of three graben widths (RING_SURVIVAL_PIECE, about what a crater as wide as the graben takes out of it with the graben's own width, a stated choice), each standing by that share, the standing ones running on as one stretch. On Callisto a ring system from 0.26 Gyr after formation keeps about 2 percent of its graben, one from 0.4 Gyr about a fifth and one from 0.6 Gyr two thirds: the youngest basins keep their rings in stretches, Valhalla's kind, and the older ones leave scattered troughs.
- **F98 Walls shed at the scale they are steep, and an icy lag from gentler slopes.** The regolith sheds from slopes past 20 degrees and stays 30 percent fresher at the angle of repose (F55), judged by the resolved slope: at 8192 by 4096, 6.4 percent of the Moon is steeper than 20 degrees, against 0.03 percent of Callisto and 0.1 percent of Mercury, so the Moon's crater walls showed white and Callisto's never did (the user: Callisto lacks the "starry" look the white rims give the Moon's small craters). Each crater now writes, in a new layer `steep`, the share of each cell's ground on walls steep enough to shed, from its own profile (tabulated at about a fortieth of its radius, finer than the grid) as it stands, degraded and on ice relaxed, so a wall sheds as much at 2048 pixels as at 8192; its ejecta bury the older walls' shares by their cover, and any other renewal clears them. The maturity takes the larger of the resolved slope's shed and the walls'. Callisto's craters are shallow (depth over diameter 0.044 for 20 to 40 km against the Moon's 0.12, as Schenk 2002 measures): their walls stand near 22 degrees at 10 km, 16 at 20 km and 11 at 40 km, under a silicate regolith's angle.
