But Callisto's crater walls and its rings' trough walls stand bright over dark plains (Galileo PIA01297 and PIA01649; Moore et al. 1999 on its dark material gathered downslope, its bright knobs and crests): an icy surface's lag is loose dust the subliming ice leaves, which the ice under it undermines, so it sheds from gentler slopes, from 5 degrees and in full by 15 (ICE_LAG_SHED_START_DEG, ICE_LAG_SHED_FULL_DEG, calibrated on Callisto), and a slope that has shed it is as clean as the ice (SLOPE_FRESHNESS_ICE 1, against 0.3 for a regolith that matures in place). Callisto's surface is speckled with bright crater rims and its ring systems drawn in bright trough walls; Ganymede's dark terrain gains bright crater rims and bright-rimmed furrows (Pappalardo et al. 2004).
- **F99 A blanket's jets run out until too thin to show.** In the ray field a young crater's blanket still ended on its stamp's footprint, three radii out, where the cover tapers to nothing over the outer quarter (F51); a strong jet's deposit, still thick there, was cut with the rest, and where a lag hides the rays beyond, as on Callisto, the jets' common end drew a circle round the star. The ray field's blanket now runs out along each jet until its cover falls under RAY_VISIBLE: to the continuous edge's distance times the cube root of the strongest jet's mass over the thickness that leaves that cover, some eleven radii for the strongest.
- **F100 Thin ejecta keep the craters under them.** At a 130 km crater on Callisto (seed 4673) the user still saw a circle: inside it only the base colour, the small craters' rims gone, the rays cut off at its edge. The crater population the finish replays is sampled from the surface ages the loop leaves (F31), and a crater's ejecta reset the surface age over its whole footprint by the cover they mix into the ground (F51), an age meant for the maturity: read as the crater record, it left no older crater anywhere under the deposit, out to a circle three radii from the centre. Thin distal ejecta subdue the craters under them rather than erase them; only near the rim is a deposit thicker than a resolved crater is deep. The cavity now renews the surface age, and beyond the rim the deposit's maturity age is kept apart in a new layer, `ejecta_age`, which the maturity reads with the surface age, the younger of the two (`LayerSet::exposure_age`); the older craters under the deposit are sampled and stamped, and buried by its thickness as before, and their walls' shares of shedding (F98) are buried by the deposit's thickness against the ground's relief, as the relief is, rather than by its cover. Round that crater the share of bright rim pixels is now even at every distance, 0.25 to 0.34, where it was 0.10 to 0.12 within three radii and 0.22 to 0.33 beyond; the replay places 81,834 craters on that Callisto (59,516 before) and 80,630 on the Moon (58,070).
- **F101 Shields are built over by their eruptions.** A shield's flanks were the loop's lava with radial ridges of noise drawn down them, its outline a circle (audit T1). A shield now shows the eruptions since its edifice last grew by three sheets of flows on average (SHIELD_FLOW_COVER, FLOOD_SHEET_THICKNESS 50 m, over its mean height), each a compound flow field as a flood's is (F94): the flood's field is now a routine the two share, a trunk from the vent, branches, and breakouts on the lobes already laid until they cover a field half as wide as its run-out is long (IO_FLOW_FIELD_AREA, as Io's fields do) one and a half times over. A quarter of the eruptions come from circumferential fissures round the summit caldera, 1 to 1.3 of the chamber's radius out; of the rest, half come from fissures on the rift zone, within 0.35 rad of its axis and 0.4 to 1 of the way to the foot, and run out 0.2 to 1 shield radius, the largest eruptions spreading onto the plains as aprons (vents at the ends of Arsia and Ascraeus Montes' rift zones feed aprons more than 100 km long; Mauna Loa's rift zones' flows run the farthest), and the others from fissures anywhere on the flanks, 0.1 to 0.7 of the way out, which with the circumferential ones run 0.1 to 0.4 shield radius and build the edifice (Chadwick and Howard 1991 on the Galápagos shields' circumferential and radial fissures); none runs farther than 1.4 times the foot's distance. The eruptions come evenly through the growth until their lobes cover the shield three times over (at most 120), the later lying on the earlier; Peters et al. (2021) measured Tharsis flows 15 to 310 km long. The loop dates the edifice's surface no later than the eruptions' start, and the finishing pass draws them then, or just after a resurfacing they outlast, which buries the earlier ones; on Venus the resurfacing had made tessera of the standing shields, and the shield's lava is now laid over it. Every flow now narrows where the ground is steep and spreads where it flattens, its width going as one over the root of one plus the square of the slope over 0.027 (Hulme 1974's levéed flows; calibrated so that a 5° flank narrows a flow to a third of its width on the plains: Peters et al. measured Tharsis flows 0.5 to 29 km wide, the narrowest on Olympus Mons's flanks), and on ground steeper than a degree the fall line's pull on a flow grows with the slope, up to ten times, as gravity's push across its front does, so that on a shield's flank a breakout swings downhill within a few kilometres rather than walking sideways (FLOW_STEEP_SLOPE, a stated choice above the plains' slopes). On a shield's flanks a flow is a channel finer than the grid at the standard quality, spreading into lobes on the summit plateau and at the foot; the plains' floods are unchanged and 0.7 percent of Io's pixels change by more than 16 levels, the same fields with lobes moved where they cross ground steeper than a degree. A shield's lavas come from one reservoir under its summit, which mixes the batches that feed it: they take the source there, the drift by their time and the shield's own batch, an eruption's varying about it by 0.3 of a flood's (each Hawaiian volcano keeps its own composition); the edifice's lava between the eruptions takes the composition of the eruptions' start, out to the edifice's outline. The edifice is longer along its rift zone by an axis ratio of up to 1.4 (a stated choice: the Tharsis Montes stretch along the line of their rift aprons, Ascraeus Mons 375 by 870 km with its aprons), and its foot is lobed by harmonics of orders 3 to 24 whose amplitudes fall as the order to the 0.75, as the ejecta curtain's do (F91), 4 percent rms, coming in as the square of the distance from the summit so that the summit stays round.
- **F102 Calderas from summit collapses.** A summit had one to three flat circular pits, the first a tenth of the shield's radius at the summit, the others smaller beside it. A caldera is now its summit's collapses: each time an eruption drains the chamber its roof sinks into it, a pit about as wide as the chamber; between collapses the vent wanders by up to 0.6 of the last pit's radius and the chamber shrinks as the supply wanes, each pit 0.5 to 0.95 of the last and sunk deeper, toward 12 percent of the shield's height (at most 3 km), into the floors the later lava ponded in; a younger pit cuts the older ones it overlaps, its outline scalloped by its wall's slumps (6 percent of its radius), its long axis up to 1.3 times its short. A summit sinks in one to seven collapses, fewer more often (Olympus Mons's summit holds six in 65 by 80 km, the youngest the smallest and the deepest: Mouginis-Mark et al. 1991; Mouginis-Mark 2021). The first pit's radius is 0.05 to 0.28 of the shield's, log-uniform (Zeus Patera, Olympus Mons's largest, some 60 km across on a 600 km edifice; Arsia Mons's caldera about 120 km on 450 km; Pavonis Mons's older one about 100 km on 375 km; Mauna Loa's 6 km on some 120 km). The caldera floors are the lava the last summit eruption ponded in them.
- **F103 A shield buries the troughs it grows over.** A canyon or graben formed before a shield kept its full depth through the shield's growth: the loop laid the shield's lava as an even drape over the canyon it had carved, and the finishing pass cut each trough's detail out of the final ground, so troughs ran through cones kilometres thick (the user: "The canyon partially cuts through the shield's cone, leaving a bit of the cone visible in the valley"; on that Mars the canyon was done 3.49 Gyr ago, and the shield began 3.46 Gyr ago and grew 17 km high). Lava ponds in a trough it comes to rather than draping it. In the loop a shield's growth now fills the canyon depth carved under it as fast as the shield thickens there; in the finishing pass a trough's depth, and its coarse part the loop carried, is less the lava the shields laid on it since it formed (`shield_burial`), its raised flanks likewise. A trough older than a shield's growth is gone under the shield's upper flanks and comes out from under its foot, where the lava thins; one younger cuts it. Where a graben does cut a cone, its floor keeps the cone's slope, as a down-dropped block does.
- **F104 A rise turns the body under it.** A plume's rise stayed where its plume rose: Mars's came out at 83 degrees south, its shields round the pole (audit M9), where a load the size of Tharsis turns a planet until it lies on the equator. A body turns until its axis of greatest moment of inertia is its spin axis. The rotational bulge resists the turn only in the share that the elastic lithosphere holds frozen as the body turns under it (Willemann 1984; Matsuyama et al. 2006): the fluid bulge, (k_f / 3) ω² R⁵ / G, k_f from the moment of inertia factor by the Darwin-Radau relation (3/2 for a uniform body, 0.93 for the Earth), times the share of a degree-two load the lithosphere holds up by its membrane strength (Turcotte et al. 1981, with E 100 GPa and ν 0.25, its bending negligible at degree two), its elastic thickness taken when the rise grows. A plume holds its rise up rather than isostasy, so the rise's mass, its dome's at the crust's density, turns the body against that frozen bulge alone (the Earth's two great upwellings lie under its equator; Bouley et al. 2016 on Tharsis's turn of Mars). The rises are drawn where their plumes rose, the inertia tensor summed over their domes with the frozen bulge about the old axis, and the body turned the shortest way to its greatest axis; everything on the rises, its shields and its rifts, is placed where they stand now, and each rise records the latitude it began at (the sidecar's paleo_lat_deg). Mars's lithosphere holds 1.6 percent of a degree-two load when its rise grows, and its rise is carried from 83.1 degrees south to 0.8 south; Venus, whose slow spin leaves almost no bulge, turns its three rises from 63 north, 36 south and 28 north to within 12 degrees of its equator, the great circle their masses choose. Nothing on a rocky body yet depends on the latitude it had before the turn; the climate's processes, in Phase 5, will.
- **F105 Graben and canyons follow the rise's stress.** A rise's faults ran out along its radii from random points on its flank, so its graben were spokes and its canyon a spoke among them, and they formed only until 2 Gyr after the body did, so the lava that kept flooding the rise buried nearly all of them (audit T1). The lithosphere bends over a plume's dome: on its crest it is in tension every way alike, on its flanks in tension round the rise and squeezed along the radius, at its foot squeezed, along the radius the most; and the rise's load stretches the ground beyond its foot round the rise, as a load on a spherical shell does (Banerdt et al. 1992). The surface stresses now go as the dome's curvatures, -(w'' + ν w'/r) along the radius and -(w'/r + ν w'') round it, with the load's hoop tension beyond the foot, a tenth of the crest's there and falling as the square of the distance. A fault starts where the tension stands above a twentieth of the crest's, as often as the tension is strong (drawn from the tabulated distribution, where a failed rejection had left some starts at random as far as the far side of the body); it strikes across the most tensile direction, along the radius where the hoop is the more tensile and round the rise where the radial is, turning toward it by a quarter a step as far as the stress is anisotropic; it keeps its own heading where the tension is within 15 percent of the same every way, so that the crest is cut by short graben every way, a maze as Noctis Labyrinthus's; and it dies out where the tension falls below a twentieth of the crest's, so that the graben stand within 1.4 rise radii and the canyons, the longest faults, run from near the crest out along the radius. A fault slips as far as the length it reached allows. Faults form from the rise's start to the present at the pace of the body's volcanism, which builds the load (Tharsis faulted from the Noachian into the Amazonian: Anderson et al. 2001), so on Mars a quarter of the graben are younger than 1.7 Gyr; the floods bury the older graben and the shields the ones under them (F103), and the younger cut both. These shares are stated choices.
- **F106 Canyon walls fall in landslides.** A canyon was a flat floor between smooth walls, scalloped a little, so a chasma read as a clean slot. Valles Marineris's walls have retreated in giant landslides, some fifty large ones from 3.5 Gyr to 50 Myr old, 350 to 460 km³ each, whose debris ran out across the floor tens of kilometres, the larger the farther for their fall (Quantin et al. 2004; Fahrboschungen of 0.07 to 0.12, McEwen 1989). Where a trough's walls stand at least 1 km high, they now carry landslides one after another along each wall: an alcove 1 to 4 wall heights in half width (log-uniform), cut back into the plateau by 0.3 to 0.8 of that, the next 0.5 to 3 alcove widths on; its volume, half an elliptic cone, runs out across the floor 7 to 14 times its fall, as far as the floor goes, in a lobe that widens by half its alcove's width toward its rounded toe, thins to its edges and drapes the wall's lower slope, holding the alcove's volume. An alcove 30 km wide cut 8 km back into a 6 km wall holds some 380 km³ and lays a lobe 400 m thick running 60 km. The sizes go with the wall's height, so the landslides grow with the canyon; the shares are stated choices. On the Mars of the standard quality the landslides do not show: the large canyons opened in the first half of its life and are mostly filled by the floods that came after them (audit M14), their walls worn since, and the young canyons' walls, a kilometre and a half high, cut alcoves a few kilometres across, under the grid. Fixed in the forty-second milestone: where a grid's walls left the floor no room, as the loop's cell-wide walls do near a canyon's ends, a lobe a metre long had made the debris tens of kilometres thick and emptied the canyon at each landslide, so the coarse canyon ran in gaps along its length and the finish rebuilt the ground the floods found a kilometre too low there, in bars across the floor. The debris now spreads over its lobe and the drape on the wall's foot together, the landslides follow the canyon's own walls whatever the grid draws them at, and a grid draws only the landslides wider than its cell, leaving the finer to the finish.
- **F107 A full lake spills into the next low.** A flood filled one lake from the nearest low, taking cells in height order across passes: past a pass it went on taking the ground that fell away beyond, until the lake would have spread thinner than 50 m on average, and filled all it had taken to one level. Where it stopped, on the far side of a pass, the lake stood level over the open ground beside it, as a liquid cannot. On Mars, in 69 percent of the floods the ground beside the lake lay more than 50 m below its level, in 18 percent more than 100 m, and in the worst 4.6 km. Under the thin sheets of the floods that followed, those steps were the staircase of straight edges a loop cell (21 km) long on Mars's and Venus's volcanic plains (audit W7). A lake now fills only to its lowest pass. A cell on its edge more than a flow's thickness (50 m) below the highest ground it has taken lies past a pass, so the lake is full to the pass, and the rest of the lava runs on from that cell by the steepest way down to the next low, where it fills a lake of its own, and so on, up to 32 lakes (FLOOD_MAX_SPILLS, a stated choice; one flood each on the Moon and Mercury reached it). A cell joins a lake only where the volume lifts the lake and the cell to a flow's thickness above the cell, so a lake ends in a flow's front no higher than 50 m wherever it stops; this replaces the rule of 50 m on average. When a later lake of the same flood reaches an earlier one, it takes the earlier lake's lava as its ground and the two rise as one. No lake on the four bodies with floods now stands more than a flow's thickness over the open ground beside it, except where its lava there is thinner than that, draped over a crest. About half the floods fill one lake, a fifth fill two, and the rest fill three or more. The flows' reach, 1,500 km from the vent, now ends the cascade. A lake at the reach rises no more than a flow's thickness above the ground beyond it, and the lava left over is not laid: 1 percent of the Moon's erupted volume, 9 percent of Mercury's, 18 percent of Mars's and 24 percent of Venus's. That lava would have run further than a flow reaches, leaving its sheets on the slopes it ran down, which the loop does not draw (audit M15). In the finish, the hollow fill (F95) now takes a hollow in the ring round a flood's low only where the fine ground lies a flow's thickness below the ground the loop left there, which marks a crater the loop never saw. Where the ring stands between the low and lower ground beyond, the coarse resampling's ramp also carries the fine ground below the level, but that is an open slope the lava runs down, not a hollow it ponds in.
- **F108 A rise's shields take its eruptions.** A rise drew the floods' vents all its life, by its fertile mantle and its dome, so on Mars the floods kept coming over the rise to the present and filled the canyons that opened on it (audit M14). Mars's volcanism gathered at the large shields of Tharsis and Elysium in the Amazonian, after the widespread plains volcanism of the Hesperian (Carr and Head 2010), and Valles Marineris, younger than the plains about it, stands open. A rise's shields now take its eruptions as they start. From each shield's start, what the rise adds to the vent weights, over the weight a cell would have without it, goes by that shield's share of the shields' volume to the shields, whose edifices and flows are their own process (F101), and leaves the floods' weights and budget. On Mars the share is nothing until the first shield starts at 1.05 Gyr, 72 percent from 1.2 Gyr, when the largest starts, and all of it from 2.23 Gyr; the shields take a quarter of the body's eruptive budget. Venus's shields, all started before its resurfacing, take 61 percent of what erupts after it. The bodies without rises keep their floods as they were. The canyons carved after a rise's shields start now keep their relief: along its trace, Mars's largest, 7.4 km deep and carved from 1.0 to 2.0 Gyr, keeps a median 2,980 m where the floods had left 790 m, one 3.8 km deep carved from 1.4 to 2.4 Gyr 1,750 m where they had left none, and those on the rise carved since 1.9 Gyr 460 to 1,120 m where they had left up to 630 m (the narrow ones show only about half their depth on the loop's 21 km cells); one beyond the rise's foot, in the plains, is buried by their floods, as it was before. Those carved before 1.4 Gyr, during the plains volcanism, stay buried, as the old graben under Tharsis's lavas are. The rise's crest now shows its older ground, its graben and craters, where the late floods had buried them. The shields keep their own size rule, so their edifices hold 8.3 million km³ of the 61 million km³ they take; the rest is not laid. Two other ways were tried and set aside. With the share growing as the shields grew, Mars's largest canyon was filled during its carving, when the shields held less than half the rise's eruptions. With the shields' share erupted as floods from the shields, what ran on from the shields filled canyons 1,300 km away; held to the shields' aprons, 98 percent of it ran past them and was lost, since the loop ponds lava only in closed lows (audit M15).
- **F109 Floods leave their sheets on the slopes they run down.** The loop's lava ran from its vent, and from each full lake's pass, down the steepest way to the next low and laid nothing on the way, ponding only in closed lows (F107), so the slopes it crossed stayed bare and lava whose cascade ran past the flows' reach of 1,500 km was not laid at all: 1 percent of the Moon's erupted volume, 9 percent of Mercury's, 12 percent of what Mars's floods erupt and 9 percent of Venus's (audit M15). It now leaves its sheet, a flow's thickness (50 m), on each cell it crosses, and as wide as its volume spreads it: its area at a flow's thickness, laid in a swath 2.2 times as long as wide round its path, on the cells it need not climb to, nearest first, until its volume is spent or it reaches a low, where the rest ponds as before (FLOOD_SHEET_ASPECT, from the Roza flow field of the Columbia River basalts, some 1,300 km³ over 40,300 km² and 300 km long: Thordarson and Self 1998). A flood of 100 km³ lays a swath 30 km wide, one of 10,000 km³ a swath 300 km wide. Less than 1 percent of any body's lava is now lost past the reach. Sheets carry 45 percent of the Moon's lava, 44 percent of Mercury's, 56 percent of what Mars's floods erupt and 83 percent of Venus's, lakes the rest; the Moon's lava now covers 16.3 percent of its surface (12.5 before), against the 17 percent the maria cover (Head 1976), and Mercury's 30.9 percent (23.0), against the 27 percent of its smooth plains (Denevi et al. 2013). In the finish a sheet lies its thickness over the fine ground it crosses, the coarse thickness sampled smoothly along the slope, rather than filling it to a level, which a sheet on a slope cannot be; it drapes the hollows it crosses rather than ponding in them, and its surface is no level for the hollow fill (F95) to fill a hollow beside it to. A hollow the fill takes is finer relief: the fine ground lies below the floor the lava's level stands over while the ground the loop saw there, the base less what came after, does not (the gaps that the sheets' fine outlines leave on a slope stand below the base as well, and the test of the fine ground against the base alone had let the fill walk 40 km down a slope, to a lake's level). An older flood now leaves the ground alone only inside the rim of a younger crater: beyond it the crater's restore buries the flood's relief by its ejecta's thickness, so the flood's sheets and gaps fade under the ejecta rather than stop at a ring 2.3 crater radii out, where the base's coarse lava under the whole ejecta had stood each young crater's ejecta on a faint step that the sheets made plain. Where a flood's flows did not reach, the surface it found comes back only if no crater formed since covers the cell, whose coarse cell the loop's record of that surface can miss.
- **F110 A flood's flows cover each of its runs, and its fills follow the fine relief.** After F109 the Moon's plains showed blocks a coarse cell across, outlined by steps a few hundred metres high (the user, on the forty-second milestone's images). Three things made them. A visible flood's flows were bound by its cap and started at its vent, where its lava came in; a cascade's vent lies on the cap's edge, so a wide trunk's margin crossed the bound before it began: on the Moon 47 visible floods drew no flows at all, and the flows of the rest covered about a third of their cells. Each flood's pass also takes its coarse smear off where its flows do not reach, and the lava goes nowhere: the fine passes keep about four fifths of the loop's lava (audit M16), and where floods stack the fine ground drifts below the loop's, 490 m at one traced cell. And a lake's fill restored that drift only within its own coarse cells: a cell beside it was filled only if its own coarse cell's level put it below the lake, so the fill stopped in a step along the coarse cells' edges, at the cells of a flood's sheet or the ring round them. A flood's flows are now traced for each run of it, a sheet or a lake, from the run's cell nearest the vent toward the run's middle, until they cover the run FLOOD_LOBE_COVER times over, and they stay within two coarse cells of the flood's own cells, which lie inside the cap its pass may write. A lake's fill enters any cell beside it lying below its floor where the loop's own ground stands at the floor, whatever the cell's coarse cell says, so it follows the fine relief; and a sheet starts fills too, filling the finer hollows it crosses to its surface, while down an open slope the loop's ground falls below that and the slope stays as it is (the strips the flows missed time after time had stood as trenches 100 to 150 m deep). A buried flood's sheet lies everywhere the loop laid it: its outline, hidden under the flows after it, had cut a step a sheet deep along the coarse cells. Lava now covers 15.5 percent of the Moon (17 percent measured), 27.5 percent of Mercury (27 percent) and 50 percent of Mars. Tried and set aside: spreading each pass's lost lava evenly over the cells it laid. With the flows covering about a third of their runs the laid cells took one to four sheets more and the plains turned grainy, and capped at a sheet the flows' lobes dimmed and ghost craters showed through.
- **F111 A rise's faults grow in straight segments that link; graben are as wide as their faulted layer, and rifts are half-graben.** A rise's troughs read as worms (the user): each fault's trace curved smoothly as it steered toward the stress field step by step, its depth and width came from its length (a thousandth and a half of it deep, thirty depths wide), so every trough tapered as a lens from tip to tip, and the long ones swelled and scalloped like chasmata. Graben are not like that. Their bounding faults meet near the base of a faulted layer near the surface, so a graben's width is set by the layer, not by its length: Tharsis graben stand 1 to 8.7 km wide, 3.7 km on average, on a profile across northern Tharsis (Plescia 1991), over faulted layers 0.4 to 4 km deep (Davis, Tanaka and Golombek 1993, after Golombek 1979), and the width correlates poorly with the layer's thickness (Schultz 2000). Faults grow by the linking of segments, and their throw peaks near the middle of each, about a hundredth of its length, with minima where segments link at relay ramps (Hauber et al. 2013, for faults round Tharsis; 0.007 in Memnonia Fossae). Each fault on a rise now draws its graben's width, log-normal about 3 km with a spread of 0.5 in its logarithm (0.8 to 12 km), and its faulted layer's thickness apart from that, log-normal about 1.2 km (0.4 to 4 km). A fault whose throw over its whole length, as before, would exceed the layer cuts deeper and opens a rift, as wide as its throw allows; the others are graben, whatever their length. A fault grows in straight segments, 2 to 10 trough widths long (log-uniform): within a segment its trace keeps to a great circle; where one ends the next turns toward the stress field by 0.6 of the way as far as the stress is anisotropic, and by a few degrees more or less (a normal spread of 4 degrees), and three times in ten it steps sideways by half a width to a width and a half over a link at 45 degrees, always to the same side along a fault (en-echelon). A graben's segment throws 0.008 of its length at most, near its middle, with the log-normal scatter faults have, no more than its layer nor a fifth of its width (the graben on Plescia's profile stand 10 to 636 m deep); where segments link the throw falls by half, at the fault's ends to nothing as an ellipse, and the trough keeps its width between straight walls to within a width of its tips, where its faults meet. A rift keeps its throw along its length, tapering at its ends as before, its width swelling and its walls scalloped as chasmata, and each of its segments is a half-graben: its floor tilts down toward its master fault, the far wall standing 0.4 lower, and the master fault's side flips from one segment to the next half the time, the floor turning over a transfer zone a width long about each link (the East African rift's basins switch polarity across accommodation zones every 60 to 100 km, Rosendahl 1987). The extension a fault takes up is its heave over each segment's mean throw. A neighbour en-echelon to a fault follows its host's segments. On Mars, the graben's median width is 3.0 km (3.3 before) and their median depth 267 m (110), deep enough to read at the finish's cells, and of its 22 troughs wide enough for the loop, 17 remain, the long rifts. On Venus, whose standard finish cells are 9 km across, the graben are mostly finer than the grid and its rises' radial fans of graben, drawn 10 to 15 km wide before, now show faintly; at the high quality they show at their width. Rings round basins (F69) and fractures round coronae keep their profiles, unchanged. Segments, linkage and half-graben are stated choices of their shares; the layer as a field over the rise (the faulted layer thickens toward Alba's summit, Davis et al. 1993) and pit chains along the graben are left.
- **F112 Graben keep their throw along their length, their walls are rough, and they stand in parallel sets.** After F111 the user saw graben that looked short, walls of an even width and too straight, and few graben running parallel, and asked that each be checked against measurement before any change. On the MOLA relief (the user's reference map, 2.6 km a pixel) the graben of Claritas and Ceraunius Fossae run unbroken across hundreds of kilometres, pinch and swell along their length, wander a little, and stand in sets of four to six parallel graben some 10 km apart; ours read as chains of dashes, since each segment's throw fell by half at every link, and a neighbour stood 0.8 to 3.5 widths from its host, merging with it at the map's cells. Faults confined to a layer have flat-topped displacement profiles, with low gradients along most of their length and steep ones at their tips (Soliva and Benedicto 2005), and they grow by linking, their greatest throw rising far more slowly than their length. A graben's throw now runs along its whole length at SEGMENT_THROW_PER_LENGTH of its segments' mean length (with the log-normal scatter faults have, within its layer and a fifth of its width), rising from each tip at twice that rate; its segments scatter about it by 0.15, the profile turning smoothly from one segment's throw to the next, and dips by a quarter at each link (by half before, a stated choice; minima at relay ramps, Hauber et al. 2013). A fault's surface is rough at every scale, self-affine with a Hurst exponent of about 0.8 across its slip (Candela et al. 2012, from surface ruptures 200 m to 50 km long), so its trace is too: a graben's two walls, each such a trace, stand in or out on their own by 0.3 of its width over wavelengths from six widths down, so it pinches and swells, and every fault's trace, a graben's or a rift's, wanders off its segments' great circles by half its width over wavelengths from twenty widths down (three octaves, each 2^-0.8 of the last; stated choices). Confined faults' spacing is linear in their mechanical layer's thickness, log-normal, at least a quarter of it (Soliva, Benedicto and Maerten 2006): a graben that joins another as its neighbour now stands apart from it, wall to wall, by four thicknesses of its faulted layer, log-normal with a spread of 0.5, no less than a quarter of one (the median a stated choice, setting the graben of a set a width or two apart as on the MOLA relief); chasmata keep their spacing of a width or a few. The user's sense that the graben should be longer was half right: our graben average 85 km on Mars, as the 8,500 faults of the Mars fault catalogue average 80 km (Knapmeyer et al. 2006), with the cumulative exponent near -2 that fault populations show (Zou et al. 2024); but Tharsis's longest graben systems, Memnonia, Sirenum, Icaria, Thaumasia and Claritas Fossae, radiate 3,000 to 4,000 km from the rise (Wilson and Head 2002), and those are read as the surface of giant dike swarms the plume fed (Ernst, Grosfils and Mège 2001), a process the model lacks (audit M17). Mars keeps 16,970 graben (16,280), their median depth 188 m (267), within the 10 to 636 m measured across northern Tharsis (Plescia 1991).
- **F113 A rise's plume and volcanoes send out dike swarms, a graben above each dike.** Tharsis's longest graben systems, Memnonia, Sirenum, Icaria, Thaumasia and Claritas Fossae, radiate 3,000 to 4,000 km from the rise (Wilson and Head 2002), and Venus carries 163 radial systems of graben, fissures and fractures, at least 72 percent of them made by dikes, radii averaging some 325 km and some beyond 2,000 km (Grosfils and Head 1994); Mars bears up to 16 giant dike swarms, most about Tharsis (Ernst, Grosfils and Mège 2001), and dikes lie beneath the graben south of Arsia Mons (Goudy and Schultz 2005), though whether one swarm or several, and how much of the radial faulting is the regional stress instead, is debated. The model's graben were tectonic only, averaging 85 km on Mars, and these systems did not form (audit M17). A rise's plume head now sends out 8 swarms over its active life, each reaching 0.5 to 1.4 rise radii (log-uniform), and each volcano on the rise one, reaching 2 to 8 of its radii; a swarm fans over 30 to 360 degrees (log-uniform) with 8 to 30 dikes (log-uniform). Each dike leaves its source on its own heading within the fan, from 0.1 to 0.3 of the source's radius out, and runs 0.2 to 1 of the swarm's reach (log-uniform), on along the rise's stress trajectories in segments as a fault does (F111), magma-driven whatever the tension. A graben opens above it as long as the dike, twice as wide as the depth to its top (Mastin and Pollard 1988; nearer one to one at Krafla, Rubin 1992), which the graben's width distribution stands for, subsiding as a graben over its faulted layer does (F112). A swarm's dikes come at one time, drawn as the rise's faults' are, from the volcanism's history. The swarms take none of the rise's extension, the dikes' own opening taking it up. On Mars, 68 graben now run more than 1,000 km (3 before) and 13 more than 2,000 km, the longest radiating across the rise and onto the plains; on Venus, 63 more than 1,000 km, in fans about its volcanoes. The counts, reaches and shares are stated choices, the giant swarms' reach from Tharsis's; the swarms' magma is not yet the plume's intrusive budget, nor are pit chains drawn where the ground collapses into a dike (Mège and Masson 1996).
- **F114 A rise grows along an axis: its volcanoes line up on it, and its stress gathers the faults along it.** The user saw that Tharsis's graben do not radiate the same every way: its giant dike swarms and their graben run out mostly to the south-west and the north-east, and the ground between is largely free of them; was that later volcanism burying them? In part, and the model draws that: the floods and shields that come after a graben fill or bury it, and on Mars young lavas cover the ground west of the rise. But the bilateral pattern is also the regional stress. Tharsis's dike swarms propagated through a stress field made by the rise's growth, and its faults formed where that stress met magmatism concentrated along the Tharsis Montes' axial trend (Orlov et al. 2023, in Tempe Terra); at Alba Patera a regional extension, to which the local uplift added its own radial stress, shaped the dikes (Cailleau et al. 2005); dikes follow the total stress, a source's radial stress and the regional stress superposed (Odé 1957; Muller and Pollard 1977, at the Spanish Peaks, where a regional stress difference under a twentieth of the magma's driving pressure turned them; two to five times that by Mériaux and Lister 2002). The model's rise stress was axisymmetric, its volcanoes stood round its centre at any azimuth and its swarms fanned any way, so its faults and dikes radiated evenly. A rise now grows along an axis through its centre, its azimuth drawn per rise, as the first dikes across its crest, where the tension is the same every way, break its symmetry. Its volcanoes stand on the axis to either side of the centre, within SHIELD_AXIS_SPREAD_DEG (20 degrees, normal). Its stress adds a pure shear, tension across the axis and compression along it, RISE_AXIAL_STRESS (0.07) of the crest's tension, carried along the great circles from the centre and fading beyond the foot as the load's own stress does, as the inverse square of the distance. The faults start as often as the total tension exceeds RIFT_MIN_TENSION, over the distance from the centre and the azimuth from the axis, and run, as the dikes do, along the total stress's trajectories; a swarm fans along the axis, to one side or the other of its source. On the crest the tension stays nearly the same every way, its maze kept, the share being under RIFT_LABYRINTH's 0.15; along the axis beyond the foot the load's tension and the axis's add, so the faults reach some 1.8 rise radii instead of 1.4; across it the tension stays under RIFT_MIN_TENSION everywhere beyond the foot, and none form. A pure shear fading as the inverse of the distance, tried first, overtook the load's stress beyond some 1.4 radii and turned the dikes into the axis there, as the regional stress did at the Spanish Peaks, but it kept the ground along the axis faulting out to 2.3 radii and raised canyons alone on the plains as far as 5,600 km from the rise's centre, which Mars does not show; dikes turning far from their source wait for a regional stress from its own sources (audit M18). On Mars the graben and canyons beyond 1.1 rise radii, 604 spread evenly about the rise before, are 1,437, all but 6 within 60 degrees of the axis and 1,038 within 30; on the rise they favour the axis a little (2,623 within 15 degrees of it, 1,506 at right angles), and the volcanoes line up along it; Mars keeps 17,622 graben (17,290 before) and 19 canyons (17), 3 of them beyond the foot (1); Venus's three rises take axes of their own. The share and the spread are stated choices; the azimuth is drawn, not yet computed from the stresses that would set it (audit M18).
- **F115 A shield's lava buries what it grows over.** The user saw craters half on a shield and half on the plains about it, the half on the shield raised sharply with it. The loop grew a shield by adding its thickness to whatever stood there, so a crater on the ground it grew over rode up on it: it filled a canyon it came to (F103) but nothing else. A 210 km crater at the foot of a shield at 135°W, 18°N, which formed 2.27 Ga as the shield grew, kept a floor tilted from 13.4 km on the flank down to 5.7 km against 5.0 km on the plains. A shield's lava now buries the ground it grows over as a deposit buries what it falls on, by the ejecta's law (BURIAL_FACTOR_LOW and _HIGH, provisional, a stated choice for lava as for ejecta): the relief of that ground, measured against its mean in SHIELD_BURIAL_RINGS (32) rings about the edifice's centre, the surface less the lava the shields have piled there and less the canyons' open depth, fills where low and drowns where high as each step's lava thickens. In the finishing pass a crater, restored from the loop or drawn there, and a trough (F103) keep the share of their relief that the lava the shields laid on them after they formed has not reached, 1 - T/A for lava T thick over a relief A, across their whole extent (`lava_keep`), and a crater's surface no longer shows where the lava took most of it. F103 had taken the lava's thickness off the trough's depth, which, the faults now being older than the shields (F116), left only the deepest line of a buried graben's floor, a hairline wandering over the flank. A bowl 1 km deep at mid-flank on Mars's largest shield, made before it grew, keeps under a tenth of its depth; one beyond the foot keeps it all. The crater at the foot keeps its eastern half as an arc against the flank, the half the lava reached gone under it. Lava that reaches a depression at the edifice's foot should pond across it; the edifice's outline is still its own rule (audit M19).
- **F116 The rise's faults form as its dome rises; a dike swarm comes with the magma that feeds it.** The graben the user saw drawn over the shields were young. The rise's faults formed at the pace of the body's volcanism from the rise's start to the present (F105), so on Mars 54 percent formed in the Amazonian and 13 percent in the last 1 Gyr, after the shields had grown, and drew over them. But the rise's stress is its dome's bending and load (F105), and the dome rises between RISE_START_GYR and RISE_END_GYR (0.3 and 1.5 Gyr), its stress growing as it rises and no more once it stops. On a stagnant lid the rise's faults now form at the pace of the dome's growth, the rate of its smoothstep, and the plume head's dike swarms with them; each volcano's swarm comes as the volcano grows, at its growth's own pace (SHIELD_GROWTH_GYR); on an episodic lid both still come after the resurfacing event. Mars's graben now formed 40 percent in the Noachian and 60 percent in the Hesperian, its volcanoes' dikes on into the Amazonian; Tharsis's faulting began in the Noachian, about half its graben then, and decreased through time (Anderson et al. 2001, from 24,452 structures in five stages). The shields bury the faults older than them (F103). Mars keeps its 17,622 graben and 19 canyons, the youngest canyon now done carving 2.61 Ga (at the present before). The canyons now open in the Hesperian, and the rise's eruptions before its shields take them (F108) fill them in part: at the preview quality the deepest standing canyon stands 1.7 km below its rims (2.7 km before), where Valles Marineris stands up to 7 km deep (audit M14).
- **F117 Io's lava erupts as floods; how fast a lava pours out sets the form of its flows.** Io's flow fields shared the flow tracer the floods and the shields' flows were built from (F59, F94, F101), but not the floods' later physics: a flow ended where it would climb out of the hollow it had ponded in (IO_FLOW_FILL_DEPTH, 150 m) rather than filling it and spilling on (F107), and laid no sheet on the slopes it ran down (F109) nor lobes from each run (F110); there was no flood process on Io, its lava the paterae's flow fields drawn at the finish alone (audit M20). Io's eruptions are now floods. At each kept step of the loop the paterae alive then erupt as the old fields' generations did, the active ones now, a share of the quiet ones and every one's earlier generations, their ages log-uniform over the years the surface keeps; each from a vent on its patera's rim where the smoothed ground falls away, past the pit's wall as the loop draws it, as much lava as lays a flood's sheet over the share of its reach squared a compound field covers (half the reach squared, the reach log-uniform from 40 to 500 km). The lava runs down, fills and spills over the lows it comes to and lays its sheets as any flood does, and the paterae are cut after it: a patera is born at the step's end, and its collapse goes through the lava before it, its floor lava of its own (the lava had filled the pits first). At the finish the smoothed plains carry the floods' coarse lava just as their passes take it up, the floods are drawn as their flows over the mountains, every one of them, since a successor's lobes leave gaps a buried flood shows through, and the paterae after them; a flood's cells become Io's lava, the fresh silicate flow or a patera's floor where it ponded on one, their coverage varied by the flow units within the lobes, their age in years for the plumes' fallout to coat (F84), and a lobe's margin covers its cells by its share, since Io's colours read the coverage; a margin cell no pass reached keeps the plains. An Io flood's lobes lay their sheets wherever they go, and its lava ponds only in the closed lows of the fine ground that its lobes run into, within its cells and their ring: each a lake at one level, where it spills (a priority flood, Barnes et al. 2014), no higher than the loop's pond stood in any of its cells. The loop's ponds stood in coarse lows that the plains, smoothed over the loop's cell, hold in part or not at all; filled to their levels wherever the ground lay below them, they had stood perched on slopes and ended in steps along the coarse cells. Two rules for every other body's floods are not Io's: the finer hollows of F95, craters the loop never saw, since the gaps an earlier flood's lobes leave lie below the loop's ground too, which carried that flood's coarse lava, and had filled across its coarse footprint; and the levelling of a flood's own floor (F36), meant for the facets of a coarse base, which where the units resampled from the loop's cells said lava had cut Io's smooth slopes into terraces a coarse cell square, since withdrawn for every body (F120). How fast an eruption pours out sets its flows' form (Walker 1971: compound flows from the slow effusion of fluid lava, fewer and larger flow units from faster): at or below 300 m³/s a compound field, short broad breakouts off narrow insulated paths (the old Io fields' shares: IO_FLOW_BREAKOUT_LENGTH and _ASPECT, IO_FLOW_TRUNK_WIDTH, their wander and overlap), at or above 10,000 m³/s the floods' long sheet lobes (FLOOD_LOBE_*), between them by the logarithm (LAVA_COMPOUND_RATE, stated choices: Io's Prometheus laid thin compound pahoehoe at 49 m³/s on average, Davies et al. 2006, Amirani at 60 to 600 m³/s, Keszthelyi et al. 2001; Tharsis's long flows came at 3,000 to 35,000 m³/s, Peters et al. 2021, the Imbrium flows at 1e5 to 1e6, Wilson and Head 2024). A flood's rate is log-uniform from 1e4 to 1e6 m³/s (FLOOD_EFFUSION_RATE), so every other body's floods keep their sheets and are unchanged, Mars to the metre and the pixel; an Io eruption's is its volume over the span its field grows over (IO_FLOW_SPAN_YR, 3 to 100 years), so most of Io's are compound fields, with sheets among them where a large field grew fast. Io's flows now run down the ground and pond in its lows and in the paterae they reach; they cover 19.9 percent of the surface (16.9 before; Io's flows 28 percent, Williams et al. 2011), the plains 74.5 (77.6). Still open: the flood model lays its lava as sheets FLOOD_SHEET_THICKNESS (50 m) thick on every body, so an Io eruption's volume is its field's area at that thickness, some ten times the 1 to 10 m of Io's flows (Pillan's about 10 m), and its rate as much too high; a compound field's flow units should be thinner than a sheet flow's; one eruption's lobes keep its one age, where the old fields' breakouts each had their own (F85).
- **F118 A patera collapses piecemeal; its margin wanders; its floor lies in patches.** The user saw a patera as a perfect oval, 217 km across at 55°W, 68°N: one collapse, hardly elongated, no fault across it, drew a smooth ellipse (F88). A first try, small collapses round the rim, left a circle with bites in it. Io's paterae are piecemeal collapses, their margins scalloped, straight or irregular, few smooth (Radebaugh et al. 2001). Besides its first collapse and the chain along its axis, a patera now has two to five more collapses (IO_PATERA_PIECES) of 0.45 to 0.9 its size, set off from its centre by 0.3 to 0.7 of it in directions spread round it, so that they break out of the first and its margin is a run of arcs of different curvature meeting at corners; and its margin wanders off those arcs by 6 percent of its radius (IO_PATERA_WANDER) at every scale from a quarter of its outline down, as noise may warp an outline the collapses place. Its floor's lava lies in patches a third of its radius across (IO_FLOOR_PATCH_SHARE): a lake's crust from its last overturn up to IO_FLOW_ACTIVE_YR old (Loki's lake overturns in a wave every 540 days or so, Rathbun et al. 2002), a quiet floor's up to twice older or younger than its age, each coated by the plumes' fallout as old as it is. A lake's floor stays nearly uniformly dark, at Io's darkest reflectance of 0.05, its crusts too young for the fallout to show on; a quiet floor shows its patches. The counts and shares are stated choices.
- **F119 A rise's shields gather its dikes as its stretching wanes.** A rise's shields took its eruptions from times drawn between 1.0 and 2.5 Gyr (SHIELD_START_GYR), each by its share of their volume once started (F108); the audit's direction asked for the floods' share to fall as the lithosphere thickens, the shields starting at a threshold in its thickness (M14). Tested, no threshold in the lithosphere holds across the bodies. The Moon's maria erupted as floods through a lithosphere the model makes 210 to 320 km thick while they formed; Io erupts floods through a lithosphere its volcanism keeps thick by carrying its heat up (O'Reilly and Davies 1981); and a threshold set where Mars's volcanism turned, its thermal lithosphere about 100 km thick, would never start Venus's shields, whose lithosphere the model makes 65 to 71 km thick, with no elastic layer under its hot surface. Nor does a volcano's height follow the lithosphere's thickness or its source's depth in any simple way (Wilson, Head and Parfitt 1992), and the flood basalts' eruption rates, nearly a hundred times other basalts' (White, Crisp and Spera 2006), are long-term rates the Moon's maria fall well short of. What makes a volcano's vents central is its load: a rising dike bends toward a volcano from several of its widths away where the load's pressure outweighs the regional differential stress, a feedback between the edifice and the ground it draws dikes from (Muller, Ito and Martel 2001). While a rise's dome grows it stretches its lithosphere, its faults and dike swarms taking up the stretching (F105, F113, F116), and its dikes run along that stress to erupt as fissure floods; as the growth slows the stress falls, and the shields' loads gather the dikes. The stress follows the dome's growth rate, as its faults do (F116), and a rise's shields take the share of its eruptions by which that rate has fallen from its peak, (2x - 1)^2 with x the share of the growth gone by, past its half (a stated choice for the comparison of pressures whose magnitudes the loop does not carry): none while the growth quickens, all once it has stopped. Each shield starts when that share reaches what the larger ones hold, the largest as the growth turns, so that on Mars they start at 0.90, 1.33, 1.42, 1.46 and 1.48 Gyr, in order of size, where they started at random between 1.05 and 2.23 Gyr. The shields take 25.7 percent of Mars's eruptive budget at the preview quality (24.0 before), their share of what erupts rising from the dome's turn to 54 percent at 1.5 Gyr, where it had stood at 40 from 1.2 to 2.0 Gyr. At the standard quality Mars's 19 canyons keep a median 914 m below their rims at a quarter, a half and three quarters along them, half their carved depth, where they had kept 705 m, 38 percent; those opened after 0.4 Gyr gain the most, and 6 of the 57 points lie buried flush, against 9. Venus's rises grow long before its resurfacing, so its shields take all that erupts on them after it, as before; its floods are as many and cover as much, their margins falling a little differently. The bodies without rises are unchanged. Still open: the canyons opened early in the dome's growth fill with the floods of that growth, the faults and the floods both following it; and the shields keep their own size rule, so their edifices hold less than the magma they take.
- **F120 A rise keeps its mantle's melt clock since its lid last formed.** Every rise grew between 0.3 and 1.5 Gyr after its body formed (RISE_START_GYR and RISE_END_GYR, set for Tharsis), so Venus's three rises and nine shields grew 4.2 to 3.0 Ga, three billion years before its overturn at 0.5 Ga drowned the shields and reset their surfaces; and its rifts, drawn after the overturn by a special case, outlived the stretching that drove them (F116) by three billion years. Venus's rises, Beta, Atla and Western Eistla Regiones, and their great shields are among its youngest features: their flows lie on the regional plains, their rift zones cut those plains, and some of their volcanoes may be active now (Smrekar et al. 2010; Herrick and Hensley 2023). A rise now grows as its plume's mantle melts beneath its lid, since the lid last formed: on a stagnant lid since the body formed, on an episodic lid since its overturn, which foundered the lid and the domes on it. Its head rises once the mantle has made 2,700 m of melt per unit area since then (RISE_START_MELT) and its dome is built by 10,300 m (RISE_END_MELT); a canyon the loop carries is carved over the 6,300 m about its time (RIFT_CARVING_MELT); and a shield grows by a factor e of what it has left over 5,500 m (SHIELD_GROWTH_MELT), its time from its mantle's melt rate as it starts. These are stated choices, set on Mars's history so that Tharsis keeps the times it had: its rise from 0.3 to 1.5 Gyr, its canyons carved over the gigayear about its dome's turn, its shields growing over a gigayear as they start. The faults and swarms follow the dome's growth on every lid, the special case gone; a rise still growing has stretched by its growth so far, and one whose plume has yet to rise not at all. Mars's rise grows from 0.30 to 1.49 Gyr, and its shields start as before and grow over about 0.9 to 1.1 Gyr where they grew over 1.0. Venus's mantle makes 4.3 times Mars's melt per unit area after its overturn (its regime's efficiency, audit S1), so its rises grow from 0.40 to 0.10 Ga, and its shields start from 0.25 to 0.10 Ga and grow over 0.21 Gyr, still growing today, their flows on its plains; the floods its rises send out before their shields gather the dikes (F119) erupt on its plains too, 819 floods where 462 had come after the overturn. Venus's young floods showed two faults in how lava's composition was laid. A flood's pass gave its own composition to the cells its flows did not reach where it gave back the lava it had found, tinting its whole coarse footprint; it now gives back that lava's composition with its unit and age. And the resurfacing event laid its lava without a composition, so that it kept the coarse cells' tint of the floods after it, in blocks a loop cell square; it now takes one lava's, its time's drift, over the whole plains (taking the mantle's source field at every place, first tried, painted that field's noise across them, which the user saw; each basin's own lava is for the plains' units of audit M10). The levelling of a flood's own floor (F36), withdrawn for Io in F117, is withdrawn for every body: on Mars it cut terraces a loop cell square into a shield's flank and square pits into old craters' floors; without it Mars's heights change by 0.8 m on average and by more than 10 m over 1.2 percent of the surface, with no strips left dry along the loop's cells.
- **F121 A rocky mantle stores its heat under a lid that can break.** The thermal history's mantle had no heat of its own: its heat flux was its radioactive heat times a factor for secular cooling by its size (F12, F19), so that every body cooled steadily, and a body's regime was named by cuts on its present heat flux and radius (audit S1), Venus episodic at 40 mW/m² and 5,000 km, its overturn set at its crater retention age, 0.5 Gyr ago. A rocky mantle now stores its heat (mantle.rs). Its temperature rises with its radioactive and tidal heat and falls with what it convects into its lid's base; its heat capacity is the rock's and the core's (Stevenson, Spohn and Schubert 1983) less the lid's, which grows as the mantle cools. The convected flux follows the boundary-layer scaling of stagnant-lid convection (Solomatov and Moresi 2000's form, its prefactor 0.5, within the 0.37 to 0.65 of published fits), the viscosity 5e19 Pa s at 1,600 K and Arrhenian with dry olivine's 300 kJ/mol in diffusion creep (Karato and Wu 1993); the surface loses that flux and the heat the lid gives up as it thickens. The lid breaks when the stress the convecting sublayer puts on it, along a cell as long as the mantle is deep, exceeds its friction over its thickness, after Moresi and Solomatov (1998), who found that a lid mobilizes only with a friction well below the laboratory's: it founders into the mantle, which takes up its cold, and a new lid grows as a cooling half space, the heat it loses over each step averaged exactly, until it is as thick as a stagnant lid and can break again (Turcotte 1993: the interior reheats under a thickening lid until the lid fails). Its effective friction, 0.010, is a stated choice set so that Venus last overturned within the half gigayear its craters record. A dry rocky lid that broke within the last half of its body's life is episodic, its retention age and its resurfacing age the time since it last broke; the plates' rule (surface water, 50 mW/m², 4,000 km) and the Io regime stay as they were. Venus's lid breaks 21 times, every 0.1 Gyr or so while its mantle is young and 0.36, 0.47 and 0.68 Gyr apart at the end, last 0.61 Ga, its mantle held near 1,750 K throughout; its heat flow is 27 mW/m² now, where the equilibrium gave 69, and surges after each overturn; its tessera keep the surface the overturn before the last laid, 1.29 Ga. Mars's, the Moon's and Mercury's lids never break, their mantles cooling under them to 22, 10 and 15 mW/m², and they keep their equilibrium histories for now (below). A mantle's plumes each carry a share of its heat, so their number now grows with the heat it loses in all rather than per square metre (PLUME_POWER_PER_PLUME, 4 TW, a stated choice keeping Mars's one and Venus's three, which a count per square metre would have cut to one at Venus's own heat flow). On the melt clock (F120) Venus's rises now start 51 Myr after its overturn, carried by its surge, and are nine tenths grown today; the largest shield on each started 0.21 Ga and grows over 0.6 Gyr, a third grown, the smaller ones to start within 0.15 Gyr. Still open: the stagnant bodies keep the equilibrium history, since the stored heat gives Mars some 40 mW/m² early where the equilibrium gives 140, which would move everything built on it, and the Moon's rock, which the composition model sets at 62 percent of its mass beside a 30 percent core and 8 percent ice (audit S2), leaves it 10 mW/m² only with its lid's own cooling; the plates' rule and the Io regime are not yet the mantle's; and the last overturn's time hangs on the friction, a whole cycle more or less coming with a small change (0.009 gives 0.41 Ga, 0.0095 0.87, 0.010 0.61).
- **F122 A body formed too warm for ice carries none, and every dry rocky mantle keeps its own heat.** The composition model (F8) took a density below the chondritic mix of iron and rock for ice, wherever the body formed: the Moon came out 30 percent iron core and 8 percent ice beside 62 percent rock, and Mars 4 percent ice, though both formed far inside the snow line, where water ice could not condense (audit S2). A body now takes up ice by where it formed: fully at or below the snow line's temperature, 170 K (Hayashi 1981, at 2.7 AU), not at all from 200 K, between them by its temperature (SNOW_LINE_WET_K, SNOW_LINE_DRY_K, the second a stated choice), its blackbody temperature at its distance from its star today standing for its birthplace's; where it took up none, its density's deficit below the chondritic mix is iron it lacks. The Moon is now all rock with no core, where its measured core is one or two percent of its mass (Weber et al. 2011); Mars's core is 16 percent of its mass, where InSight measured about a quarter, a core made lighter than the model's pure iron by its sulfur (Stähler et al. 2021); Ceres, at 167 K, and the giant planets' moons are as they were. With its rock the Moon's mantle makes its measured heat flow, and every dry rocky mantle outside the plates' rule and the Io regime now keeps its own heat's history (F121): Mars, the Moon and Mercury, whose lids never break, as well as Venus. Moving them showed three faults in how F121 kept its lid. The lid took a stagnant lid's thickness at every step, so that when a heating mantle thinned it, the heat its base took up was drawn from the surface, and Mars's and the Moon's surfaces gained heat in their first hundred million years. A young lid lost a half space's heat from the mantle beside its own cold, nearly twice what a half space loses. And a foundered lid's cold went to the mantle without its own rock, which joins it. The lid now grows where it conducts more than the mantle convects into its base and thins where it conducts less, by the heat its base takes up or gives (a Stefan condition at its base, as in the parameterized histories of stagnant-lid planets, Spohn 1991): grown from nothing it cools as a half space, and grown old it follows a stagnant lid's thickness. The surface loses what the body's energy says it does, a foundered lid's cold is shared by the whole mantle, and a body's first lid grows from its surface as it forms, as after an overturn. A lid may break once a half space would have grown as thick as a stagnant lid since it formed, as in F121: under a cooling mantle the lid itself only nears that thickness. Mars's heat flow is 76 mW/m² at 50 Myr, 42 at 0.5 Gyr while its mantle heats under its lid, 51 at 1.5 Gyr and 27 now, where the equilibrium gave 176, 138, 87 and 37; the Moon's is 71 at 50 Myr and 15 now, Mercury's 65 and 15. Venus's lid breaks 42 times, every 0.1 to 0.2 Gyr while its mantle is hot enough to break it, last 0.49 Ga and the time before 0.69 Ga; its effective friction is 0.014 (LID_FRICTION, a stated choice: any from 0.0138 to 0.0148 gives the same last overturn, where 0.010 had sat a small change from another cycle), its heat flow 27 mW/m² now. A plume carries a share of the heat its mantle loses while it rises, so their count follows the mantle's mean heat loss over its life rather than today's, long after its plumes rose: Mars's 6.1 TW keeps its one, Venus's 35 TW its three. The melt clock's constants (F120) are set again on Mars's new history so that Tharsis keeps its times: its rise from 1,400 to 5,400 m of melt per unit area, 0.30 to 1.50 Gyr; its canyons carved over 3,300 m; its shields growing by a factor e of what they have left over 3,500 m, about a gigayear. Still open: the thermal state's mantle temperature and lithosphere still come from the heat flux, by the provisional 1,400 + 200 (q / q_Earth)^0.5 K and by F14, not from the mantle's own temperature and lid; the plates and the Io regime keep the equilibrium history; Io, inside the snow line of the warm disc it formed in around Jupiter but outside the Sun's, keeps 6 percent ice; and on their own heat the Moon's mantle, keeping all its rock's heat, erupts until 1 Ga and lays lava over 21 percent of its surface, while Mercury's, with about the Moon's heat per unit area, floods 19 percent of its surface before its contraction closes the vents, where the real figures are 17 and 27 percent: a share of the heat-producing elements in the crust, whose heat goes straight to the surface, and the core's own heat from its formation, which heats a thin mantle from below, are the next step.
- **F123 A crust holds a share of the heat-producing elements, and a mantle melts only above its solidus.** On its own heat (F122) the Moon's mantle kept every radioactive element of its rock, ran some 200 K hotter than parameterized histories that put a share of them in the crust, and erupted until 1 Ga: a third of its lava after 2.5 Ga, young flows whose composition drifted lighter, laid in patches over the great basins' older floods. Two things were missing. The melts that made a crust took the incompatible elements of the mantle they came from, so a crust holds a share of the heat-producing elements by its volume's share of the rock weighted by their enrichment, about one over the melt fraction (CRUST_ENRICHMENT 20, a stated choice for melts of some 5 percent): the Moon's 40 km crust holds 59 percent, Mars's 44 against the half its thorium implies (Taylor and McLennan 2009), Mercury's 65 and Venus's 34; the crust's heat leaves through the surface, and the mantle heats with the rest. And a mantle melts only while it stands above the rock's solidus at the base of its stiff lid, where the mantle rising under the lid stops, 2.24 rheological temperature scales below the mantle's temperature (Davaille and Jaupart 1993), the dry peridotite solidus Hirschmann's (2000): the share of the heat flux that goes into melt (F17) is now the mantle's own part, without the crust's radioactive heat, and it melts as far as it stands above that solidus over one rheological scale, the plumes of a body that has them rising 200 K hotter than the mantle about them (PLUME_EXCESS_K, a stated choice within the 100 to 300 K of Earth's hotspots, Herzberg et al. 2007), so that they go on melting where it no longer does. The Moon's mantle now peaks at 1,758 K where it reached 1,886, and falls to its solidus by about 1 Ga: it erupts 453 m of lava per unit area where it erupted 715, 68 percent of it by 1.5 Gyr after it formed and 81 percent by 2.0 Gyr, and none for the last billion years; its heat flow is 14.8 mW/m² now. Mars's and Venus's plumes go on melting today. With less heat in their mantles, Venus's lid breaks 25 times, every 0.3 Gyr at the end, last 0.64 Ga and the time before 0.94 Ga; its effective friction is 0.0105 (LID_FRICTION, set again so that the last overturn falls within its craters' age: any from 0.0101 to 0.0108 gives the same, where below 0.0101 the lid breaks whenever it has grown back and last 0.30 Ga). The melt clock's constants (F120) are set again on Mars's history by the same rules, so that Tharsis keeps its times: its rise from 1,360 to 4,100 m of melt per unit area, its canyons carved over 2,250 m, its shields growing by a factor e over 2,200 m. Still open: Mercury's mantle, with no plumes, its crust holding two thirds of its heat-producing elements and nothing heating it from its core, erupts 235 m where it erupted 291 and the equilibrium history 447, so that its smooth plains shrink further: the core's own heat from its formation, which needs a mantle rheology under pressure for the large bodies, is the next step; the melt's share of the heat flux is still by the regime's name, and the crust's thickness still by class.
- **F124 A core keeps its own heat.** The mantle's core had moved with it as one temperature, adding its heat capacity to the mantle's (F121): as the mantle warmed under its lid the core took up half the heat, and nothing heated a thin mantle from below, so that Mercury, whose core is three fifths of its mass, made about the Moon's melt per unit area and its smooth plains covered 16 percent of its surface (F123) against the 27 they cover (Denevi et al. 2013). The core now keeps its own temperature. Its formation leaves it hotter than the mantle's adiabat at their boundary by 300 K (CORE_SUPERHEAT_K, a stated choice: core formation releases enough energy to warm Mercury by some 480 K and Mars by 280 K were it all kept, after Solomon 1979's energetics), and it loses heat into the mantle through the boundary layer at their interface, as in the parameterized histories of Stevenson, Spohn and Schubert (1983): the layer grows until its own Rayleigh number reaches 1,000 and breaks away (CORE_BOUNDARY_RAYLEIGH, Howard 1966), its viscosity the mantle's at its mean temperature, and it carries heat only while the core is hotter than the mantle's adiabat there. A foundered lid's cold now goes to the mantle alone, the core answering through its boundary. Mercury's core starts at 2,155 K and cools to 1,756 K, giving its mantle 25 mW/m² at 50 Myr, 10 at 0.5 Gyr and 2.6 now; the mantle peaks at 1,791 K where it reached 1,750, and erupts 281 m of lava per unit area where it erupted 235 (447 on the equilibrium history of F121); its mantle melts until about 3 Gyr, but its contraction closes its vents by 1.5 Gyr (F16, Byrne et al. 2016). Venus's core passes its superheat to the mantle within 0.25 Gyr; Mars's gives it little; the Moon has none. Venus's lid now breaks 30 times, last 0.66 Ga and the time before 0.93 Ga, at an effective friction of 0.0112 (LID_FRICTION, set again: any from 0.0109 to 0.0115 gives the same, while from 0.0101 to 0.0109 the last falls at 0.35 Ga); the melt clock's constants are set again on Mars's history by the same rules (1,380, 4,280, 2,400 and 2,350 m). Still open: Mercury's smooth plains cover 18.5 percent where they cover 27. A superheat of some 850 K would give them back, but only if core formation's energy had all stayed in Mercury's core, so the rest is more likely the crust's buoyancy: the Moon's anorthositic crust, lighter than its basalt, holds most of its magma down (Head and Wilson 1992), while Mercury's reduced, iron-poor magma ocean floated no such crust (Vander Kaaden and McCubbin 2015); the model gives every body one extrusive share, and the crust's composition is audit S7's. The viscosity at the core's boundary takes no account of pressure, so the large cores give up their superheat within a few hundred million years; the inner core's growth and its latent heat, and contraction from the cooling itself rather than F16, are not yet the model's.
- **F125 A rocky crust's materials come from the processes that make it.** The bedrock, the lava and the deep bedrock were chosen by composition class and regime (audit S7): Mercury's surface was "Mercurian regolith", its lava "smooth plains" and its deep rock "low-reflectance material", the Moon's lava "mare basalt", Io's bedrock "sulfur coated", names of bodies and units in one list with rocks. The list now holds materials, and a rocky body's are chosen by what made them. Its mantle keeps iron in its silicates by the warmth where it formed, linear in its blackbody temperature from 2 percent FeO for a body formed as hot as Mercury, whose surface carries some 1.5 percent iron, much of it in sulfides (Weider et al. 2014), to 18 percent for one as cool as Mars (Taylor and McLennan 2009) (body.mantle_feo; FEO_REDUCED, FEO_OXIDIZED, a stated relation: it gives the Moon 14 percent, Venus 11, and Earth 14 where its mantle, its iron drawn into its core under pressure, keeps 8). Its magma ocean floated plagioclase where its last liquid, the 22 percent left when plagioclase joins the cumulates (Snyder et al. 1992), lay within the plagioclase field, above about 1 GPa, from half to all of it, if the liquid was iron-rich enough to be denser than plagioclase, above 4 to 8 percent FeO: an anorthositic crust over a noritic lower crust; an iron-poor ocean floated graphite instead (Vander Kaaden and McCubbin 2015), a graphite-rich crust under lavas that carry its graphite; otherwise the crust is the body's basalt. Its basalt carries its mantle's iron, from the iron-poor end-member, bright and reddish (Mercury's plains, Denevi et al. 2013), at 3 percent FeO to the iron-rich, dark (the lunar maria), at 13. Where impacts garden no fine regolith, under an atmosphere or on a surface its plumes renew faster, a material shows its rock, darker than its fresh regolith. Plates with water still make a felsic crust and a tidally heated body's plumes a sulfur deposit. The crust's buoyancy now decides how much of the magma erupts: magma rising from the stiff lid's base gains buoyancy through the mantle part of its path and loses it through a crust lighter than itself, and erupts by what the loss leaves of the gain, the magma's density by its iron (2,670 + 1,650 FeO kg/m³, a stated fit through some 2,700 for an iron-poor lava and 3,000 for an iron-rich one) and the crust's by what it is (2,750 for anorthosite, 2,850 + 1,000 FeO for a basaltic or graphite-bearing crust, 2,700 felsic). The Moon's dense basalt, under its light crust, is held down while its lid is thin and less as the lid thickens, so its eruptions come later; Mercury's light lava and Mars's rise through their crusts. A rocky body's extrusive share is 0.13 where its crust does not hold its magma down (VOLCANISM_EXTRUSIVE_FRACTION_ROCK, a stated choice within Crisp 1984's 5:1 to 10:1, set so that the Moon keeps the lava its maria hold); cryolava keeps 0.10. Retired names in saved projects read as their replacements. Still open: the icy bodies' materials and the three presets that pin theirs, the next milestone; the crust's porosity, which would make every crust lighter near the surface; the crust's thickness, still by class; and a body's carbon, which the graphite crust takes for granted.
- **F126 An icy crust's materials come from the processes that make it.** Three presets pinned their materials (audit S7): Callisto a bedrock of dirty ice under a regolith three quarters carbonaceous, Ceres a "carbonaceous ice" under one four fifths carbonaceous, Ganymede a regolith seven tenths dirty ice; the defaults gave a rock-ice body clean ice if it had an ocean and no air and dirty ice otherwise, and a regolith of its bedrock alone. Now an icy crust is the rock and ice the body accreted: its dirty ice carries the share of the crust's volume that is not ice, the body's rock and metal against its ice from its own composition and the densities that give it (body.crust_impurity: Callisto 0.26, Ganymede 0.29, Ceres 0.38), and its colour is the intimate mixture of clean ice and carbonaceous rock at that share, Hapke's mixing of their single-scattering albedos (Hapke 1981; a stated choice of one grain size for both, so that the shares of their cross-sections are those of their volumes), which reproduces the library's dirty ice of 30 percent rock (0.36) and Ceres's carbonaceous ice at 75 (0.16). A shell that renews itself everywhere shows the ocean's clean ice (Europa), and the lanes and polar terrain a thick shell renews stay clean. The lag is the crust's rock, left as its ice sublimes, and the regolith carries it: impact ejecta spread what the warm latitudes grow over the old crust, so the regolith is the crust and its lag by the share of the surface whose ice sublimes into a lag over the surface's age, each latitude at the temperature its own reflectance gives it, settled from the fresh crust, so that a dark lag warms its latitude and grows faster (Spencer 1987's thermal segregation): 56 percent of Callisto's regolith, 60 of Ganymede's old crust's, 98 of Ceres's, none at Saturn and beyond, and none under an atmosphere. A warm crust loses its ice deep: where its lag runs deeper than the half-kilometre layer craters and slopes expose, what they bring up is lag, and the dirty ice shows the lag's colour fresh by the share of that layer the lag has taken (materials.lag_depth_share: 0.92 on Ceres, whose lag the law takes kilometres down at its equator, so that its fresh craters and walls are dark and the body one grey; 0.02 on Callisto, whose lag is tens of metres). Impacts also trade regolith between terrains (REGOLITH_EXCHANGE, 0.3 of the other terrain's regolith by its share of the body, a stated choice from the lunar maria, whose soils hold highland material carried in by impacts, some 0.1 on average in their top metres and more near their shores (Liu et al. 2021), over a fifth in the Apollo 11 and 12 soils (Huang et al. 2017), and which it gives 0.25): Ganymede's old crust takes 21 percent of its renewed ice and its lanes 9 percent of the old crust's regolith, so its dark terrain is 0.30 at low latitudes and its lanes 0.56, against the real 0.29 and about 0.5; lava contacts mix by F95 instead, and the basalts' colours are measured soils that hold their share. A cell's lag grows from the crust's rock rather than the regolith's, at the temperature of its own reflectance, the body's Bond albedo no longer scaling it, and the body's Bond albedo counts the share its shell renewed as clean ice with what impacts brought it: Callisto 0.21, Ganymede 0.52, Ceres 0.090, Europa 0.66 as before. Cryolava freezes to its liquid's ice, the rock having settled out of it, and the ice that flows when a crater relaxes is the shell under the half-kilometre crust, the frozen ocean under a shell that floats on one, so Callisto's largest craters relax somewhat more. Callisto, Ceres and Ganymede pin nothing now. Triton and Pluto show the rock their crusts accreted, 0.32 and 0.36 against the measured 0.76 and 0.5: their brightness is nitrogen and methane frost, a climate process (Phase 5), and Triton's surface is young from heating the model does not have; Triton's two validation checks, its Bond albedo and its temperature, are set aside with Pluto's. Retired names (carbonaceous_ice, rock_ice_mixture) read as the body's dirty ice. Still open: the frosts of nitrogen, methane and carbon monoxide; Ganymede's lanes, clean ice a little brighter than the real ones, and its polar caps, which the magnetosphere's sputtering brightens; dust from outside, which would dirty an old surface by its age; and the central pits, which could now follow the crust's rock (S9).
- **F127 Rays end a quarter of the way round, the lag hides a crater's floor, and deposits are drawn by share.** Rays were drawn on flat ground with Elliott et al.'s lunar ray lengths, measured on craters up to 85 km, carried to any size, so a young basin's rays wrapped the body and met at its antipode: the Moon's at seed 75, a 378 km basin 246 Myr old at 62 degrees south, and Ceres's, a 352 km crater 432 Myr old. A fragment launched at 45 degrees lands an angle theta round a sphere at the speed whose square over g R is k = 2 tan(theta/2) / (1 + tan(theta/2)), the circular orbital speed a quarter of the way round and the escape speed at the antipode; a spread of launch angles of plus or minus 10 degrees lands a jet's fragments within some tenth of their range near the crater, over a third at k = 0.8 and from 70 to 110 degrees round at k = 1, so they lay no ray. A ray fades from k = 0.75 (RAY_COHERENT_K, a stated choice, about 60 degrees round, beyond the Moon's longest rays, Tycho's 1,500 to 2,000 km) and ends a quarter of the way round. That trimmed the rays' ends but not their cause, which the user saw in a globe view: the fit carried to a 378 km basin gave it a median ray of 5,100 km and, at the same distance in crater radii, four times the share of excavated ground Tycho's rays have, so every jet saturated into a solid wedge. Beyond the largest crater the fit was measured on (Tycho, RAY_LENGTH_MAX_RADIUS_KM 42.5 km) the rays now scale with the crater: a large crater's ejecta are self-similar (the gravity regime), its fragments and secondaries as much larger as its ejecta are thicker, so its secondaries cover the same share of the ground at the same distance in crater radii. Inside its rim a young crater was fully fresh, and its continuous blanket bright, however thick the lag over them, where its rays already faded by the lag's skin (F91): on Ceres, whose lag regrows in centuries, the young crater's floor stood fresh while its melt kept the lag's maturity, and the floor showed the melt as dark blobs on lighter ground. The floor and the blanket now fade under the lag as the rays do. Cryolava's ice sublimes into the crust's lag and is gardened into its regolith, as deep ice and melt are, bright fresh and maturing to the ground; it had kept clean ice's colour at maturity, white floods on Ceres's floors since F126.
