The cover a salted unit or a crater floor holds is its salts, no longer coloured or mixed sideways as lava. A deposit a field bounds is drawn by the share of each cell its margin covers (fields::level_share: at the cell's scale the margin is a straight edge, from the field's value and gradient at the centre), not where the field passes its level in each cell, which drew Ceres's faculae as staircases of whole cells; the deposits still drawn by whole cells are listed in the audit (W8).
- **F128 A ray is the clusters of its secondaries.** Rays were drawn as smooth bands, the share of the ground the jets' fragments excavate, saturated wherever they excavated it more than once: at Tycho's scale streaks, at a young basin's solid wedges, which the user found in a globe view of the Moon at seed 75. A ray is now the clusters its fragments' secondaries make. A cluster is a chain of secondaries RAY_CLUSTER_WIDTH of the largest there wide and RAY_CLUSTER_ASPECT times as long along the radial, opening downrange, the herringbone whose V points back at the crater (Oberbeck and Morrison 1973); the largest secondary is Croft (1992)'s 0.07 D^0.87 km for a crater D across, one fit for the Moon, Mercury, Mars and Ganymede, placed at the continuous blanket's edge where the largest lie (a stated choice), and shrinking outward as the distance to -1/2 (SECONDARY_SIZE_FALL; Singer et al. 2020 find the largest fragment falling with the ejection speed to a power between -0.3 and -3, here the speed to -1, a stated middle). The clusters fall at random in proportion to the area the fragments excavate, up to RAY_CLUSTER_CAP per area, each laying the area of its footprint on the cells it covers, so that on average they excavate what the fragments do and Elliott et al.'s ray lengths hold; where the jets excavate more, the rest of the ray is solid, as a fresh crater's near rays are, and far out a ray breaks into its clusters with ground between them. Each secondary the finish stamps is a cluster of its own, its fresh ground where it lies. The quarter-round limit (F127) now thins the fragments that land rather than dimming the ray, so a ray breaks into its clusters as it ends. Beyond the largest crater Elliott et al. measured, a crater's rays are shorter in its own radii the larger it is, in place of the self-similar step: its largest fragments fall off ever more steeply with their speed (Singer et al. 2020, from about -0.3 for kilometre craters to -3 for basins), so a basin's far-flung ejecta are too fine to make secondaries that show; the median ray grows as the radius to 0.4 (RAY_LENGTH_LARGE_EXPONENT), a fit from Tycho's 773 km to Orientale's secondary chains, lines of 10 to 30 km craters reaching at least 2,000 km from its centre to the northwest (Wilhelms; the extent a stated reading). The seed's basin, 378 km across, now has a median ray of about 1,400 km, seven of its radii, where self-similarity gave 3,400. Still open: Mars's rays do not show, since above 100 Pa nothing weathers its ground and a ray has no mature ground to freshen, much as real Martian rays barely show in visible light (Tornabene et al. 2006 found seven rayed craters, by their thermal contrast), yet under 1 kPa its rays are drawn for an airless body's 1.5 Gyr: two thresholds that disagree, both the climate's (Phase 5); the stamped secondaries are only a loop crater's near ones, at 2.5 to 8 radii; and the rays take 23 s on the Moon where they took 9.
- **F129 One population of secondaries.** The finish stamped secondaries by a rule of their own (F91): 400 for every 100 km of the primary's diameter, 0.5 to 4 percent of its size whatever their distance, in groups of eight from 2.5 to 8 of its radii, apart from the rays' clusters (F128), so that most bright clusters had no crater under them and most secondaries no ray over them. A crater's secondaries now follow the most complete census there is, Orientale's: 2,728 secondaries of 2 km and more mapped from its rim to six of its radii, the largest 27 km, their cumulative counts a Weibull of exponent 1.32 (Guo et al. 2018), that largest being Croft (1992)'s for Orientale's 930 km. The census is carried to every crater in units of its own largest secondary, a stated assumption of self-similarity; below the census's floor the count steepens to the slope of Tycho's secondaries, 3.3 to 4.0 (Hirata and Nakamura 2006), here 3.65. Each secondary the grid can show is sized from the count and lands in one of the clusters its crater's rays make beyond the continuous blanket, among those that hold secondaries its size, chosen by the ground they excavate, and lies in that cluster's footprint: the larger lie nearer, the rest along the jets, and a cluster that takes several holds them as a group; it strikes low along its path, and while its crater's rays last its fresh ground shows in them. Choosing the clusters by the ground their fragments excavate before the cap put nearly every secondary in the curtain's strongest jet, whose mass is lognormal with a spread of 2.5: the secondaries follow the clusters the rays show. The craters the finish samples throw secondaries as the loop's do. At 8192 pixels the Moon's 378 km basin at seed 75 throws some 1,200 of 2.7 km and more, where the old rule gave it some 500, most of them 3 to 5 km, so its clusters stay soft patches of ground dug by secondaries finer than the grid. The count corrects F128's account, which read Croft's largest secondary as the largest in each cluster: it is the largest a crater throws, and while the clusters' footprints are still sized from it (a stated choice), most of the secondaries in them are smaller. A secondary now lands only on ground older than it, as the population's craters do: where lava, ice from below or a younger crater's floor or blanket renewed the ground since, it is gone with its parent, where before only the renewed ice of F9's lanes took it (at seed 75 the Moon's young basin carried older basins' secondaries on its fresh blanket). The census is of a basin 3.8 billion years old, so at its small end it is a floor on what the basin threw. The six constants of the old rule go; `cratering.secondary_fraction` scales the count from the census's 0.2.
- **F130 A cluster's grain.** A cluster was laid as one Gaussian over its footprint, a soft blur however many cells it covered: a fleck at Tycho's scale, at a basin's a smudge tens of cells long, which the user asked about at seed 75. A cluster is RAY_CLUSTER_WIDTH of its largest secondaries across, and each of those lies among smaller ones by the same rule at its own scale: its ground is now laid as clusters a RAY_CLUSTER_WIDTH-th as wide and RAY_CLUSTER_ASPECT times as long, as many as its footprint holds, scattered over it as the cluster lays its ground and sharing that ground by their footprints, each split the same way down to the narrowest cluster drawn (RAY_CLUSTER_DRAW_CELLS). A cluster keeps its whole ground and its place, and its inside takes the grain of its secondaries: flakes and streaks of bright ground with darker ground between, finer the finer the grid. A cluster within a few cells of the grid is laid as before, so Tycho's rays are as they were, and the secondaries the finish stamps keep their own ground whole. The rule adds no constants. A crater's clusters are laid in parallel where its rays cover much of the body, which halved the rays' time on the Moon, to some 11 s.
- **F131 Deposits drawn by share (audit W8).** The deposits a field bounds that were still laid by whole cells now cover each cell by the share their margin covers (`fields::level_share`), as the faculae have since F127: Venus's tessera against the overturn's plains, a corona's interior lava (laid by the loop on whole coarse cells, now redrawn by the finish), a pancake dome's lava, a shield's flows, summit pond and edifice, a flood's lobes and outline where they hold less than the whole cell, Io's patera floors and Ceres's cryodomes. A cell takes a deposit's time and unit where the deposit covers more than half of it and keeps its own otherwise, its cover, relief and the substrate's deep material and melt blended by the share. The albedo reads a cell's cover by its unit, lava on most and salts on a dome, a crack or a crater floor, so a deposit whose cover is read so, a patera's lava or a dome's salts, takes its unit and time wherever it lies, its cover the share, as a flow's margin on Io does; without it a dome's edge cells kept their old unit and their salts did not show. The staircases the maps showed, Io's dark patera floors and Ceres's bright domes, are smooth; Venus's tessera and plains differ too little in colour, and the Moon's and Mars's flow margins are blurred by gardening, for theirs to have shown, and their maps changed by at most 2 percent. Enceladus's polar terrain was already drawn by share in the finish (`take_polar`), as Europa's chaos and bands and Ganymede's lanes are. A partial lava margin over a crater floor keeps the floor's unit, whose cover is read as salts, as before.
- **F132 A groove is a chain of fault segments.** A lane's grooves were a wave across it, each groove a line the lane's whole length, gated by noise that seldom broke it: texture at Ganymede's scale, but at Enceladus's, where the user looked, straight lines running the lane's length and stopping on its edges. A groove is now a chain of fault segments. Each slot of GROOVE_SEGMENT_SPACINGS along it holds a segment with GROOVE_SEGMENT_PRESENCE, half to one and a half slots long (GROOVE_SEGMENT_LENGTH), its throw falling to its tips as one less the square of the distance along it, and its line stepped across the groove's by up to GROOVE_STEP of a spacing, so that where one segment ends the next takes up beside it, after a gap or alongside it in a relay: the en echelon steps that oblique extension makes, which Uruk Sulcus's youngest grooves show (Pappalardo et al. 1998, a right-lateral transtension). Each segment cuts its own trough, a spacing across, into a plateau at the mean of what the troughs take (GROOVE_DEPTH_MEAN, the mean of the faults' depths), so that the lane keeps its level, two segments in a relay both show, and no seam marks where one gives way to the next. The grooves die out over GROOVE_MARGIN_SPACINGS in from the bounding faults and any fault across an end, so that they end in their own tips rather than on a line. A later pulse lays its troughs over the earlier set's where its own segments stand. Stated choices.
- **F133 Coronae over an ice mantle's upwellings.** Miranda had no preset, and the user asked whether its coronae, named as Venus's are, should come of Ganymede's lanes. They come of upwellings. A tidal heating episode switches convection on in an ice mantle on rock under its lid, and the upwellings of the new convection lift and stretch the lid (Hammond and Barr 2014). The episode is the tidal flux above the floor that renews an icy surface, as the lanes' is (F44); a lid over steady or waning convection, as Pluto's and Ceres's over their radiogenic heat, stays stagnant, so no corona marks it. The layer convects under the lid at the episode's thinnest, from the lid's base to the rock; a body with an ocean has none, its shell being the lid. Its cells are as wide in each limb as the layer is deep (CORONA_CELL_ASPECT), so that upwellings stand twice the layer's depth apart at its mid-depth, the spacing widening to the lid's base as the radius does, and as many as hexagonal cells of that spacing fit on the sphere there: `volcanism.corona_count`, five on Miranda, whose ice 145 km deep under a 14 km lid gives cells about 370 km apart (Hammond and Barr's models give the few plumes its coronae need, low orders standing where the core is under half the radius). The lid takes an upwelling's stretching in faults of its own where it bends over a shorter length than the upwelling is wide, its flexural parameter against the distance from the upwelling to its cell's edge, the share falling as one over one plus their ratio to the fourth power (CORONA_FLEXURE_POWER); times the episode's drive it is the coronae's `volcanism.corona_strength`, 0.99 on Miranda, and no corona is drawn under CORONA_STRENGTH_FLOOR. The coronae take their time from the episode as the lanes do (`volcanism.corona_age`, `volcanism.corona_duration`), each its own within it, and the lid of that time, from the thermal relations at that time rather than the history's samples, spaces its faults (GROOVE_NECKING_WAVELENGTH, GROOVE_RIFT_LID_SHARE, F61) and sets its strength. The upwellings are placed each the farthest from those placed of CORONA_PLACEMENT_TRIES tries. Each cell is bounded by the bisectors with its neighbours, a polygon, its corners rounded (CORONA_CORNER_SHARPNESS) and its outline wandering (CORONA_OUTLINE_WANDER over CORONA_OUTLINE_WANDER_SCALE), as Miranda's coronae are "polygonal to ovoidal". The corona is the part of the cell where the flow under the lid stretches it in area, out to where it turns to shortening it: for a flow that rises at the centre and sinks at the edge with a speed that rises and falls as half a sine across the cell, CORONA_OUTLINE_SHARE, 0.646 of the way. Its outer belt, CORONA_BELT_SHARE of its radius (Elsinore's 80 km), is a ring of the grooves' fault segments (F132) concentric with its outline, closing round it without a seam; its core is crossed by two sets at CORONA_CROSS_ANGLE, their segments less often (CORONA_CORE_LEVEL), turning across it by up to CORONA_CORE_TURN over CORONA_CORE_TURN_SCALE of its radius, where the lid stretched every way over the upwelling broke in more than one direction, the two changing over CORONA_CORE_BLEND. The lid is thinnest over the upwelling, the heat the flow brings up CORONA_FLUX_CONTRAST above the mean at the centre and as much below at the edge, so that the faults' spacing, as the lid's thickness, widens outward, as Inverness's does. The upwelling stretches the lid for as long as the episode lasts, far longer than its faults take to rotate, so its blocks tilt until their faults lock: from CORONA_FAULT_DIP_DEG, the dip Arden's faults broke at (Pappalardo et al. 1997), to CORONA_LOCKUP_DIP_DEG, where friction of about 0.6 locks a normal fault, new faults then taking the strain at the same tilt. A block tilted 20 degrees stands its width times sin 20 degrees from crest to trough, the middling fault's trough below its plateau: 1.7 km on Miranda's 4.9 km blocks against Arden's 2 km, where the lanes' share of their spacing, calibrated on Ganymede's grooves, gives a few hundred metres. Each trough is a tilt block's, its scarp facing outward and its back slope long (GROOVE_FINE_SKEW), as Arden's scarps face outward. Its bounding faults face outward too, their footwall, the corona's side, up and the ground beyond down, so that the belt stands above the old ground; beyond the outline the belt's faults run on as open fractures. The corona renews the ground inside its outline as a lane does (F60, F61), by its cover, its strength, on the loop's grid at its time and on the finish's, with its own unit (UNIT_CORONA), its ice clean and its troughs darker; it stretches and breaks the old ground across it, and keeps of the old relief only what is longer than CORONA_SMOOTH_SHARE of its radius. Stated choices, but for the outline's share, which is the flow's, and the dips, which are measured.

Ice melts only where the profile reaches its melting point inside the body: the excess the profile would reach at the centre over the melting point, (T_m - T_s)(R / L - 1), is taken against one rheological temperature scale of ice, R T_m² / E, as the rocky mantle's melt is against its own (F123), in the present rate and through the history. Miranda makes none today and its domes go; Ceres's and Pluto's profiles reach the melting point at depth, and theirs stand.

The ocean worlds' magma rates fall with it (Europa's from 0.51 to 0.0012 km³/yr), which nothing they make reads: their shells do not flood.

The bands are now kept from step to step (`relaxation::Bands`): each step splits only what changed since the last, adds it to the bands the relief already holds, and scales those, so that every part of the relief decays in the bands it had when it came, by the product of their survivals. - **F135 The bombardment of any star.** The user saw Enceladus and Miranda carry Mimas's crater population, Herschel-like basins and all, where their maps show fewer and smaller craters, and asked for a bombardment that would serve a planet close to a red dwarf as well as the Solar System, fitted to the Solar System's records. A body's place against its star's snow line, x, its distance over the distance where a blackbody is 170 K (2.69 AU for the Sun, found from the insolation, so for any star), now sets which population strikes it, how hard and how fast (`cratering.snow_line_position`). The inner system's rocky leftovers keep the Neukum production function and chronology, at the Moon's rate per unit area inside x = 0.40 and twice it from 0.57 through the belt (Mars, Ivanov 2001), fading out from 1.2 to 1.6, beyond which comets strike the giant planets' moons (Zahnle et al. 2003); the Mars production function takes over from 0.447 (1.2 AU). The outer system's planetesimals, scattered from beyond the giant planets, strike from the snow line out (their share ramping in from x = 1 to 1.5): their numbers fall with size as a cumulative power law of slope 2.1 below 100 km and 4.5 above (Nesvorný et al. 2023; Wong et al. 2023), and each makes a crater by the scaling for icy satellites, D_i = 0.143 D^1.09 g^0.28 (ρ_i/ρ_t)^−0.43 v^−0.56 D_sc^0.19 in km, m/s² and km/s (Zahnle et al. 2003, as Wong et al. 2023 use it): a comet of 1 km makes a crater of 23 km on Enceladus, 18 km on Ganymede and 6 km on Pluto. Unfocused, 4.7e−7 comets of 1 km or more strike a km² each Gyr inside x = 3.56 (Saturn), more as x² beyond, nearer the scattered disc, times the focusing of the planet a moon circles, or of the body itself: Nesvorný et al. 2023's rates on the moons of Jupiter, Saturn and Uranus, each over its planet's focusing, come to 5.3, 4.0 and 16.5 e−16 per km² per year, and the rule gives Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto, Enceladus and Miranda their rates within a factor of 1.3. The comets' flux declines from formation as a stretched exponential, exp(−(t / 1.6 Myr)^0.317) of it still to come (Wong et al. 2023's fit for Saturn), in all 2475 Gyr of today's flux: the 26e−3 and 18e−3 comets of 1 km per km² Wong et al. give Mimas and Enceladus over their whole history, over today's rates, are about 550 times 4.5 Gyr, as the Neukum chronology's early bombardment is about 500 times the Moon's. They strike at the share of the orbital speed about the star that falls from 0.5 at the Earth's place to 0.4 by the snow line, a moon adding three times the square of its own orbital speed (Zahnle et al. 2003): Io 31, Europa 24, Ganymede 20, Callisto 15, Enceladus 22 and Miranda 12 km/s, within 7 percent of Nesvorný et al.'s. Around another star the leftovers clear on its own orbital times: the early bombardment's timescales go as the orbital period at the snow line, (L/L☉)^0.75 (M/M☉)^−0.5, and its amount per unit area as the disc's solids, which go as the star's mass, over the area within the snow line, M/L (stated choices); the steady flux after it depends on belts and giant planets the engine does not model, and is `cratering.late_flux_multiplier` times the Solar System's at the same place, the early bombardment kept as it is. A planet two thirds of the Earth's insolation from an ultracool dwarf, as TRAPPIST-1e, meets its rocky leftovers at 26 km/s (Kral et al. 2018: 15 to 110), and its early bombardment, decaying over 1.7 Myr, is over long before its crust solidifies, so that it keeps a record of the late flux alone. The loop draws craters to the hold, a rocky body's beyond 1.5 radii, the stamp's limit, as mega-impacts (F168). Mimas's record then starts 4.09 Ga, near its plains' 4.16 to 4.21 Ga (Wong et al. 2023), and holds 1.31e−3 craters of 10 km per km² and 2.68e−4 of 20 km against the 1.38e−3 and 2.85e−4 counted (Kirchoff and Schenk's counts as Wong et al. give them); Enceladus's starts 4.25 Ga and Miranda's 4.20 Ga; Ceres's 3.97 Ga, after which it holds 13 craters over 100 km against the 16 counted (Marchi et al. 2016); the Moon, Mercury, Venus, the Earth, Io and the large icy moons keep their record from when their crust solidified; Mars's starts 3.95 Ga, its rocky flux, twice the Moon's with the Mars production function continued to basins, giving it two impacts wider than itself after its crust solidified. The comets' slope runs on below 1 km: Singer et al. 2019 found few craters under 13 km on Pluto and Charon, a deficit of comets under 1 to 2 km, but the moons of Saturn keep the steep slope down to craters of 2 km (Kirchoff and Schenk's counts), and with the deficit Mimas held a third of its craters of 10 km and Enceladus a tenth of its craters of 2 km (stated choice; Wong et al. 2023 date the moons either way). Jupiter's moons record fewer small craters than Saturn's, Zahnle et al. 2003's case A against their case B; one population everywhere gives them Saturn's. The gardening diffusivity follows the body's present production of craters of 1 km against the Moon's, the small impacts that do the gardening coming as often: about the Moon's on Mars, Ceres and Pluto, 1.9 times it on Callisto, 4 on Ganymede, 17 on Enceladus, whose craters of 1 km need comets of 33 m.

Each makes its crater by pi-scaling with the target's strength, D_tc = k d [g d / (2 v²) + (Y / (rho v²))^((2 + mu) / 2)]^(-mu / (2 + mu)) (Holsapple and Housen 2007, as Bottke et al. 2024 write it), k 1.1 and mu 0.55 for cold ice (Holsapple 2022), Y its 35 MPa or 7.6 MPa for rock (Holsapple 1993's soft rock, stated choice), the transient cavity collapsing by McKinnon and Schenk's law: under its strength a 1 km comet makes a crater of 18 km on Mimas, 17 on Enceladus, 12 on Miranda and 19 on Ganymede. - **F138 The thermal-orbital history of an icy moon.** A moon's eccentricity and its interior now evolve together, in one integration over its life in steps of half a million years (`orbital`), for every icy moon locked to its planet. The heat its tides make is the eccentricity's orbital energy, G M m e² / 2a, spent, and a free eccentricity decays by the moon's own dissipation in an e-folding time 2E / P: Mimas, with the porous core and the thin shell the stateless model gave it at its present eccentricity, 228 GW, would damp its eccentricity of 0.0196 in 0.4 Myr, against Meyer and Wisdom (2008)'s 325 Myr for a cold Mimas. A resonance that holds a moon now holds its forced eccentricity (`orbit.resonance_forced`: the Laplace resonance's Io, Europa and Ganymede, and Enceladus in its 2:1 resonance with Dione), with the cycle of F77. A past resonance (F50) supplies the energy the planet's tides feed it, stated as a heat (`orbit.resonance_heating`) or as the eccentricity the dynamics found, which supplies what that eccentricity gives a warm moon, as the dynamics assumed of it; the eccentricity rises until the moon dissipates as much, the equilibrium heating of Meyer and Wisdom (2007), which does not depend on the moon, so that a moon that dissipates little is pumped high and one that dissipates much is held low (a safety cap at 0.2, where Nakajima et al. 2019 find Enceladus's orbit crossing Tethys's). Otherwise the eccentricity is free: before a moon's first resonance it has none, whatever it formed with damped within a few Myr by its warm interior; after its last, its present value is the remnant of that resonance, which lets it go at the latest time that lets its eccentricity decay to the present value by the present, and the eccentricity never falls below the present value after (the remnant of an excitation the inputs do not state); a free moon with no resonance stated keeps its present eccentricity. The moon answers with its ice: its shell thickens and thins by the heat reaching its base and made in it against what it conducts, with the latent heat of the water it freezes and melts (a Stefan balance, stepped implicitly), from a warm start, the H2O layer liquid under the shell a warm moon's heat would hold or under a crust of 1 km (stated choice); frozen through, it melts once the heat reaching its base exceeds what the whole layer conducts with its base at the melting point. Its porous rocky interior dissipates (F68) only while its pores hold water: while an ocean touches it, freezing when the moon freezes through, and thawing again only once an ocean has lain over it for R² / κ, the time heat takes to diffuse through it (κ 1e-6 m²/s; Mimas's 72 km take 0.16 Gyr). The shell's own tides (F75) give the moon thresholds: a hard shell's heating and its conduction both go as the inverse of its thickness, so that above one eccentricity it thins away and below it freezes through (Mimas 0.026, Enceladus with a dry core 0.032, Miranda 0.043), and ice frozen to the rock, whose tide is an elastic sphere's, melts only at a higher one still (Mimas 0.066, within the two to three times its present value at which Walker and Rhoden find its melting began; Miranda 0.11). Mimas is a new reference body: frozen through by 0.3 Gyr and cold for four billion years; Meyer and Wisdom's Mimas-Dione 3:1 e²-Mimas resonance, 0.70 Gyr ago for a Saturn Q of 18,000 with an equilibrium heating of 1.9 GW, is 83 Myr ago at Saturn's dissipation as Lainey et al. 2017 measure it, supplying 16 GW; its eccentricity is pumped to 0.071 and its ice begins to melt 33 Myr ago, the shell overshooting to 11 km as the runaway is quenched, and the resonance lets it go some 5 Myr ago: it has today its eccentricity of 0.0196, a shell 28 km thick over an ocean 33 Myr old and a dry core, dissipating 6.6 GW, its eccentricity's e-folding time 15 Myr (Lainey et al. 2024: a shell 20 to 30 km thick over an ocean younger than 25 Myr). Miranda freezes through by 0.3 Gyr; its resonance, supplying the 42 GW that Ćuk et al. 2020's eccentricity of 0.0165 gives a warm, dissipating Miranda, pumps its eccentricity to 0.10 until its ice melts at 1.08 Ga, and holds it at 60 mW/m² under a 15 km shell, its eccentricity 0.044 while its core is dry, until it escapes at 0.85 Ga; frozen through again by 0.7 Ga. Enceladus, held from its warm start, keeps its 22.5 km shell, its wet core and its 29 GW. Ganymede's eccentricity rises toward 0.018 as its resonance's supply does and decays over 0.15 Gyr after, so that its lanes trail the resonance by 0.2 Gyr and the episode is placed 2.2 Gyr ago for its bright terrain of two. Europa's shell lags its cycle, 28.7 km today against 30.3; the thick oceans of Titan, Callisto and Triton are still freezing from the warm start (Triton's shell 260 km against 296).
- **F139 The elastic lid's flexural support.** The cold lid of an ice shell holds up the loads its flexure can carry and lets the longer ones settle onto the ductile ice below; F39 took the support as four times the lid's thickness, the same on every body. A thin plate's flexural wavelength is 2π (D / ρ g)^¼, its rigidity D = E Tₑ³ / 12 (1 − ν²) (Turcotte and Schubert 2002, section 3.17): it goes as Tₑ^¾ g^-¼, so that a small moon's lid holds loads many times its thickness. Mimas's 12 km lid over its young ocean holds Herschel up (its flexural wavelength 450 km, where four times the lid made 49 km and its ocean flattened Herschel within years), Enceladus's 12 km lid 370 km, Ganymede's 32 km lid 420 km; Ganymede's shell of 18 km at 4.5 Ga, its lid of 7 km, holds 130 km, so that its basins still relax to palimpsests early and its craters of 70 km keep their relief. The share of a load that settles rises smoothly from none at two thirds of the flexural wavelength to all at four thirds, about the thin plate's degree of compensation 1 / (1 + (λ_f / λ)⁴), which is half at the flexural wavelength; the plate's law itself leaves a short load a small share that never vanishes, and the relaxation's survivals, multiplied step after step, wore that share away (a 30 km crater on a shell with a 3 km lid lost half its relief over 24 steps where the plate holds 97 percent). Relaxation now wants lids of a kilometre or two, as Enceladus's relaxed craters do (Bland et al. 2012: 150 mW/m² or more), and the relaxation beside a renewed terrain under an episode's thinned shell (F66) takes less: a shell thinned to a fifth holds every crater under 75 km. A floating shell's isostatic share of a load, (ρ_w − ρ_i) / ρ_w, about 8 percent, is not added: it decays by the flow at the shell's base, a channel of the base's rheological thickness δ with 12 η (λ / 2π)² / (Δρ g δ³) for its time, within 1 to 80 Myr on shells with a basal viscosity of 1e14 Pa s (F137 had supposed it would keep their large scales); what keeps them is the lid's flexure.
- **F140 The shell's surface processes follow its history.** The focusing of tidal heating at a pole (F43, F53), the lanes (F44), the cracks (F76, F82) and the plumes (F57) took the shell's present state, or the peak of its history, as having held over the body's whole life, as it had for every body while each time's shell was the steady state of its heat. Mimas's ocean, 33 Myr old, gave it an Enceladus-like terrain at its pole, seven older ones the body had turned from it, lanes over a quarter of it, cracks of four billion years and plume fallout. A new expert node, `interior.ocean_age`, gives how long the shell has floated without freezing through, up to the present. The cracks form only within it, a young ocean's few against the retention age's; the focusing's past episodes only while the shell floated. The focusing thins the shell where its tides meet its conduction: a shell that conducts more than is made in it is freezing and thins by the share made, and the thinning grows over the shell's conductive time d² / κ since its ocean formed (both 1 for a body in a steady state). The lanes renew by the tidal heat the lid carries above the floor over the shell's life, the lesser of its tides and what it conducts, so that a shell melting under a lid still thick or freezing from a warm start renews nothing, against the same heat carried over LANE_RENEWAL_GYR, 0.56 Gyr, calibrated as ICE_RESURFACING_FLUX was on Ganymede's two thirds (0.69): a brief spike of heat renews little and a steady flux all it can. Mimas keeps no active polar terrain (its thinning 0.21 against the 0.3 that renews one), no past episodes, no plumes and a few cracks of its young ocean; its ocean's heat, 13 mW/m² carried for 33 Myr, three times the excess of Ganymede's episode, still renews a tenth of it in young lanes by this law, where Lainey et al. 2024 find its ocean too young to have marked its surface: the lanes want the strain of the lid, not its heat alone. Enceladus, Ganymede and Europa are as they were: their oceans are as old as they are and their shells in balance.
- **F141 The renewed ground keeps the base relief.** The floor a lane, a corona or a polar terrain laid was a plain low-pass of the relief at the terrain's smoothing length, so every old crater near that length survived as a soft dimple and all texture went with the craters: the user saw the renewed ground as a Gaussian blur, on Mimas's young lanes, on Enceladus's polar terrain and in Miranda's coronae, and asked whether the base noise field could be kept under the new terrain so that the craters smooth out and the micro-detail stays. A terrain levels the relief over what it stretches across, its own scale: a lane over its width (or the lanes' smoothing length, if more; it was the smoothing length alone, 10 km on Mimas against lanes 20 to 40 km wide, so that its 10 to 20 km craters stayed as dimples), a corona over half its outline's radius as before, a polar terrain over its zone's radius (it was a fifth of the body's; the thinned zone reworks everything inside it, and Enceladus's south polar terrain keeps no crater). What it levels is what impacts and older terrains added: under the floor the base relief (F137), the relief the body formed with, is kept below that length, in full at half the length and below and fading to nothing at it by the logarithm of the wavelength, so that two lanes of nearly the same width keep nearly the same relief and no seam runs between them. Below the spacing of the terrain's own fabric, its grooves, faults or folds, which overprint what is finer than themselves, the base relief is toned down to FINE_TEXTURE_SHARE of its amplitude, 0.4, over an octave (a stated choice, for the smoother look of new ice between its grooves, which the user asked for over the full relief's grain). The base relief is regenerated from the same field at whatever resolution draws it, down to two of its cells: the loop restores the octaves its grid holds when it lays the floor, as the bands the renewed cell gave up, which relax from then on like anything new; the finish adds those below the loop's cells, and all of them where it lays the floor itself, out to the lane's resolved edge. The floor's low-pass is read from the pyramid's levels blended by the logarithm of the length. Three tries went before: every octave below the smoothing length at full amplitude buried Mimas's grooves (108 m at 1.8 km under 340 m of relief); each lane's floor from its own pyramid level put sawtooth seams where overlapping lanes of a set, a level apart, crossed; and the base relief kept only below half the fabric's spacing left pixel grain over a blank mid-scale, grainier than the old ground in the shading and still blurred in the height map. Mimas's lanes are now crater-free ground with the base relief's undulation and a fine grain, their grooves faint on so thin a lid; Enceladus's polar terrain a plain at its depression with its folds, the ghosts of its 20 to 50 km craters gone; Miranda's coronae their faults over the relief their plains have.
- **F142 A lane's ends taper.** Every lane end that did not meet an older lane was cut off at a fault of its own across the lane, so that nearly every end was square (the taper for an end without a cut ran almost nowhere). An end that has run its length and meets no older lane now dies out in the old ground: its bounding faults converge and its grooves fade with their throw, the width closing as a linear wedge over three widths (a fault's displacement falls to its tip), so that the lanes pinch out in the long points Ganymede's do. Venus: 71,000 ridges, against 78,000 before and the 65,000 Bilotti and Suppe mapped, 87 percent of their length below the mean height where 68 percent of its plains lie (75 percent at a share of 0.1, the most of the shares tried from 0 to 0.1; audit M26); Mars: its plains' ridges concentric to Tharsis as Lunae Planum's are, 72 percent of their length within four rise radii within 30° of round it.
- **F157 The floods' loads on the lid, and the field through time.** The lid's stress field read the ground before the rises, low-passed over the flexural wavelength of the lid of the present, and held no load of the floods; the plains' ridges stood in for the floods' load with the lava's thickness, their seeds weighted by its gradient and their courses along its contours, and with a stated drive past the strength (F156, audit M26). The loop now reads the field at epochs, every 0.3 Gyr, at an episodic lid's renewal and at the end: the relief as the ground then stands, less the rises' domes the field holds itself, low-passed over the flexural wavelength of the lid as thick as it is then, which carries its potential energy's stress; and the stress of the lava laid since the last load, once 10 m of it lies anywhere (less is carried on), bending the lid of its time. Each load bends the lid as it was when the lava came and the stresses add, the lid that thickens beneath a load later bearing none of it. The lid is a shallow spherical shell over the mantle, D ∇⁴w + E h w / R² + ρ_m g w = q, of the rigidity of its elastic part then, under the lava's weight at the magma's density (F125); its membrane, with the Airy stress function ∇²Φ = −E h w / R, stiffens it and adds its own stress through its thickness to the bending stress at its top, the deflection's mean left out, which an incompressible interior lets no lid make (Turcotte et al. 1981). It is solved on a cube-sphere grid with two cells across the flexural parameter (D / ρ_m g)^¼, within faces of 16 to 128, by conjugate gradients on D Gᵀ M G + k M, the operator the plate's bending energy gives, G the Laplacian read as the trace of each cell's curvature, fitted by least squares on a quadratic over the cell and its eight neighbours, exact for a quadratic on every cell across the cube's edges and corners (a two-point Laplacian was hundreds of percent off at the face edges, where the grid kinks); the stress function by BiCGSTAB on G. Against the Hankel transform's solutions of a disc of lava 434 km across under a 60 km lid on the Moon, the plate alone comes out squeezed by 47.8 MPa at the middle against 47.1, 54.4 at two thirds out against 53.7, and stretched by 44.7 radially at 1.2 radii against 43.6; the shell sinks 1,451 m against 1,471 and is squeezed by 74.1 MPa at the middle against 74.3. The flat plate squeezes a filled basin most round it near its margin and stretches the lid outward beyond it while squeezing it round, so its thrusts would run outward in spokes and its margin would slip sideways, the paradox of the flat plate's mascons (Freed, Melosh and Solomon 2001); the shell squeezes the basin most outward, so its thrusts run round it, and pulls the lid beyond the margin both ways (at 300 km out 76 MPa outward and 59 round against 78 and 64; at 1.4 radii 19 outward and 13 round against 16 and 13). A thrust now breaks only where both horizontal stresses, the shrinking's squeeze with them, press harder than the overburden, as Anderson's thrusts need; the field decides its strike against a tenth of the strength or of the mean squeeze, whichever is greater, as a weakness lowers a plane's strength by a share of the stress pressing it shut, so that a squeeze nearly alike every way decides little however strong; and the thrusts before the surface's record began are gone with it, as the lid's faults are (F147), the lid failing and bearing them all the same: the Moon's thousand scarps up to 600 km long came of its first lid, 4 km thick under the primordial relief. The plains' ridges read the field alone, their seeds as the plains are squeezed and their courses across the field's compression where it decides them, their own way where it does not; the lava's thickness, its gradient and its contours are gone, the loads in the field standing for what they stood for, and so is the stated share of the plains' drive: the plains stand at their strength at least, brought there by drives the field does not hold (a stated choice with nothing fitted: Venus's climate warming its plains after their renewal, Solomon et al. 1999, which the model's atmosphere does not carry, and the plains' own cooling), and the field decides where they fold and where they do not. Without that floor Venus's plains, whose lavas were laid on the thin lid after its overturn and stressed by tens of MPa, would stand nowhere past their strength of 110.
- **F158 The graben the loads open.** Beyond the margin of a filled basin its load stretches the lid's top past what breaks its faulted layer, and normal faults open there, as the arcuate graben about the lunar maria do (Rimae Hippalus about Mare Humorum; Solomon and Head 1980). A population from the faults' generator (F111, F112) spends the extension the loads add past the yield, the field's tension over the lid's stiffness beyond what the field holds without them, so that the rises' and the limbs' own are not spent twice, on graben within the faulted layer, as the lid's own faults are (F147; a stated choice, the loads' stretch being small): each starts where the loads stretch the lid past the yield, runs across the most tensile stress as the field leads it while the loads still stretch the lid there, and forms as the stretch at its start grew through the epochs, when it reached a uniform share of what it came to; those formed before the record began are gone with it. Like every normal fault, a load's graben forms where the most tensile stress passes the yield (F150). The shell's stress about a filled basin runs them round it just beyond its margin and outward farther off, where the membrane's pull round the basin overtakes the bending's outward (at 1.6 radii 7 MPa outward and 17 round). The Moon: 187 graben, a median 82 km from the maria's margins, 59 km long at the median and 3 km wide, formed between 4.05 and 3.2 Ga; Mercury 142 about its smooth plains; Mars some 300; Venus 22, its plains' lavas laid on a lid too thin for them to stretch it much.
- **F159 The ridges' sizes.** Every ridge stood 200 m high, ±40 percent, whatever its length, and ran a length drawn evenly in its logarithm from 20 to 300 km, so that once the field turned the plains' ridges alike they filled a basin with parallel lines all as plain as one another, a fingerprint where the maria have a few ridges standing out of a fainter many. A ridge is a fold over a thrust (F155), and now takes the thrusts' laws: its length from their power law, its height RIDGE_HEIGHT at 100 km (a stated reference: lunar mare ridges stand some 100 to 300 m over lengths of tens to hundreds of kilometres) growing with its length as a thrust's displacement does, by the scarps' exponent, ±40 percent, and its arch and crest widening with its height, down to a quarter of their widths: most ridges short and low, a few long and high. The thrusts beneath the plains' ridges take the same heights.
- **F160 Thrusts and ridges in systems.** The thrusts and the plains' ridges were drawn each on its own, so they lay as a scatter of separate lines, or, where the field turned them alike, as a whorl of arcs filling a basin, where Mercury's scarps gather in systems and the maria's few prominent ridges run in rings broken by gaps. They now gather as the lid's faults do (F112, F147). Half of them start beside one drawn before near where they start, the thrusts beside a thrust and the plains' ridges beside a ridge, the thrusts' ridges among them: a host chosen by length among those whose traces pass within a coarse cell or so of the start, so that a system grows where the lid is squeezed now, the newcomer running along a span of it about its point nearest the start, as long as its own length, to one side at 0.6 to 1.6 of the host's reach, the width over which it changes the ground (a stated choice: the Tharsis plateau's ridges some 30 km apart, Watters 1991; lobate scarps in arrays tens of kilometres apart), the plains' ridges only as far as the squeezed plains reach. The rest run their own way, across the field's compression as far as it decides them, and stop where they come within a quarter of their reaches of one drawn before at least as long, whose slip took up the squeeze they would have run on (a stated choice, as the lid's faults' arrest). The drawn are indexed on a coarse grid of cells 120 km wide. Mercury: 9,600 scarps against 6,800, shorter for their arrest, in clusters of parallel scarps; Mars's lava basin east of Tharsis: a set of long sinuous ridges among fainter ones where the ridges were even dashes; the Moon's central mare: groups of arcs with quiet ground between, where a whorl of arcs filled it.
- **F161 The shields' loads in the field.** The lid's field held the floods' lava as loads (F157) but not the shields', the greatest loads a lid bears. Each epoch's load now holds the height the shields have piled since the last as well, at the magma's density: the lid beneath and about a great shield is squeezed, and stretched in the flexural arch beyond, where graben ring the load (Comer, Solomon and Head 1985; McGovern and Solomon 1993; Alba Mons's own stress shaped the graben about it, Cailleau et al. 2003), and the thrusts, the plains' ridges and the loads' graben (F158) read it. The rises' domes stay out of the loads, held up from below by their plumes.
- **F162 The ground bent under its loads.** The loads stressed the lid but left the ground as it lay, so a shield stood on level plains and a filled basin kept the floor it was filled to. Each epoch's deflection is now laid into the ground, at the end of the step it falls in. Under the floods the ground sinks with the lid, and later floods fill what it opens; beneath a shield the edifice's lava fills the sag as far as it is thick, its surface staying as it built it, its height the magma's to raise and its root in the sag, while the ground about it sinks into a moat, deepest at its foot, and rises in a low bulge beyond (Olympus Mons's moat, some 2 km deep and partly filled by its aureole; McGovern and Solomon 1993). The edifice's root, the lava that fills its sag, is left out of the load (a stated choice: counted, it would deepen the sag again each epoch). The finish's floods see the subsidence after their step, as they see the shields' and the canyons' growth, so that their lava lies on the ground they found; the lava's own subsidence (LAVA_SUBSIDENCE) now stands for the pile's compaction alone. Mars's largest shield, 684 km across and 25 km high, stands in a moat 2.4 km below the ground at 2.5 of its radii, its summit 26.0 km where it was 27.2, and the region about Tharsis sinks 1.5 to 2 km under its lavas and shields, its lowest basin to −8.0 km; the Moon's central mare sinks some 1 to 1.5 km under its fill, while the lava in its craters, too narrow to bend the lid far, sinks less; Venus's resurfacing fill sinks the lid by up to 1.7 km; Mercury's deepest basin to −9.3 km from −7.2.
- **F163 The shields' lava at their feet.** A shield's flows stopped at its foot, its edifice all its lava, and the moat its load bends the ground into (F162) stood open. A shield's eruptions are F101's: fissures round its summit caldera, on its rift zone low on the flank and on its upper flanks, each a flow field whose area, its run-out squared, is its volume, spread evenly along its run as its breakouts are drawn by the lengths of the lobes laid (a stated choice). What their runs carry past the foot is 41 percent of a round shield's lava, nearly all of it from the rift zone, whose flows run 0.2 to 1.0 of a radius from vents low on the flank (Tharsis's young flows run 15 to 310 km, Peters et al. 2021, and the vents at the ends of Arsia and Ascraeus Montes' rift zones feed aprons onto the plains), and less where an elongated edifice's rift zone runs farther to its foot. Each step a shield sends that share of what its edifice grows by past its foot, in eruptions of a flood's size poured out at a shield's rates (SHIELD_EFFUSION_RATE), so that they come as compound flow fields of its own lava. Each starts at a point along a flow's run beyond the foot, drawn by the lava the flow carries there and never on another edifice standing higher than a flow is thick by then, whose flanks the lava would not climb (a neighbour that has hardly grown does not turn it, and where no point along the runs is kept the eruption starts at the foot at an azimuth of its own), and runs on down as a flood's lava does, ponding in the moat and spilling past its bulge; it loads the lid as the floods' lava does, so the moat sinks further under its fill (Olympus Mons's moat is thought mostly filled with lava and landslide deposits; Isherwood et al. 2013). Mars's shields send 5.4 million km³ past their feet, some two thirds of what their edifices hold: the largest shield's moat fills to 0.25 to 0.55 km below the ground at 2.5 of its radii, where it was 2.3 km deep, its lava hemmed in between it and its western neighbours standing to 7.1 km, and the smaller shields' moats stand 0.5 to 2.1 km deep, where they were 0.4 to 3.7. Venus's shields send 1.0 million km³ and fill their shallow moats by 0.1 to 0.4 km.
- **F164 The shields' melt is one budget.** A rise's shields took a share of the floods' eruptions (F108) that left the lava's budget whatever they did with it, while their edifices grew by a clock of their own (F120), and the two did not meet: Mars's shields took 6.6 × 10⁷ km³ of its floods' budget while their edifices held 8.3 × 10⁶ km³, and Venus's edifices and the lava past their feet held ten times what its shields took. A shield now grows from the lava its rise's eruptions bring it. Before the loop, step by step, each rise's started shields take the share of the step's extrusive budget that what the rise adds to the floods' vent weights, by its fertile mantle and its dome, takes of their whole, by the share they gather (F108, F119), read against the ground the loop begins on; they share it by their edifices' volumes, and the floods lose exactly what the shields take. A shield's edifice holds what its flows lay on it until it is full, while its flows carry their share past its foot (F163). A full edifice lifts no more, and the magma its rise still brings it intrudes beneath it (a choice: erupted as plains about the rise, Mars's would bury Tharsis under kilometres of lava; handed back to the floods, it would flood the dome and canyons as before F108). A full edifice's flanks go on being renewed by eruptions too small to count, at the pace its supply comes (a stated choice), its volcano's dike swarms come at shares of its growth (F116), and its calderas sink by the share of its height it built. Mars's budget is right by the measure: Greeley and Schneid (1991) put its volcanic deposits since the Early Hesperian at 6.45 × 10⁷ km³ and its Amazonian rate at 0.012 to 0.014 km³/yr, where the model's late rate is 0.012 to 0.016. Mars's shields take 7.1 × 10⁷ km³: their edifices fill by 1.6 to 1.9 Gyr, the largest by 2.9 Ga (Olympus Mons's main stage had ended by 2.54 Ga, Isherwood et al. 2013), holding 8.3 × 10⁶ km³ with 5.6 × 10⁶ km³ past their feet, and 5.7 × 10⁷ km³ intrudes beneath them. Venus's shields take 3.4 × 10⁵ km³ of its small budget after the overturn and stand 6 to 15 percent built, 0.4 to 1.6 km high (audit M29).
- **F165 The plumes' melt at their rises, and a volcano's weight gathering its rise's lava.** The mantle's melt was reckoned with its plumes' excess heat over the whole body (F123), and an episodic lid kept a stated 2 percent of it after its overturn, "the rest having gone into the event". The ambient mantle now makes the share of the melt its own gate keeps, and the plumes the rest only under their heads, the area their rises' provinces cover: the floods' budget is the ambient mantle's melt anywhere and the plumes' over their heads, and the vents weigh the ambient mantle's provinces by its share. Once the mantle under the lid has cooled below its solidus there, only the plumes melt, and their lava comes out at the rises. Venus's ambient mantle stops melting 0.45 Ga ago, as its overturn's surge ends; Mars's tapers from 1 Ga to nothing at the present, and its eruptions since 1.3 Ga come to 1.19 × 10⁷ km³ where Greeley and Schneid (1991) put its Middle and Late Amazonian deposits at 1.18 × 10⁷ km³ (they had been 1.95 × 10⁷). On an episodic lid the melt that comes while the ambient mantle still melts after the overturn is the overturn's own resurfacing, which the event lays; the floods after it are the plumes'. A rise's started shields share its lava by the area each gathers dikes from: its own vents' area and the area over which its weight's stress, its weight over that area, outweighs the regional stress, the rise's, at the lid's yield at its peak and falling as its growth wanes (F119; a load turns rising dikes toward it from where its stress outweighs the regional stress, Muller, Ito and Martel 2001). As the rise's growth ends the shields gather by their weights alone: the leading volcano keeps its lead, and one that starts as the rise stops never grows and sends no dikes. Venus's lava after its overturn, 1.05 × 10⁶ km³, all comes out at its rises, and each rise's leading volcano takes most of it: 5.1, 4.6 and 2.8 km high, 296 to 328 km across, as Maat Mons stands 4.9 km over its plains, 395 km across, with flanks of 1.5 to 2 degrees; their neighbours stand 0.3 to 0.9 km or never grew. Mars's largest shield fills by 3.0 Ga, the next two by 2.1 and 1.3 Ga, and the two smallest stand half and a third built.
- **F166 An edifice's root weighs on the lid.** The lava that fills a shield's sag was left out of its load (F162): counted epoch by epoch it would have deepened the sag again each time. It is now counted where it lies. Beneath an edifice the lid sinks into the edifice's own lava, which fills the sag as far as the edifice is thick, so that the mantle pushes back there by the difference between the rock's density and the lava's, not by the rock's whole, as a load sinking into its own infill does (Turcotte and Schubert, Geodynamics): the plate is solved with its restoring force cell by cell, the share of the sag the edifice fills read from the deflection it fills and then once more, and a floor of 100 kg/m³ (ROOT_MIN_CONTRAST, a stated choice) keeps a lava as dense as the rock afloat. The lid's strength holds the root within a few times the sag of the edifice alone: a shield 600 km across and 20 km high sinks 16.4 km beneath its summit on a Mars lid 70 km thick, where it sank 10.0 km, 1.25 times as far on a lid 30 km thick and 1.16 times on Venus's 25 km. Mars's largest shield now stands in a moat some 3 km below the ground at 2.5 of its radii, the next in 4 km, the lava past their feet filling part of them, their summits where they built them; Venus's leading volcanoes' surroundings sink some 0.2 km. The magma a full edifice cannot lift (F164) is not a load beneath it: Mars's largest shield's would stand as a column some 115 km thick over its footprint, thicker than the lid, so it spreads beneath the rise as the underplating of its crust, lighter than the mantle it replaces, which the rise's dome holds (a stated reading).
- **F167 A crater's elongation follows its cratering efficiency.** A cavity came out elliptical below an angle fixed for every crater on every body, the one below which 5 percent of impacts strike (about 12°; Bottke et al. 2000), so the largest basins were as round as the small craters. A cavity comes out elliptical, longer than 1.1 times its width, below a threshold angle that rises as the cratering efficiency falls, the diameter of the crater a vertical impact makes over the impactor's: laboratory impacts in sand, aluminium and lead and simulations in the strength and gravity regimes fall on one trend (Collins et al. 2011, Figure 1), whose line is 90° times the efficiency to the power of −0.73 (ELLIPTICAL_THRESHOLD_EXPONENT, digitised from the figure, within 0.2° from efficiencies of 1 to 100). The efficiency is the transient cavity over the impactor, from the body's own crater scaling (F135) at its mean impact speed for every population that strikes it (a stated choice), tabulated by the crater's diameter: the larger the crater, the lower its efficiency in the gravity regime and the steeper its threshold. Below the threshold the elongation grows as simulations of oblique impacts on a flat target show, 1.1 at the threshold, 1.25 at three quarters of it, 1.7 at half and 1.01 at one and a half times (Collins, Miljković and Davison 2013), up to 1.8 as before. Mars's craters of 10 km come out elliptical in 5.8 percent of impacts and those of 30 km in 8.3 (some 6.5 percent are observed from 10 to 40 km; Collins et al. 2011), its basins of 2,500 km in 35 percent (their model, near 30); the Moon's craters of 10 km in 3.5 percent. The largest basins are elongated as Borealis (10,650 by 8,520 km; Andrews-Hanna, Zuber and Banerdt 2008), Hellas, Utopia and South Pole–Aitken are (aspect ratios above 1.2; Andrews-Hanna and Zuber 2010). Planetary curvature, proposed to elongate them further, suppresses elongation in simulations instead (Collins, Miljković and Davison 2013) and is left out. The node cratering.oblique_fraction is retired.
- **F168 The dichotomy is an outcome.** The primordial crust carried a hemispheric contrast, half an amplitude times tanh(3 cos θ) about a random axis, the amplitude by the regime's name (20 km of crust on every stagnant lid): a smooth slope over thousands of kilometres that the basins hid (T5). The crust now starts without it, and a dichotomy comes of the impacts the body takes. A rocky body holds a basin as wide as a hemisphere: Mars kept the Borealis basin, 10,650 by 8,520 km (Andrews-Hanna, Zuber and Banerdt 2008), its long axis half the planet's circumference, where F135 held a rocky body to its own diameter (Vesta's Rheasilvia, 1.93 radii, a small body's survivor rather than a planet's limit), so Mars's record starts 0.47 Gyr after its formation rather than 0.57, and it takes about twice the craters it took (21 basins over 1,000 km where it took 11; Frey 2008 counted some 20, buried ones among them); the Moon's, Mercury's and Venus's records start when their crusts solidify, as before. The impacts wider than the stamp draws, from 1.5 body radii up to a hemisphere, are mega-impacts, which take a form of their own. The basin excavates the crust inside its elliptical rim (F167) down to the crust its melt sheet makes, half the body's starting crust (MEGA_FLOOR_CRUST_SHARE, a stated choice between Mars's northern lowlands, some 32 km against the southern highlands' 58, Neumann et al. 2004, and the thinner crust of South Pole–Aitken's floor, Wieczorek et al. 2013), the excavation giving way to the rim over the outer twentieth of its radius (MEGA_WALL_SHARE, a stated choice: a boundary scarp for the surface processes to wear). The excavated crust lands beyond the rim, thinning with distance as a basin's ejecta does (BASIN_EJECTA_EXPONENT), its volume kept, and thickens the crust there. The ground stands where the crust's buoyancy in the mantle holds it (Airy, as the primordial crust's does): the floor, where the melt ponded level, at the height its new crust floats to against the body's mean column, whatever stood there before, and the ground beyond raised by the ejecta's crust, the older relief under the ejecta buried by its thickness as a crater's ejecta buries it, and the continuous ejecta renews the surface within two radii, as a crater's does. The thin crust the basin leaves draws the later floods to it (F36), which fill its lowland. The crust a stamped basin thins under it, by excavation and the mantle's rise, was lost, so that the bombardment drained the crust it struck, Mars's from a mean of 40 km to 28 by its mega-impact; it now lands beyond the basin's rim, thinning outward as the basin's ejecta does, the crust kept and thickest in an annulus about the basin, as the lunar basins' are (Wieczorek et al. 2013). On the Mars preset (seed 1048) the basin's floor stands some 2.2 km below the datum and the ground beyond its rim some 2.2 km above it, 4.4 km apart (the Martian dichotomy is some 5.5 km), the floods having raised the floor by some 0.4 km since. Over 200 seeds Mars takes 1.34 mega-impacts on average, at least one on 74 percent of them and one wider than 2.5 radii, a Borealis, on 22 percent; the Moon 0.58, on 42 percent (South Pole–Aitken, some 2,500 km, falls just short of one), Mercury 0.46, on 35 percent; Venus and the Earth few, the icy moons none, their hold below the stamp's limit. The nodes tectonics.dichotomy_mode and tectonics.dichotomy_amplitude are retired.
- **F169 An ejecta deposit levels only the hollows it can fill.** A crater's ejecta buried the older relief under them by their thickness against its height above the mean of the ring about the crater the point lay on, and a mega-impact's (F168) against the ground low-passed over its basin's radius: around the largest basins the reference was the mean of thousands of kilometres, so that a deposit kilometres thick buried swells as wide as a hemisphere, and the user saw a Moon struck by one (master seed 6342) left flat but for its basin, where the primordial relief (F137) had been believable ground. A deposit fills the hollows it can and drapes the rest: it levels the ground over the width of the fresh crater as deep as it is thick, the largest hollow it can fill, from the body's own crater scaling, but no farther than it can spread, as far as a landslide's debris runs out for its fall (LANDSLIDE_REACH's longer end, 14 times, F106). On the Moon a deposit 10 m thick levels over some 50 m, 100 m over 500 m, 1 km over 5 km, 3 km over 32 km and 10 km over 140 km. A crater's rings split into sectors about that long, whose means are the reference; a mega-impact's reference is the ground low-passed over that width, cell by cell. The longer relief stands under the ejecta as it stood, draped. The Moon of master seed 6342, struck by a mega-impact 4,302 km across, keeps its far side's swells and lowlands under the ejecta, and the belt beyond its basin's rim, which the old reference had partly buried as relief, stands some 6 km high, as the highlands about South Pole–Aitken reach 6 to 8 km; Mars's highlands along its dichotomy stand higher and roll, the heights of its lowland gathering about −1.8 km and its highlands spreading to some 5 km.
- **F170 A rocky lid's lower crust flows where it is warm.** An ice shell's relief relaxed (F39) and a rocky lid's never did. Where the base of a rocky crust is warm its lower crust flows, a channel at the crust's base as thick as the depth over which its viscosity falls by e along the crust's conductive profile, δ = R T² / (Q dT/dz), beneath the crust it holds rigid; the pressure of the relief above drives it, moving the crust from under the high ground to under the low, so that, the crust kept compensated, the relief diffuses with κ = (Δρ/ρₘ) ρ g δ³ / η and a wavelength decays as (λ/2π)² / κ, the longest lasting (McKenzie, Nimmo and others 2000; Nimmo and Stevenson 2001), where the elastic lid does not hold it up (F139's support, with rock's modulus). The lower crust creeps as dry diabase (Mackwell, Zimmerman and Kohlstedt 1998: n 4.7, Q 485 kJ/mol, A 8 MPa⁻ⁿ s⁻¹), its viscosity read at the stress a kilometre of relief drives and never below 10¹⁸ Pa s (stated choices); the crust's base stands on the lid's conductive profile from the surface to the mantle at the lithosphere's base, under the crust's tenth thickest, whose base is the warmest (a stated choice; the relaxation is the body's, band by band, not the cell's). The relief relaxes toward the domes the plumes hold up, which are not the crust's. A stagnant rocky lid relaxes (a stated scope): a mobile lid's crust is its plates' (Phase 6); Io's heat pipe is not the conductive lid its thermal history describes; and an episodic lid's highlands, Venus's tesserae, stand on the primordial relief rather than on a crust their tectonics thickened, which its crust, its base above 1,600 K through its first billion years, relaxes away, leaving Venus flat but for its volcanoes (audit M31). The model's thermal histories keep the base of Mars's, the Moon's and Mercury's crusts below 1,200 K, where diabase holds its relief for the age of the solar system, as Nimmo and Stevenson found for a Martian crust thinner than some 100 km, so that their dichotomies and basins stand as they formed and their maps are unchanged; a body whose crust is thicker or whose interior is hotter relaxes: under a crust 100 km thick, Mars's crust's base would stand at 1,600 K at 0.1 Gyr, and in its first half billion years its relief 1,000 km long would relax away and two thirds of that 3,000 km long, while its elastic lid held up that 300 km long.
- **F171 The floods fill the low ground outside the provinces too.** A flood's vent was drawn by the crust's thinness and the ground's lowness (F36) times the magmatic provinces' weight, a Gaussian about each province over a background of 0.03 of its centre: the provinces outweighed everything outside them some thirty times, so that on Mars, whose northern lowland is its lowest ground on its thinnest crust and draws five to thirteen times the vents per area that its highlands do, the floods spread as evenly as the provinces lay, 643 m of lava over the lowland against 659 m over the highlands, of which some 270 m Hesperian. Mars's Early Hesperian plains filled most of its northern lowlands to some 800 to 1,000 m, and its highland basins too, a flood-basalt phase over the low ground rather than within provinces (Head, Kreslavsky and Pratt 2002). A province's centre now stands four times the rest (MAGMA_PROVINCE_FLOOR 0.25, fitted on Mars): the lowland holds some 900 m of lava against 600 m on the highlands. The Moon's lava does not gather in its province at any weight, its basins' thin crust deciding where it lies (audit M33). The lowland's craters keep their rims under it, the lava pooling on their floors: craters of 50 to 200 km stand 2 to 3 km deep, and the lowland's plains bury their own to ghosts with the sediments of Phase 5 and the young surface they leave.
- **F172 An edifice its crust cannot hold sheds its flanks.** A shield's slope (SHIELD_SLOPE_DEG) would build it as high as its radius allows, and the relief its crust's strength holds (interior.max_relief, F15) caps it: the edifice that reaches that cap stood at it untouched, as its smaller neighbours do below it. Olympus Mons is ringed by a basal scarp 6 to 10 km high and by its aureole, lobes of landslide deposits, Lycus Sulci the largest, reaching some 700 km beyond its edge and more than 2 km thick where two overlap (DLR and ESA 2023). An edifice past what its crust holds now sheds its flanks once it has filled to the cap: the lava its supply would have stacked above the cap spreads them instead, as it comes, as much of its toe as that lava is pushed out beyond its foot and breaks away, and the edifice ends in a scarp where the toe beyond it holds that much, its wall at 30° (SCARP_WALL_SLOPE_DEG) and its reach varying round the edifice by 6 percent of itself (SCARP_WARP; stated choices). The toe and the lava together, twice the spread lava, run out beyond the scarp as its aureole, in four to seven lobes sharing the circle (AUREOLE_LOBES), each as far as a rock avalanche of its volume runs for the scarp's height (Scheidegger 1973's trend as it is quoted, log₁₀(H/L) = −0.15666 log₁₀ V + 0.62419 with V in m³, which gives the canyons' landslides of some 400 km³ a reach of 16 beside F106's 7 to 14), widening by LANDSLIDE_SPREAD to its toe as a landslide's lobe does, its surface ridged across its run some 12 km apart, the ridges' crests wandering by up to a spacing and their height coming and going over a few, as Lycus Sulci's are curved, broken and uneven (AUREOLE_RIDGE_SPACING, AUREOLE_RIDGE_SHARE, AUREOLE_RIDGE_WARP; stated choices), the ridges drawn by the finish. The edifice fills first and its flanks fail as the lava after that comes, the toe beyond the scarp breaking away and the aureole spreading with it, the supply covering the aureole with the edifice (F164); the aureole weighs on the lid in the moat with the lava (F162). An edifice its supply never fills never fails: Venus's three largest shields would build 12.9 to 14.3 km against the 10.5 km its crust holds, but its supply builds them only part of the way (F165), and none fails. Mars's largest shield, 626 km across and 25 km high where its slope would build 27.4 km, ends in a scarp at 0.81 of its reach, 4.5 to 9.9 km high round it, and lays seven lobes that run out 120 to 350 km, shorter than Olympus Mons's longest, a smaller share each running less far; its smaller neighbours stand below the cap and keep their toes. On the map the largest shield is ringed by its cliff, and its aureole's corrugated lobes lie in its moat and beyond.
- **F173 The mantle's convection holds the ground up.** A rocky mantle's history (F121) was one temperature, one lid and one flux, with nothing across the body: its rises stood where they were drawn, and the ground beyond them where the primordial relief (F137) and the impacts left it, though some nine tenths of Venus's relief is held up by its mantle's flow (Simons, Solomon and Hager 1997), its rises over the upwellings and its lowlands over the downwellings. Beneath a stagnant or an episodic lid the mantle now convects in the layer from the stiff lid's base to the core, in cells as wide, an upwelling to the next, as 2√2 times the layer's depth (CONVECTION_CELL_WAVELENGTH, a stated choice: the wavelength at which convection between free-slip boundaries sets in, Chandrasekhar 1961), its power spread over the degrees about theirs (CONVECTION_SPECTRUM_WIDTH, a stated choice): a few large cells in a thick mantle and many small ones in a thin one, as King (2008) found Mercury's thin mantle convecting in many narrow rolls where Venus's, the Earth's and Mars's rise in a few plumes. The ground the flow holds up, raised over the upwellings and drawn down over the downwellings, has a root mean square of 2.2 times α ΔT_rh δ_rh, the convecting sublayer's thermal contraction over its thickness, its temperature drop LID_BASE_SCALES rheological scales and its thickness k ΔT_rh / q from the mantle's own stagnant-lid flux (CONVECTIVE_TOPOGRAPHY: the mean over the eleven time-dependent stagnant-lid simulations of Guimond, Rudge and Shorttle 2022, 1.5 to 3.2 across viscosity contrasts of 10⁶ to 10⁹, whose sublayers are k ΔT_rh / q thick within 8 percent; their own fit in the interior Rayleigh number and the viscosity contrast runs away beyond the range it was fitted on, where this form holds); an elastic lid holds it flatter at the wavelengths it bends over, a wavelength's deflection 1 / (1 + D k⁴ / ρ g) of the mantle's own. A layer convects once it is several sublayers deep (CONVECTION_ONSET, a stated choice). The pattern renews itself in the time the flux takes to carry off the heat the sublayer holds, ρ c_p ΔT_rh δ_rh / q, and at each overturn of an episodic lid, one state turning into the next along a quarter circle so that it keeps its strength. The pattern is chaotic, so it is drawn from the body's seed, as the craters' places are; its scale, its strength and how fast it changes are the mantle's. The loop lifts and lowers the ground as the pattern stands at each step's end, before the step's craters and floods, so that the plains pond in the lows of their time and are warped as the pattern moves on; the lower crust relaxes toward it rather than away (F170), and the lid bears none of it, as it bears none of the rises' domes (F150): the relief the field reads leaves it out, as the overturn's tesserae do. A body's plumes rise where its mantle wells up most strongly as they start, the strongest first, each apart from those before, where they were drawn anywhere, and the pattern turns with the body as its rises turn it (F104). The upwelling limbs between plumes (F150), which pull the lid apart across them, ran along the great circles between plumes nearer than four of their radii together; they now follow the ridges of the pattern the plumes rose from, each joining two plumes whose ridge stands above the mantle's mean all the way between them (UPWELLING_LIMB_SADDLE, a stated choice), along the way that keeps highest (UPWELLING_LIMB_RIDGE, a stated choice), however long, so that the plumes a downwelling parts stay apart, and the rifts they open and the coronae they draw (F151) follow the mantle's flow. Venus's cells are of degree 4.3 now, its pattern renewed 287 times, the ground it holds up 302 m at its root mean square and from −1,350 to 890 m; Mars's of degree 2.5, 396 m and −930 to 1,070 m; the Moon's, whose mantle reaches its centre, of degree 1, 641 m and about a kilometre each way; Mercury's of degree 14, 658 m before its thick lid holds it flatter and 144 m after, about half a kilometre each way. On the maps Venus's rises stand at its upwellings and a lowland 2 km deep lies over a downwelling, two of its plumes joined by a ridge 14,800 km long, arching north between them where the great circle is 9,100 km, its rifts along it, the longest some 1,900 km, and the third plume parted from both by downwellings; Mars's rise straddles its dichotomy's boundary at the western end of its highlands; Mercury and the Moon roll gently over their mantles' cells.
- **F174 The melt is one budget, and a rise is a pile as well as a dome.** A rocky body's melt erupted by the share its crust's buoyancy left (F125, an eighth at most), and the rest, seven parts in eight or more, was lost: on Mars 1.1 × 10⁹ km³ of its 1.25 × 10⁹, as were the 4 × 10⁷ km³ its full edifices could not lift (F164). Tharsis's long-wavelength gravity and Mars's shape are explained by a load of some 3 × 10⁸ km³ of magma (Phillips et al. 2001), most of it in place before about 3.7 Ga (Hynek et al. 2011), where the model's rise was a dome its plume held up and no volume of its own. The melt that stays below now intrudes the crust beneath where it rose from, as the vents' sources are without the ground the eruptions find their way through: the provinces the ambient mantle melts in by the share of the melt it makes, and the plumes' heads the rest (F165), reckoned as the eruptions' budget is, over the same times, so that what erupts and what stays below make the melt; and the magma a full edifice could not lift intrudes beneath its rise. At each epoch the loop reads the lid (F157) the crust thickens by what intruded since, the ground rises as much and the intrusions' weight bends the lid as a load of its own, so that the ground stands as the crust's buoyancy and the lid's strength hold it, Airy's isostasy at the longest wavelengths; the edifices on it sink with the ground, their lava filling only the sags their own weight makes (F166). The finish sees the intrusions at the epochs the loop laid them, as it sees the ground's sinking (F162). On Mars 1.16 × 10⁹ km³ intrudes, 3.2 × 10⁸ km³ of it beneath its rise, as much as Tharsis's load, the most in its first billion years; the rise's flanks stand some 2 km higher, at some 7 km, 22 km intruding beneath its centre against 4 km where the least does, the old basin it stands over raised some 2.5 km to 2 to 3 km and its largest shield 27.3 km on the new ground. Venus, whose melt after its overturn is small, changes little; the Moon and Mercury, whose crusts held most of their melt down, the Moon's light crust early and Mercury's shrinking lid after its first billion and a half years, swell over their provinces by a kilometre or two, the Moon's raising the lowland its province lies in, where its maria do not gather (audit M33). The intrusions raise the ground over a province about evenly, so that the basin keeps its depth below its rim; only lava fills a hollow, and the vents favoured low ground against the body's median (F36), so that a hollow on a raised rise drew few of the floods where Tharsis's lavas erupted high on theirs (F176). The rise's dome still stands 0.28 of the relief its crust holds, the share Tharsis's whole height was fitted to before it had a pile (a calibration on one body to revisit: the dome from its plume head's buoyancy).
- **F175 A deposit's level has no steps.** A crater's ejecta level the old ground against the mean of the sector of its ring a point lies in (F169), sixteen rings out to the ejecta's edge, each split into as many as 64 sectors about as long as the deposit levels: each point took its own sector's mean, so that beneath a thick deposit, where the burial takes nearly all the relief, every sector stood as a flat patch at its own level, stepping along the straight radial edges between sectors and the arcs between rings. The level is now the sectors' means interpolated round each ring between the sectors' middles and across the rings between theirs, so that it changes smoothly round and away from the crater, the relief longer than a sector still draped rather than buried. On a plain tilted 3 m per km a crater 600 km across leaves the ground beneath its thick deposit near the rim stepping no more than 13 m between points 3 to 4 km apart round it, where the sectors' edges stood as steps of 28 m; beneath the deposits of Mars's and the Moon's basins, on relief kilometres high, the terraces are gone.
