- **F219 The craters too small to draw shed on their walls.** Only the craters the finish draws shed on their walls (F98, F218), and it draws them from two cells of its grid, 5.3 km on the Moon and 7.5 km on Mercury at the standard resolution, smaller at the high: the steep ground depended on the quality, Mercury's drawn walls 1.08 percent of its surface at the standard resolution and 1.24 at the high. Now the craters smaller than the finish draws shed as well, from 10 m (FINE_WALLS_SMALLEST) and the atmosphere's cut-off up, in 24 size bins a decade (`fine_walls`). They come by the body's production today, the gardening's equivalent time their clock, and survive since the surface formed as the finish's own population does: no longer than the retention age, at most at the equilibrium the craters reach (EMPIRICAL_SATURATION_FRACTION), the survivors the younger where they near it, those that formed e ago surviving as e^(-p e / n_eq) for p craters a Gyr and n_eq at equilibrium per km². Each sheds over the area its walls stand steep as its profile stands after the gardening since it formed, the diffusivity times that time as the drawn craters degrade, a complex crater's along its terraces, the mean of four draws of them (`shed_area`), and their walls take the ground the drawn walls and slopes leave as 1 - e^-cover does. A fresh lunar crater of 1 km sheds over 0.73 of its bowl's area, softened by a blur of 0.3 radii 0.21. At a 2,048-pixel map they shed on 0.56 percent of the Moon's and of Mercury's surface, 0.96 of Callisto's and 0.69 of Ganymede's, most of it from the craters of 1 km up to those drawn, little from those under 100 m. They brighten the mean albedo by 0.11 percent on the Moon, 0.43 on Callisto and 0.14 on Ganymede, no pixel of the Moon's map by more than one grey level, and add some 1.4 s to a finish. Mercury's walls, drawn and too small to draw, cover 1.64 percent of its surface at the standard resolution and 1.55 at the high, where the drawn walls alone differed by 15 percent: the drawn craters from 3.7 to 7.5 km shed on 0.16 percent where the statistical walls give them 0.25, a difference not traced (one candidate: later ejecta bury the drawn walls, which the statistics leave out). What Mercury's map still lacks against the Moon's are drawn simple craters: the Moon's run from 5.3 to 16.5 km, and Mercury's end at its transition, 7.2 km, under the size the finish draws (audit M38).
- **F220 A sparse cluster digs as deep as a dense one.** F212 made a ray's secondaries freshen the ground by the share of what they throw out from beneath the weathered skin, 1 - h/d, reading their depth from the jet's excavated area: h/d was that area against the area at the ray's end, where half the ground is excavated (Elliott et al. 2018's P = 0.5), to -SECONDARY_SIZE_FALL / RAY_DENSITY_SLOPE, and nothing where the jet excavated under half the ground. The user saw the regression it made: the fine streaks that milestones 59 to 61 had made of the rays' clusters were gone, leaving smooth spokes near the craters (2026-10-29). Clusters are drawn where the jets excavate from 0.005 to RAY_CLUSTER_CAP of the ground, so the sparse ones, under half, were all multiplied by nothing, and the rays were worked out only as far as the strongest jet passed a half. But a secondary's depth goes as its size, and that as the speed its fragment left at, not as how many fragments land about it: it falls with distance as the largest secondary does (SECONDARY_SIZE_FALL), a faster jet carrying the same fragments farther by its speed squared. Now h/d = (r / end)^SECONDARY_SIZE_FALL with the end the median ray length (`ray_length`) times the jet's speed squared, the same as F212's for the median jet; a thicker skin ends it sooner by its share of the area to 1 / RAY_DENSITY_SLOPE, as before. On the Moon at 4,096 pixels the rays brighten 6.2 percent of the surface past a tenth of the fresh contrast, 4.4 under F212, the light azimuths' clusters filling the ground between the jets. An end at twice the median ray length, the Moon's longest rays reaching some twice its fit (Tycho's 1,500 to 2,000 km against 770), was tried and not kept: the far rays' clusters came back but the brightest half percent of the surface rose to 0.228 against the reference's 0.192.
- **F221 The fragments dig secondaries where they land apart, and the blanket carries the ground it lands on.** Young craters stood too bright at their rims: from 1.4 to 2.8 crater radii the median of ten model craters younger than 0.35 Gyr and larger than 30 km stood at 2.08 times the ground 10.6 to 13 radii out, where the reference's young craters stand at 1.10 to 1.66 (Tycho 1.22, Copernicus 1.46, Aristarchus 1.43, Kepler 1.66, Giordano Bruno 1.38, Jackson 1.10, Ohm 1.15, King 1.37) and farther out the model matched them (2.8 to 4.7 radii 1.43 against about 1.30, 4.7 to 7.1 radii 1.18 against 1.18, 7.1 to 10.6 radii 1.02 against 1.07). The jets' secondaries were drawn from the rim out, and the jets excavate the ground many times over near their crater, so a solid, fully fresh ray filled the continuous blanket. A fragment digs a secondary only where it lands apart from the others: now the rays freshen only the ground the continuous deposit leaves, by the share its cover does not take. And the deposit is fresh by the share of it that is the crater's own ejecta: ballistic sedimentation takes up the ground it lands on (Oberbeck 1975), from none at EJECTA_PURE_RADII to all of it at the continuous edge, as F216 mixes the deep bedrock, the ground taken up keeping its own maturity (a stated choice: what the deposit reworks reaches below the skin as well). The near ring falls to 1.70 (1.92 with the deposit's mixing alone), the rings beyond stay at 1.33, 1.15 and 1.02, and the brightest half percent of the Moon's surface is 0.202 against the reference's 0.192 (0.207 under F212). The solid spokes are gone: a young crater stands in a soft bright halo from which fine streaks run out, as Copernicus and Aristarchus do. Mercury's rays brighten 1.0 percent of its surface past a tenth of the fresh contrast, 0.9 before, in softer systems of finer streaks. Still open: the near ring at 1.70 against the reference's median of 1.37; and the maria's bright excess, which is not the rays but the rims of craters standing in and about the lava (audit M36).
- **F223 The tesserae stand on the lid's flexural length, not two cells.** Venus's tesserae were the highest 8 percent of the old crust's height smoothed by twenty passes of the one-cell kernel, some two cells, 80 km at the standard quality and 170 km at the preview, so that their outlines changed with the quality (audit W3). The relief a lid holds up on its own strength, shorter than its flexural parameter, is not the plateaus of crust standing on their roots (F184) that the tesserae are. The height is now smoothed by a Gaussian as wide as the flexural parameter of the elastic lid the overturn broke, down to ELASTIC_ISOTHERM_ROCK: on Venus 19.3 km thick before its last overturn, its parameter 38 km, four passes on the standard grid where there were twenty. The tesserae cover 7.8 percent as before; 0.14 percent of the surface changed between tessera and plains, and the history after the event took another course in places, a volcano standing elsewhere.
- **F224 A young deposit's edge on old ground ramps over the craters that make its roughness.** The unresolved craters' roughness (F54) followed each cell's surface age smoothed by twelve passes of the one-cell kernel, about two cells whatever the cell (audit W2). Its largest scale is the craters of the anchor diameter, the smallest the finish draws, and those about the edge of a young deposit lie partly on each side of it, so the strength now blends over the anchor crater's radius, a spread of half its diameter. On the Moon it moved the heights by 4 m at the root mean square.
- **F225 A lid yielding in compression opens no lane.** The heat a lid carries above the floor renewed its lanes over the time it lasted, whatever the body's change of radius did to the lid (F44, F140): Mimas's young ocean, 13 mW/m² over 33 Myr, renewed a tenth of it in lanes, one of them a hexagon, where Lainey et al. (2024) find no surface expression; its ocean melts, and the body shrinks by 3.5 km, its lid taking up 4.8 percent shortening on its thrusts (F147). A lane is a rift, which the lid breaks in tension, and a lid yielding in compression breaks in none: the heat now renews no lane over the times the lid's strain records shortening, the threshold the lid's own compressive strength, Anderson's thrusts at the faulted layer's depth. The lid's strain reads its inputs from a struct a derivation can fill (`strain::LidInputs`), so the renewal's derivation reads it with every node declared. Mimas's lanes fall to 5.5 percent, 20 of them, the hexagon gone; the rest open in the first part of its ocean's growth, while its shrinking stands within what the lid holds elastically. A stricter line at any compression would have taken Ganymede's lanes too: its lid stands some 5 MPa in elastic compression through its episode, after the shrinking that began it, and its lanes stand at 69 percent as before. Enceladus's, Europa's and Miranda's are unchanged.
- **F226 Every set of tilt blocks tilts to lock-up.** The lanes' fine set, the tilted fault blocks on the grooves' necks, stood at 0.4 of the grooves' relief, where a corona's blocks tilt until their faults lock, from 50 to 30 degrees (F133), two laws for one kind of block (audit T6). Collins et al. (1998) read Uruk Sulcus's blocks as stretched at least 51 to 58 percent, past the 53 percent at which they lock, and the grooves themselves as necks of the brittle layer: so the fine set now tilts to lock-up as a corona's blocks do (`corona::lockup_relief`), its relief from crest to trough the block's width times the sine of the tilt, about 200 m on Ganymede's 1.2 km blocks where it was 100, and the grooves keep their necks' share of their spacing, which Enceladus's ridged plains check. The fine set draws only where it spans several cells, below the grid at 4,096 pixels on Ganymede.
- **F227 A shell renews itself by degrees.** A shell over an ocean thinner than 0.025 of its body's radius renewed itself by chaos and cracking, its craters kept 0.1 Gyr where its tides passed 10 mW/m², and a thicker one in lanes, its craters kept for its whole age: a step in each (audit S4, S6), Europa's tides at 0.6 of its own giving lanes over 74 percent of it and at 0.8 chaos over 24 and none. The shell is now thin by degrees about the same share, wholly so at 0.8 of it and not at all at 1.2 (THIN_SHELL_WIDTH, a stated width, Europa's 0.018 and Ganymede's 0.033 outside it): the chaos takes its share by the degree, the lanes theirs by what is left; the whole surface renews within RETENTION_ICE_OCEAN_GYR over the degree of its renewal, the thin degree times the tides' share of ICE_RESURFACING_TIDAL_FLUX's 10 mW/m², at most the body's age; the loop keeps its craters for that retention; and the regolith takes the clean ice the renewal lays by its degree, the bedrock clean ice where it renews more than half over. Along Europa's tides from 0.3 to 1.5 of its own, its shell 0.034 to 0.013 of its radius, chaos comes in from 0.001 to 0.3, lanes rise to 0.37 where the shell is half thin and go, and the retention falls from 4.5 to 0.1 Gyr, nowhere by more than threefold between neighbours (`sweeps::a_shell_renews_itself_by_degrees_between_europa_and_ganymede`). Every preset is as it was.
- **F228 The plumes begin where the ocean reaches the surface.** The focusing's episodes, the plumes and their fallout came on where the polar thinning passed 0.3 of the shell (audit S5), a share and not an event. The event is the ocean's water reaching the surface: the water a crack from the ocean holds stands below the surface by the thinned shell times one less the ice's density over the water's, and the tides open cracks from above to the depth their stress at the thinned shell bears against the ice's weight (Crawford and Stevenson 1988), so that where the second reaches the first ocean water erupts (`tectonics.crack_reach`): Enceladus 1.74, its shell 5.4 km at the pole, the water 433 m down and the tides' cracks 753 m; Mimas 0.86; Europa 0.04, its tides' cracks 69 m against water 1.8 km down. The episodes and the plumes begin past one, the fallout growing over CRACK_REACH_WIDTH more of it (0.5, a stated width); the zone their terrain renews keeps its profile. Along Enceladus's tides from 0.2 of its own, the reach passes one at 0.85 of them, where the old cut had begun at half, and the fallout grows from a quarter to all of it by 0.95 (`sweeps::plumes_begin_where_the_ocean_reaches_the_surface`). Every preset is as it was.
- **F229 A crater's target is ice by degrees.** A crater on a bedrock `is_ice` reads had a central pit from 8.5 transitions and a dome in it from 20, its transition 0.15 of rock's, its target cold ice's strength and the largest crater it holds as wide as its radius; on any other a peak ring, a multi-ring basin, rock's transition and strength, and a hemisphere (audit S9): a crust of rock and ice took one or the other whole. A target is now ice by its bedrock's share of ice by volume, rock below 0.3 and ice above 0.5, by degrees between (TARGET_ICE_SHARE, a stated width about where the rock grains lock into a framework, Durham et al. 1992; `cratering.target_ice`): a body's dirty ice is its crust's rock and ice (F126), clean ice a trace of rock. The transition's ice factor, the strength and the largest crater go between rock's and ice's geometrically by the degree; a crater past the peak-ring onset has a pit by the degree and its rings by the rest, a basin its outer ring, sculpture and ejecta's reach by the rest, and each is named by the larger share. Along Callisto made denser from 1,834 to 3,434 kg/m³, the degree falls from one where its crust is half rock to 0.05 at 0.67 rock, and to none where its class turns to rock at 0.69; the transition with gravity taken out rises nowhere more than 1.25 times between neighbours 50 kg/m³ apart (6.7 times at the old switch), the largest crater from the radius to π times it, and a crater at one and a half times the onset loses its pit, 0.22 of it across, as its peak ring rises to 0.3 of its depth (`sweeps::craters_go_from_ice_to_rock_by_degrees`). Every icy crust is ice above the width, Ceres's the least at 0.62; every preset is as it was.
- **F230 The episodic lid by its overturns.** A dry rocky lid was episodic where its mantle had broken it within the last half of the body's life (F121), and with the name came the episodic lid's melt efficiency, five times the stagnant lid's, its narrower rises, its domes, its tesserae, its resurfacing and the craters it keeps (audit S1): a Venus at 0.85 of its radius, its lid broken fifteen times and last 2.65 Gyr ago, was stagnant and kept a 3.5 Gyr surface, and at 0.9 it was episodic, its magma 5.7 times as much. What an overturn does now follows the overturns: a lid that has broken, however long ago, is resurfaced at its last overturn, keeps its tesserae and the craters since, and its retention is at most the stagnant lid's. The episodic lid's constants go by its degree (`interior.episodic_degree`), 1 while its last overturn lies within 0.4 of the body's age and none past 0.6 (EPISODIC_RECENT_SHARE, a stated width about F121's cut): the melt efficiency geometrically from the stagnant lid's 0.01 to 0.05, the rises' width from 1.5 to 0.4 of the radius, and the domes. Along Venus's radius from 0.6 of its own, the lid breaks from 0.7, last 4.0 Gyr ago, the event bringing its tesserae; as the last overturn nears the present, between 0.85 and 0.925 of the radius, the magma grows by at most twice between neighbours 0.025 of the radius apart, the domes from 1 to 130 and the rises narrow from 1.49 to 0.40 of the radius (`sweeps::a_lid_turns_episodic_by_degrees`). Venus is at degree one and every preset is as it was. The steps left are the overturns' own: the first, where the mantle first passes the lid's friction, and the last's time, which moves by an interval as another comes in.
- **F231 An ice shell afloat by degrees.** An ice shell over rock built cryodomes and cryolava floods and its deep ice was its crust's mixture, while one over an ocean built neither and its deep ice was the frozen ocean's clean ice, by the regime's name (audit S1): Pluto made 1.025 times larger, its shell 441 km against an ice layer of 433, kept its 16 domes, and at 1.05, its ocean 0.04 of its layer, it lost them all and its deep ice turned clean. A shell now floats on its ocean by degrees (`interior.ocean_degree`), by the ocean's share of the ice layer: not at all where the shell reaches the rock, wholly from 0.3 of it (OCEAN_SHARE_WIDTH, a stated width; Triton's ocean, the least of the ocean bodies', is 0.38 of its layer). The cryodomes and the cryolava floods take the ice magma budget as far as it does not, and the deep ice, what deep craters excavate and the ice that flows as a crater relaxes, goes from the crust's mixture to the clean ice by the degree. Along Pluto's radius the domes go 16, 15, 13, 9, 6, 3, 1 and none from 1.025 to 1.2 of it, the deep ice cleaning with them (`sweeps::a_shell_comes_afloat_by_degrees`). Every preset is as it was, the ocean bodies at degree one and the frozen ones at none. The step left is the shell's own: Miranda's tides made four to eight times as strong run away, its shell going from 236 to 38 km and its ocean from none to 0.74 of its layer. Not taken, the direction F230 left: the domes built only in the times the body had no ocean, and the deep ice clean by the share of the shell that froze from the ocean. The domes' budget is the present rate over the whole life, so a gate on the history alone took Pluto from 16 domes to about 1 and gave Mimas, dry for 97 percent of its history, about 1,100; and the top 15 to 60 km of every ocean body's shell never melted in its history, so the deep ice, 0.5 km down, would have turned dirty on every one of them.
- **F232 An ocean freezes on its floor as well.** The lid's strain (F147) took an ocean as freezing from its top alone, as ice I, which expands, the high-pressure ices only below ice I's limit under a shell that thick; so every icy body whose shell thickened stretched by nine percent of the water that froze, and Callisto, Ganymede and Titan, their shells thickening from some 20 to 90 km, opened some 1,300 long graben each, 0.3 to 0.4 percent of their areas (Callisto's 3,198 graben, 308,000 km of them, with their blocks), where Callisto shows none (the user's question). An ocean stands at the melting point of the ice at its shell's base, and below it its water freezes as the high-pressure ices where its pressure passes their melting point at that temperature (the melting curves of ices Ih, III, V and VI, IAPWS R14-08, 2011): the oceans of the large moons lie between ice I and the high-pressure ices, which grow from below as they cool and shrink the body as the shell's freezing grows it (Squyres 1980: Callisto's want of grooves; Mueller and McKinnon 1988). Callisto's ocean floor rises from 477 to 352 km as its shell thickens from 21 to 96 km, and its lid stretches by 0.02 percent where it stretched by 0.38; Ganymede's and Titan's not at all. Pluto's and Charon's oceans reach the rock until they freeze through, and their lids stretch as before, Pluto's by 2.1 percent (2.4), its floor freezing at the end; Triton's by 1.6 (1.9). Ice I's limit is the IAPWS triple point, 208.566 MPa at 251.165 K (Wagner, Saul and Pruss's 209.9 MPa before), so that the two fronts meet where the shell's base reaches it (`strain::tests::an_ocean_freezes_on_its_floor_as_the_high_pressure_ices`).
- **F233 A lid's faults grow as it stretches.** The lid's extension was spent on faults drawn one at a time, their lengths from the power law whose every decade takes an equal share, their depth drawn apart from their length, none growing or linking: Pluto's lid broke into some 3,500 faults 66 km long at the median and Triton's 3,600, each body's whole surface a scatter of short graben, with F152's blocks inside the wide ones (the user's question). A lid's fault now reaches down as far as its length allows, a fault surface running about twice as long as it is high (Nicol et al. 1996: 2.15), until it cuts the lid's brittle layer, its graben as wide as its faults allow (LID_FAULT_ASPECT), and the faults grow as the rock lid's do (F179): the extension is spent in increments of its whole as the lid took it up, the faults standing taking up what falls within their shadows by lengthening, deepening as they lengthen until they cut the layer, and linking where they meet one at least half as wide, before new ones open outside every shadow; what an increment's faults take up beyond it the next makes up, so that a lid stretched little opens few faults. A fault's time is its last growth, as a rock lid's graben's (F179). Pluto's lid breaks into some 2,000 faults, its longest tenth averaging 196 km long and 2.8 km deep across the whole layer, its shortest 0.8 km deep (`strain::tests::a_lid_s_faults_grow_deeper_with_their_length`); Charon's into some 350, its chasmata up to 44 km wide, the long ones 4.8 km deep at the median; Callisto's into a few dozen from its 0.02 percent. Known limitations: a lid stretched alike every way fills its whole surface at its shadows' spacing, where Pluto's faults are regional, gathered by Sputnik Planitia's load and the reorientation it drove (Keane et al. 2016), a field the icy lids lack (audit: the icy lids' faults on the same field), and its oldest are eroded or buried (Bierson et al. 2020), with Phase 5's volatiles; Ceres's crust froze from a muddy ocean, at least 30 percent salts and clathrates (Fu et al. 2017), where its lid stretches by the water a pure ocean would freeze; Mimas refreezes in its last 10 Myr (M22).
- **F234 Insolation for any rotation state.** The model knew the star's flux at the body's distance and, in the ice stage, a latitude's mean as S cos(lat) / pi, the equinox's day on a circular orbit with no tilt: no seasons, no polar day, a moon's sky as if it had no planet, Mercury's longitudes alike. The year's sunlight at any point is now one integral, the star's flux on the ground averaged over its path across the sky (`insolation::Sky`). A body that spins many times an orbit sees the star cross every longitude alike, so its sunlight is the day's mean at each latitude (Berger 1978) taken through the seasons its obliquity makes, over (1 - e^2)^(1/2) for its eccentricity whatever the season of perihelion (Ward 1974); a body whose spin is in an exact low-order resonance with its orbit (`rotation.resonance`, Goldreich and Peale 1966) sees the same sky every few orbits, and its sunlight is the time integral over them, keeping the longitudes the resonance fixes. A moon's sky is its planet's: its year and eccentricity its planet's (`orbit.year`, `orbit.star_eccentricity`), its tilt to its planet's orbit (`rotation.obliquity`, now that tilt for every body, zero only when locked to its star), and an axis that precesses faster than the loop's steps averages its tilts over the cone (`rotation.obliquity_swing`: Triton's orbit, 157° to Neptune's equator, gives its axis a tilt to the Sun between 5° and 51°). Checked against a brute-force integration with the day resolved (`insolation::tests`): the Earth's equator takes 415.9 W/m² of the year and its poles 172.4; Mercury's hot longitudes 2.53 times its warm ones; a body locked to its star the full flux at its substellar point and half 60° from it; past 54° of tilt the poles take more than the equator (Ward 1974), as Pluto's (119.6°) and Miranda's (97.8°) do; every body a quarter of its flux on average. The presets take their tilts to their stars' orbits (the Galilean moons Jupiter's 3.13°, Saturn's moons 26.73°, Miranda Uranus's 97.77°, Charon Pluto's 119.6°) and Mars's and Saturn's seasons at perihelion. Nothing reads the field yet: the energy balance is next. Known limitation: a moon's eclipses by its planet, which take some of a sub-planet hemisphere's light; Mercury's 3:2 and Venus's slow retrograde spin are still read from their presets' pinned periods, their spins from tides with 5A.
- **F235 The energy balance.** The model knew one temperature per body, the grey greenhouse's zero-dimensional mean (F21), and the ice stage a latitude's from S cos(lat) / pi. Every cell of a coarse cubesphere (32 cells a face, 2.8°) now balances the year's sunlight it absorbs (F234) and the body's own heat flowing out through it against the heat it radiates to space and the heat its atmosphere carries to and from its neighbours, Q (1 - a) + q - OLR(T) + D div grad T = 0 (North 1975, `climate::EnergyBalance`): OLR the grey atmosphere's sigma T^4 / (1 + 3 tau / 4) through its gases' optical depth at the cell's temperature, D the transport its mass and rotation allow (Williams and Kasting 1997, F33), zero on an airless body, whose every cell balances alone. Water vapour's optical depth now saturates as its bands fill, tau = 0.166 ln(1 + u / 0.103 kg/m²), keeping Earth's zero-dimensional 291.6 K: the square root it replaces made the outgoing radiation rise by only 0.31 W/m² per kelvin, where Earth's zonal means show 2.1 (North 1975), and in the balance ran the tropics away; Mars's zero-dimensional mean moves from 214.0 to 214.8 K. Under a scattering atmosphere the albedo rises as the sun's year-mean height falls, 0.32 per unit of its cosine (the fit of North and Coakley 1979's zonal albedo, 0.30 + 0.09 P2), by the atmosphere's column's share of Earth's; where water is the working liquid, ice and snow (albedo 0.62) cover the ground the year leaves between 0 and -10 °C (North 1975). Solved by Newton's method from the zero-dimensional mean, the warm branch, its systems by conjugate gradients. An Earth-like at 1 AU around a G2V star stands at 287.8 K (Earth 288), 301 within 10° of its equator and 257 beyond 80° (`climate::tests`); Mars 215.7 (210), its tropics 231 and poles 184; Venus 755 (737); Titan 91.1 (93.7). The year's mean temperature is exported (`temperature.exr`, K) and summarised in the sidecar. Known limitations: an airless body's mean is the radiative equilibrium with the year's mean sunlight, warmer than the mean of its days and nights (the Moon's equator 284 K, where its noons stand near 390 K and its nights near 100 K, Vasavada et al. 2012), until the day's cycle and the regolith's thermal inertia, the next milestone; the transport's scaling with rotation is Earth's, so Titan's poles stand 0.1 K under its equator where Cassini found 3 K (Jennings et al. 2016) and Venus's 6.5 K, until a slowly turning atmosphere's single cell comes with the wind field (5C); the albedo of an atmosphere is still the pressure class's guess (F52).
- **F236 The day and the year.** An airless body's mean was the radiative balance with the year's mean sunlight (F235), warmer than its days and nights give: 284 K at the Moon's equator, where its noons stand near 390 K and its nights near 95 K. Every place now runs a column of ground through the star's path (`climate::EnergyBalance::cycle`): its regolith's thermal inertia (`surface.thermal_inertia`), its grains touching in vacuum as the Moon's, 55 (Hayne et al. 2017), with the gas in its pores conducting at the surface's pressure (Presley and Christensen 1997) through grains of fine sand, so that Mars's comes to some 185 and a 1-bar Earth's to some 470, its heat capacity by its share of ice; the air's heat capacity on its surface; the heat the year's balance found its atmosphere bringing it, held steady. A body that spins many times a year runs through the year on the day's mean sunlight until its seasons repeat, the deep ground settled at the surface's mean, then through a day at each of 24 seasons from the ground as the season left it; a body in a spin-orbit resonance runs through the orbits it repeats in. Checked (`climate::tests`): the Moon's equator 98 to 381 K, its mean 218 (Diviner's noons near 390 K and nights near 95, Vasavada et al. 2012); Mercury's hot longitudes 696 K at perihelion's noon and its warm ones 565 K at aphelion's, its nights 89 K (some 700, 570 and 100, Vasavada, Paige and Wood 1999); Mars's equator 199 to 273 K; Europa's 79 to 124 K (Galileo's 86 to 132, Rathbun et al. 2010); Ceres's up to 228 K. Venus and Titan keep their steady means; Titan's poles swing 89 to 94 K through its seasons. The exported temperature is the year's mean through its days and seasons, the sidecar giving the coldest and the warmest. Known limitations: the Earth-like's seasons swing as land's do until its ocean holds them (5B), its summer poles at 295 K and its mean 285.8 K, 2 K under its steady balance and within the gate's 5 K; the regolith's grains are a stated fine sand until 5D's dust and sand set them; the ground's relief, its slopes' sunlight and its craters' shadows, waits on the downscaling.
- **F237 The climate brought down to the ground.** The climate's temperature stood on its coarse grid (F235, F236), read bilinearly onto the surface's: a summit as warm as the plain under it, a crater's sunward wall as its shaded one. Each place now takes it by its own height and slope (`climate::Downscale`, section 4.12). The ground sheds its heat to space and to the air; the share it sheds by radiation, 4 sigma T^3 against the air's exchange rho c_p C_H U (the bulk coefficient over land, Garratt 1992, and a stated 5 m/s wind until 5C's), follows its own sunlight and greenhouse, the rest the air over it: all of it on an airless body, 0.96 on Mars, 0.38 on an Earth-like, almost none under Titan's or Venus's air. A slope takes the sunlight of the place its normal faces, since a fast spinner's year of sunlight on a slope is flat ground's at the latitude the slope faces, and moves toward that place's temperature by the radiation's share; a height cools by the air's share of the lapse rate and the radiation's share of the thinner greenhouse above it, its dry gases thinning with the pressure and its water vapour with its own scale height, T^2 / (L/R_v lapse), never faster than the lapse rate. A sea's surface is flat at its own level. Checked (`climate::tests`): ground at the Moon's 60° north tilted 20° toward the equator stands at 40° north's temperature; an Earth-like's 3 km summit some 15 K under its sea level, Venus's 11 km summit near 650 K as Maxwell Montes stands (Pettengill et al. 1992), Mars's 20 km summit 7 K under its datum. Water vapour's saturating optical depth (F235) thins slowly with height, 2.3 K/km by itself, so the air's convection carries most of an Earth-like's cooling. Known limitations: no shadows yet, a crater floor that never sees the sun standing at its own slope's sunlight, the permanent shadows at the Moon's and Mercury's poles waiting on the horizons (5B); one wind speed until 5C.
- **F238 The spin's history from its present state.** The present rotation was a period set or derived, `rotation.mode` an input that locked a body whose locking time from its set period was shorter than its age, and Mercury's and Venus's presets pinned it free at their observed periods. The present period is now the input, measured, by default its orbit's where its primary's tide would have locked it within its age (the user's direction); `rotation.mode` describes it (locked, resonant, free), and the history leading to it is derived (`spin::SpinInputs`): the tides of constant Q its primary and its largest satellite raise (Murray and Dermott 1999), the satellite's orbit taking up what the spin loses (`satellite.mass`, `satellite.semi_major_axis`). A spin the tides hold, locked to its primary, in a resonance with its orbit or locked to its satellite, was brought there from the spin it was born with, 0.35 of its breakup rate (Kokubo and Ida 2007; Kokubo and Genda 2010); a free spin is run back from today until the body formed or its satellite stood at its Roche limit. An ocean world's Q over its history is 34, its oceans dissipating today some three times their long average (Bills and Ray 1999). Earth's day runs back to 22.4 h 620 Myr ago (21.9 h in the tidal rhythmites, Williams 2000), 19 h 2 Gyr ago and 4.9 h at its birth, the Moon at its Roche limit 7 Myr after Earth formed; Mercury's 3:2 was reached 0.81 Gyr after it formed from a 4 h spin; Pluto has been locked to Charon since 1 Myr; the moons were locked within a few Myr; Venus's slow retrograde spin runs back to 30 h retrograde, its Sun's tide too weak to have locked it. The plausibility rules flag a present state the history cannot reach: a free spin its primary's tide would have locked within the body's age (noted, not flagged, under an atmosphere of a bar or more, whose thermal tides can hold it, Leconte et al. 2015), a held spin its tides could not bring there within its age, a spin its satellite's tide would have locked to the satellite's orbit (an Earth with a twin at 30 Earth radii within 0.2 Myr). No preset pins its rotation. Known limitations: the atmosphere's thermal tides are not computed (their strength wants the atmosphere's heating profile, 5G); a satellite's own Q and composition are not inputs (its density a rocky moon's); the history does not yet feed the despinning's stress (audit M27).
- **F239 The frost's seasons.** No gas condensed on the ground: Mars's winter poles fell to 80 K, Pluto's to 20 K, under an atmosphere that kept its pressure through the year. The atmosphere's main condensable gas, the species with the largest partial pressure below its triple point among those whose frost the model draws (carbon dioxide on Mars, nitrogen on Pluto and Triton, methane where it leads), now condenses where the ground would cool below its frost point at that partial pressure, and the frost holds the ground at the point while it lies, its latent heat balancing what the ground radiates (Leighton and Murray 1966), sublimes as the season warms it and reflects at its own albedo, its colour's mean (CO2 0.69, N2 0.80). The surface layer's balance through each step, with the column below solved under a surface held at the point, is the frost it takes or gives (`climate::EnergyBalance::frosty_cycles`). Every latitude, or every place of a resonant body, runs through the year in step and draws on one atmosphere: the frost is the gas's own, so that the pressure, and the frost point with it, falls as the caps grow and rises as they sublime, each step's point the one at which the frost the places take leaves the gas at the partial pressure whose point it is. The gas's whole mass is the atmosphere's and the frost's that comes and goes, in the share of the body's pressure the air keeps through the year: all of it where the frost leaves the air its pressure, so that the air's year-mean is the body's, and little where the frost's own balance holds the air below it, as no store of gas would raise it; frost that lies all year and grows is a cap's growth, counted (`cap_growth_kg_m2_yr`) and given back in the share the air keeps, so that a closed atmosphere's caps stay. Mars's carbon dioxide frosts at 145.7 K and holds its winter poles at 144.3 to 145.7 K; its seasonal caps reach some 50° of latitude in their winters (James et al. 1992), the northern laying 1,099 kg/m² at the pole by the end of its winter, the snapshot's season (MOLA's 1 to 1.5 m of frost, Smith et al. 2001), the caps holding on average 27 percent of the gas; its pressure swings from 502 to 696 Pa, 28 percent (the Viking landers' some 25 to 30, Hess et al. 1980); its mean moves from 213.6 to 211.7 K. Frost lies 0.86 of the year at its south pole, and no carbon dioxide stays the summer: the residual cap's albedo, some 0.8 against the seasonal frost's 0.69 (Paige and Ingersoll 1985), comes with the caps. Pluto's and Triton's nitrogen lays thin seasonal caps of their own gas, and the frost's balance holds their air far below its measured pressure, Pluto's at most 0.015 Pa (1 Pa, Stern et al. 2015) and Triton's 0.20 (1.4, Tyler et al. 1989): the frost radiates as a black body where nitrogen ice's emissivity is some 0.6 (Stansberry et al. 1996), and their reservoirs of nitrogen ice wait on the caps. A Mars locked to its star at 3 AU lays its gas on its night side and poles, its pressure falling to 37 to 57 Pa, at 6 AU all of it, to its other gases' 26 Pa. The Earth-like's carbon dioxide frost point, 129 K, and Titan's methane's, 87.4 K, are never reached; Venus's gases stand past their triple points. The frost at the snapshot's season is brought to the surface's grid as its temperature is, a slope taking the frost of the place its slope faces (`climate::Downscale::frost`), and hides the ground's colour as 1 - exp(-m / 1 kg/m²), about a millimetre (FROST_COVER_MASS); exported as `frost.exr` (kg/m²) with the sidecar's frost mean and cover, the pressure's range and the caps' growth. Checked (`climate::tests`): Mars's poles at the point, its caps' reach, its north pole's frost and its pressure's swing; no frost where the ground stays above its point; Pluto's frost within its gas; an equator-facing slope at Mars's 60° north holding a small part of the flat ground's frost. Known limitations: the cycle's transport is the year's steady one (F236), and frosted ground takes the heat the air brings it but gives none, ground its frost holds below its steady temperature drawing heat rather than shedding it (a stated guard: a locked body's substellar ground, frosted at aphelion, kept exporting the steady heat at 0 K); the frost does not yet see a height's thinner air and its lower point; nitrogen frost's emissivity; the frost's albedo is its colour's, without its grains, dust or age (the caps); Pluto's transport, by Williams and Kasting's scaling with rotation, answers a local temperature 30 times as strongly as its radiation, which the steady transport does not show (F235).
- **F240 The cold traps.** The model knew where frost lies through the year (F239), not where an ice of the inventory would last: Mars's water and carbon dioxide, Pluto's and Triton's nitrogen and methane, an Earth-like's water. Each ice of the inventory the model draws (`climate::Ice`: carbon dioxide's, nitrogen's and methane's in their frosts' colours, water's clean ice, except where the water is an icy body's own shell) now runs, at every cell of the climate's grid, a column of its own through the year (`climate::EnergyBalance::traps`): at its height's pressure, the datum's thinned by exp(-h/H), under the frost's seasons, beside the bare ground about it, whose air exchanges heat with the ice as F237's air does with a slope, so that a thick atmosphere holds the ice near its own temperature and a thin one leaves it its own sunlight. The frost's own gas's ice lies all year from an unlimited store, held at its point at the cell's partial pressure, and gains or loses what its balance leaves (Leighton and Murray 1966); any other ice lies under the frost where the frost lays it, and its trap is the year-mean saturation pressure over it through the days and seasons over the share of the datum's pressure its height keeps: the vapour pressure the datum's air must hold to keep it, the coldest trap's holding the air's (Ingersoll 1970). A basin traps better than a height, its air's higher pressure raising the frost's point (the trapping that gathers Pluto's nitrogen in Sputnik Planitia, Bertrand and Forget 2016): at Mars's 80° north, carbon dioxide ice 5 km down nearly lasts the year (-2 kg/m² a year) where 5 km up it loses 553, and water there needs a sixth of the vapour. On ground at the datum, Mars's water finds its coldest trap at its north pole (2.95e-6 Pa against the south's 3.61e-6), its summer under the aphelion milder than the south's under the perihelion (the north residual cap's water ice, Kieffer et al. 1976); on the model's own Mars, whose south polar band lies lower than its north (-1.75 km against -1.26, where Mars's stands at +3 to +4 km), it finds it in a basin at 88° south. Earth's water finds its poles; Pluto's methane 20° north (Sputnik Planitia's 25°, the low latitudes its tilt of 120° leaves the least of the year's sunlight, Hamilton et al. 2016), Triton's 4° north. Mars's carbon dioxide ice at the seasonal frost's albedo, 0.69, grows only in its deepest polar basins, by 4.4 kg/m² a year over 0.02 percent of the body: the residual south cap's 0.8 (Paige and Ingersoll 1985) waits on the ice's own albedo; Pluto's and Triton's nitrogen grows nowhere at the pressure their frost holds (F239). A fast spinner's traps are run on its 91 latitudes at seven heights spanning its own and read between them, 0.1 to 0.6 s a body. The sidecar gives each ice's coldest trap and the vapour it holds the air at, and the frost's own gas's best growth and the share of the body it would grow on. Checked (`climate::tests`): the coldest traps of Mars's and Earth's water and Pluto's methane; a basin against a height. Known limitations: clean water ice's albedo, 0.83, holds Mars's air's vapour at 2.4e-6 Pa where it holds some 0.1 (Smith 2002), the residual cap's dust (albedo some 0.45, Kieffer et al. 1976) waiting on 5D's; the ice's thermal inertia is its regolith's; the traps rank where the inventory's ice would go, and the caps that fill them come next, with Mars's preset's 8 m of carbon dioxide ice to be checked against the some 0.08 m of its residual cap and buried deposits (Phillips et al. 2011).
- **F241 The caps of the inventory's ices.** The inventory's ices were counted and never laid: Mars's 25 m of water, Pluto's 5 m and Triton's 2 m of methane stood nowhere on their ground. Each ice of the inventory but the frost's own gas now lays caps in its cold traps (F240; `stages::caps::lay`), the least volatile first, each on the ground the last left. It covers the places from its coldest trap on, each place's trap its region's on the climate's grid, read smoothly between its cells: a cap kilometres thick buries the relief under it, which decides where it stands no more. Its surface stands as the lowest of the plastic profiles from the margin of the ground it covers, the margin's ground plus (2 tau d / (rho g))^(1/2) at a distance d in (Nye 1951), at a yield stress tau of 50 kPa, the least of the basal shear stresses under the ice sheets, 50 to 150 (Cuffey and Paterson 2010), a stated choice for every ice until its flow comes with 5C; the ground it covers grows until its caps hold the inventory, at the ice's own density. An ice that is the body's working liquid stands only where its ice never melts, and leaves the rest to the sea, which fills with what the caps leave. The caps raise the ground (`LayerSet::ice`), hide its colour by their mass as frost does (F239), reflect in the climate at their albedo by the share of each of its cells they cover, the climate run again over them, and are exported as `ice.exr`, their thickness; the sidecar gives each ice's laid, its share of the body and its thickest. Mars's water lays caps at both poles over 2.4 percent of the body, up to 4.4 km thick, its deepest polar basins filled to their rims (its polar layered deposits stand some 3 km over some 2.6 percent, Zuber et al. 1998; Plaut et al. 2007); its year's mean falls from 211.7 to 211.5 K. Pluto's methane gathers in domes up to 5.3 km over 0.26 percent of the body between 18 and 25° north (its methane-rich bladed terrain stands near the equator, Moores et al. 2017); Triton's in domes up to 3.9 km near its equator. Checked (`stages::caps::tests`): Mars's water in polar caps a few kilometres thick over a few percent of the body; Pluto's methane in its coldest traps rather than over the whole body; a disc of ice standing as Nye's dome. Known limitations: the Earth-like's water lays none, its summer poles at some 295 K melting any ice, its seasons land's until its ocean holds them; the frost's own gas's caps, Mars's carbon dioxide, Pluto's and Triton's nitrogen, hold the air and come next; the caps' ice is clean, Mars's dusty with 5D; their plastic profile stands for their flow (5C); a cap's own height does not yet feed back on its trap; Pluto's methane gathers in its low places where its bladed terrain stands high.
- **F242 The frost's own gas's caps.** The caps of the frost's own gas, Mars's carbon dioxide and Pluto's and Triton's nitrogen, were left unlaid (F241). They are laid now beside the other ices', last, the most volatile, on the caps of the ices under them, their coldest traps those where their ice grows the most (F240); a working liquid that is the frost's own gas lays its ice only where it grows, so that Titan's methane, its air over 90 K holding its ice above its point, lays none. Mars's preset held 8 m of carbon dioxide ice over the whole body, some hundred times what it holds: its residual cap, a few metres thick (Thomas et al. 2016), and the 9,500 to 12,000 km³ buried under its south polar layered deposits (Phillips et al. 2011) come to some 0.12 m of its liquid, which the preset now holds. Mars's carbon dioxide lays its 0.12 m in a basin at 77 to 79° north, up to 0.9 km thick over its water's ice, the basin's air raising its frost's point the most (F240). Pluto's 30 m of nitrogen lays caps up to 5.6 km thick over 1.7 percent of the body, most between 37 and 43° south, where its ice, which loses mass everywhere at the pressure the frost holds (F239), loses the least; Triton's 50 m stands in caps at both poles beyond 75°, up to 7.2 km thick. Checked (`stages::caps::tests`): Mars's carbon dioxide laid last, at its poles on ground at the datum, its whole inventory; Titan's methane none. Known limitations: the caps of the frost's own gas do not yet hold the air: the frost's seasons draw on the atmosphere's own gas (F239), and the caps' exchange with it, which would hold its pressure at their own balance (Leighton and Murray 1966), comes with the volatiles' transport; Mars's residual cap stands at its south pole, high, at an albedo of some 0.8 (Paige and Ingersoll 1985), where the model's Mars's south polar band lies lower than its north and its ice is the seasonal frost's 0.69; Pluto's nitrogen gathered in Sputnik Planitia over its history (Bertrand and Forget 2016), not in its present traps.
- **F243 The poles' shadows.** Ground at an airless pole stood at its flat ground's temperature and its slope's sunlight (F237), with no shadow: the floor of a polar crater that never sees the sun stood near its rim's temperature, 5A's gate met only in part. Ground near a pole, where the star never climbs past 20°, now traces its horizon in 32 directions out to 64 cells over the body's curve (`climate::Downscale::shade`). Where the horizon stands, in every direction, above the highest the star's disc climbs there through the year (`climate::Shade`, from the body's largest tilt through its precession and the disc's angular radius), the ground never sees the star, and stands at what the terrain it sees and the body's own heat hold it at: the terrain's share of its sky, the mean of sin² of its horizon's elevation, at what that terrain radiates through the year, the year's mean light it absorbs, with the body's heat (the bowl-shaped polar craters of Ingersoll et al. 1992, by their view of their walls); the air holds it toward its surroundings as it does a slope. A crater 60 km across and 3 km deep at the Moon's south pole keeps its floor at 41 K, the same crater at 60° south at 188 K (the floors of the Moon's polar craters 25 to 70 K, Paige et al. 2010). On the bodies' own ground, the Moon's craters keep shadowed crescents under their equatorward walls, over 0.21 percent of its surface at 39 K at the coldest (some 0.08 percent measured, Mazarico et al. 2011, with pockets under 25 K); Mercury's over 0.12 percent at 45 K (its polar craters under 50 K in places, Paige et al. 2013); Ceres's over 0.13 percent at 29 K (near a tenth of a percent, Schorghofer et al. 2016); Callisto's, its tilt 3.1°, in a few. A body tilted more than 20° to its star's orbit, Mars, the Earth-like, Titan, keeps none, and its renders are byte-identical. The sidecar gives the share in shadow and the coldest the map holds. Checked (`climate::tests`): a polar crater's floor and the same crater at 60°. Known limitations: the shadow is permanent or none, ground the star sees for part of its day or year taking its slope's sunlight (F237); the terrain a shadow sees radiates its flat ground's year-mean, where its sunlit walls stand warmer; the horizon is traced on the finish's cells, its smallest craters unresolved and a single cell's dip making a shadow near the poles, where the Moon's shadowed share is some three times the measured; the shadows' ice waits on an inventory, the Moon's and Mercury's presets holding none.

Venus's depth is 153, Titan's 4.1, Earth's 1.2, Mars's 0.2: Venus alone stands on F293's profile, and its column is no longer solved. In Venus's thick air, near its adiabat throughout, the solved profile was no smooth function of its ground's temperature: from one body to the next along a sweep of its radius by thousandths its ground leapt between some 696 and 726 K, and its surface's mean age by 0.29 Gyr where the sweep allows 0.06 (principle 15; the user chose the blend, 2026-11-12). On F293's profile a deck placed where its acid condenses rose with the ground and kept its top at about the same temperature whatever the ground's, and a deck held where Venus's measured clouds stand in its air let out 78 W/m² over every ground: the deck well mixed in its mass lets out the more the warmer the ground, and its depth sets Venus's 737 K (F326). Along the sweep Venus's ground now goes from 742.7 to 740.9 K, its surface's mean age by at most 0.046 Gyr a step. Known: above its clouds its air stands at its skin, 192 K, VIRA's 197 at 80 km, and through them on its adiabat, without the deck's own heating.
- **F331 The clouds' kinds.** The climate's water clouds by place and season, from its own air (M66), as a coarse climate model's diagnostic scheme reads them (Slingo 1987, as ECMWF ran it in 1991). The convection's cloud C = 0.246 + 0.125 ln P, P its rain in mm a day, at most 0.8 (CLOUD_CONVECTIVE): its towers 0.4 C of the place (CLOUD_TOWER), its anvils 2 (C - 0.3) (CLOUD_ANVIL; ECMWF began them at C = 0.4, a step this begins nowhere). The large-scale rain's systems cover a share of the place by the same law, and the layers of air near saturation ((r - 0.7) / 0.3)² of it, r the column's saturation outside the towers (CLOUD_LAYER, ECMWF 1991 eq. 2.55), the two overlapping at random: Slingo's own layers came from each model layer's humidity, which a column's mean saturation, some 0.7 to 0.8 under clouds saturated in their own layers, cannot show. The low cloud 0.14 + 0.060 EIS under an inversion of estimated strength EIS (CLOUD_LOW; Wood and Bretherton 2006's seasonal means over Klein and Hartmann's eight regions), none where the air under it stands under half saturated and all of it from seven tenths (CLOUD_LOW_HUMIDITY, as ECMWF's inversion cloud kept off the dry inversions over deserts and the winter pole). A place's rain is the convection's within its overturning cell's reach of the thermal equator and the large-scale's beyond, by degrees over a quarter of the reach (CLOUD_FREE_EDGE). Over open sea the air under the inversion stands at 0.8 of saturation (CLOUD_SURFACE_HUMIDITY), over land and ice at its column's saturation; each takes its own surface's inversion, the open sea's, the sea ice's and the land's by their shares. All kinds overlap at random.
- **F332 The inversion's strength.** EIS = LTS - Gamma_m(850) (z700 - LCL) (Wood and Bretherton 2006): LTS the potential temperature of the free air at 700 hPa's share of the surface pressure less the surface's, Gamma_m the moist adiabat's potential temperature gradient at the mean of the two temperatures at 850 hPa's share (CLOUD_LEVELS), z700 the height of that level over the surface, and the lifting condensation level 125 m a kelvin of the surface air's dew point depression at 0.8 of saturation (CLOUD_LCL_PER_KELVIN, Lawrence 2005). The free air over a place stands at the warmest of its overturning cell's latitudes' mean air within the cell's reach of the thermal equator, as the tropics' weak temperature gradients hold it (Sobel et al. 2001), and at its own latitude's mean air beyond, by degrees over CLOUD_FREE_EDGE of the reach, down its lapse rate: the subtropics' cool seas lie under the deep tropics' warm free air.
- **F333 The clouds' light and infrared.** Each kind reflects the star's light as a conservative layer of optical depth tau = 3 W / (2 rho r_e) (Stephens 1978), W its water's path and r_e its drops' effective radius (CLOUD_RADIUS: 12.5 um over the sea and 10.5 over land, MODIS's modes, King et al. 2013; ice 25 um), reflecting x / (1 + x), x = 0.75 (1 - g) tau, g its drops' asymmetry (CLOUD_ASYMMETRY: Slingo 1989's liquid, Ebert and Curry 1992's ice). The towers hold 1e-4 kg of liquid a kg of their air from their base to the tropopause (CLOUD_TOWER_WATER, ECMWF 1991); the layers and the low cloud 70 g/m² (CLOUD_LIQUID_PATH, the GEWEX assessment's most frequent in-cloud path); the anvils thin cirrus 1.8 deep (CLOUD_ANVIL_DEPTH, the middle of ISCCP's cirrus class), their ice's path from it (CLOUD_ICE_DEPTH). The kinds reflect together what any of them reflects, the light reflected between them and the ground. In the infrared liquid emits 1 - exp(-0.130 W), W in g/m² (Stephens 1978), and ice 1 - exp(-D (0.005 + 1 / r_e) W) (CAM3's fit to Ebert and Curry 1992). To space each kind sends from its top, the higher hiding the lower: the towers and anvils from the tropopause, the layers from 560 hPa's share of the surface pressure and the low cloud from 850's (CLOUD_TOPS, ISCCP's classes), each top's air sending through the clear air above it (the column's heights' table, F293); to the ground from its base through the clear air's window, the lower hiding the higher (CLOUD_BASES). Each effect is the change the clouds make against the clear column: in the albedo over the ground, its open sea at 0.06 (SEA_ALBEDO, Payne 1972), its ice and snow at ICE_ALBEDO, its bare land at ALBEDO_BENEATH; in what the place lets out to space; and in what its air sends down.
- **F334 The clouds as a diagnostic.** The clouds are laid on the climate's places through the year the maps use, from its own air, wind and moisture, and exported (`clouds.exr` and `clouds.png`: R the low cloud's year-mean cover, G the layers', B the anvils', A the towers', overlapping at random; the sidecar's `clouds` block over the body). The climate does not read them yet: it keeps the stated law (F329) until the planet's albedo and greenhouse come from the clouds themselves (M67; the user, 2026-11-13). Their coupling was tried and is not kept. Taken into the year as their departures from the stated law at each place and from their means over the body (the albedo by each place's sunlight, the radiation by its area), so that the planet's means stood where its zero-dimensional balance sets them, they cooled the Earth-like's mean 2.9 K, its rain rose from 1,342 to 1,572 kg/m² a year and its sea's ice at 60° from 0.37 of the year to 0.89 and no longer settled. Their infrared to space did it alone (mean 286.7 K, poles 9 K colder, tropics 1.1 K warmer): its deep convection, under rain of some 18 mm a day where Earth's is 6 to 7 (the Earth-like is nearly an aquaplanet, whose GCMs rain 10 to 20), keeps 67 W/m² beyond the stated law where Earth's clouds keep some 10, and with the mean held the poles lose some 23. Their albedo alone cooled it 0.6 K, their downward infrared alone warmed its poles 6 K. Whether the stated law's brightness lies over ice is itself unknown (North 1975's 0.62 holds clouds): held over every ground, the polar sea's ice opened at 85° in summer; held off the ice, the poles froze. M67 must first bring the deep convection's high cloud to the amount ISCCP measures and the aquaplanet GCMs give. The Earth-like's clouds on the year the maps use: cover 0.50 (some 0.67 measured), 0.59 over its deep convection, 0.29 over its subtropics, 0.61 in its storm tracks, some 0.8 at its poles, its subtropical land clear; their albedo 0.189 over its ground and 41 W/m² kept from space (CERES's -46 and +28), their downward infrared 31.6 (some 27 measured, Kato et al. 2018). The locked ocean world's gather under its star, 0.77 there and 0.03 over its night (Yang et al. 2013). TRAPPIST-1e's cover 0.15 of it, under its star and over its night's land, whose columns stand near saturation at some 230 K where its frozen sea's stand at 0.1 (known: such a column holds some 0.15 kg/m² of vapour, too little for the deck it draws; the clouds' water by their column's is M67's).
- **F335 The clouds' water held to their air.** Each kind's water came from a stated path whatever its air held: TRAPPIST-1e's night, whose columns hold some 0.15 kg/m² of vapour at 230 K, lay under a full deck of 70 g/m². The low cloud's and the layers' water is now the adiabatic water of a deck of its depth, F Gamma_ad h² / 2 (Albrecht et al. 1990), Gamma_ad the water their air condenses a metre it rises at their base (`clouds::adiabatic_water`, within 2 percent of the research notes' table from 300 to 230 K and of Albrecht's measured 2.5 g/m³ a km), h the field cases' median stratocumulus, 320 m, for the low cloud and the midlatitudes', 420 m, for the layers (Wood 2012), F the 0.63 of two years' retrievals (Merk et al. 2016): at 285 K a low deck holds 67 g/m², the 70 the in-cloud paths most often take, and at 230 K 2.7. The towers hold ECMWF's 1e-4 kg/kg of their air where their base holds the tropical oceans' 19.5 g/kg, a share of what their ascent condenses (CLOUD_TOWER_SHARE). Their water is liquid by degrees from 241 to 271 K, half at 256 K as CALIPSO's tops imply (McCoy et al. 2016), ice's optics below (CLOUD_PHASE). CLOUD_LIQUID_PATH and CLOUD_TOWER_WATER retired.

The convection's high cloud is now as ISCCP-H measures it against GPCP's seasonal rain over the tropical oceans (2001 to 2018, this project's fit from public climatologies, `research/research_notes`): all of it 0.054 + 0.185 ln P, P in mm a day, its towers (optical depth over 23) 0.0095 P as Kubar et al. (2007) found them proportional to the rain (CLOUD_HIGH, CLOUD_THICK); and the anvils thicken with the rain as its thin share falls from 69 percent at 1.5 mm a day to 23 at 17, a share 0.23 + 0.022 P of them medium-thick at ISCCP's middle depth, 9.1, the rest thin cirrus of 1.8 (CLOUD_ANVIL_MEDIUM). CLOUD_TOWER and CLOUD_ANVIL retired; Slingo's law now the large-scale rain's systems' alone.
- **F337 The anvils' place.** The anvils reach from the foot of ISCCP's high class, 440 hPa, whose cloud F336 counts, to the tropopause, and let out from the middle of the span in the pressure's logarithm (CLOUD_BASES; near the 220 K the tropics' anvils' tops stand at, Hartmann and Larson 2002), the towers from the tropopause.

- **F339 The year under its clouds.** Built and checked, not yet the finish's (F341): a coupling carrying a year's clouds (`Coupling::with_clouds`) runs the year after it under them (`EnergyBalance::under`). Its air's streams its clear air's; each place's own ground, its sea at SEA_ALBEDO, its land at its bedrock's mature colour (the Earth-like's felsic 0.207), its ice and snow brightened over them, its caps over its land by their share (the Climate keeps them); its clouds' deck at each place through each step, their reflection by the delta-Eddington layer at the sun's height (Joseph et al. 1976) and their infrared at each step from the air as it stands, what the air loses changed by what they change of what leaves to space and to the ground, the ground taking what they send down. A first try with every absorber over the clouds ran away to 319 K, the vapour darkening the clouds' own light as it warmed.

The diagnostic's Earth-like: cover 0.465, 0.49 over its deep convection, 0.26 over its subtropics, 0.61 in its storm tracks; its clouds' albedo 0.146 over its ground under its air, 31.6 W/m² kept from space where M66's kept 41.1 (CERES 26 to 28), 29.0 sent down (some 27); its poles' 8 (CERES 6 to 14). - **F298 The air's spectrum.** The column's water vapour, methane and nitrogen were grey (F293), their depths set by the outgoing radiation alone, which said nothing of what the air sends down to its ground: Earth's ground took 207 W/m² from its air where 342 is measured, and Titan's ground saw space through a window of 0.30 of its own radiation where some 0.04 is real, so that under its own budget (next) Titan's ground would stand colder than its air and take dew. The user chose the published bands first (2026-11-08). The column now gives three streams over its ground: what it lets out to space, the share of the ground's own radiation that reaches space through it, and what its air sends down, each layer emitting into the transmission below it as into the transmission above it, its source linear in its depth (Clough et al. 1992), the mean of its levels' where it is thin and its edge's where it is thick, where each layer emitted at its middle's temperature (Venus's ground lost 138 W/m² net through its thick lowest layer, 109 now, some 1 K of its ground); the graph tabulates the window and the downward stream beside the outgoing share (`atmosphere.window_share`, `atmosphere.downward_share`), the three from one table of each column. Water vapour absorbs by its bands (Williams 2026, the same coarse-grained HITRAN2016 fit as carbon dioxide's band): its rotation band 37 m²/kg at 500 hPa, flat to 200 cm^-1 and falling by e every 56 cm^-1 above it; its vibration-rotation band 5 m²/kg, flat from 1,450 to 1,700 cm^-1, falling by e every 37 cm^-1 below and 52 above; both broadened as the pressure; its continuum 0.004 m²/kg to 1,700 cm^-1 and 2e-4 to 2,500, by its own pressure against 224.92 Pa and e^(0.02 (260 - T)). Earth's clear air at 288 K lets out 268.5 W/m² (CERES 267, Wild et al. 2019) and sends down 293 (314: its foreign continuum, ozone and the trace gases absent), its window 0.23 (0.17 measured, Costa and Shine 2012). The clouds and the air's other absorbers, its ozone and trace gases, stated until the weather's clouds (5G), are grey and well mixed in the air's mass, a u^b of the column's vapour u: 0.1625 u^0.2346, set by Earth's zero-dimensional 291.6 K (F21) and its outgoing radiation's 2.1 W/m² per kelvin as the grey water was (F235); its air then sends down 341.5 W/m² where 342 is measured, its window 0.11 between the clear 0.17 and the cloudy 0.055, its clouds' effect 38.5 W/m² at the top where CERES's 28 leaves some 10 to the ozone and trace gases they stand for; over a 250 K ground it lets out 169 W/m² (the Arctic some 190 over its 258 K). Two forms were tried and dropped: clouds lying as the vapour lies sent down 390 W/m² and took the slope to 1.3; clouds filling as the vapour grows, tau_0 (1 - e^(-u / u_0)), kept both anchors only by thinning to nothing in a dry polar air, which let out 179 W/m² over a 250 K ground, and the Earth-like froze to 280.8 K, its sea at 65 degrees north iced all year and the water's ice in its tropics. The gases' collisions absorb by HITRAN's tables, coarse-grained by 10 cm^-1 between their temperatures (data/collisions.txt): nitrogen's (Chistikov et al. 2019), nitrogen and methane's (Finenko et al. 2022, fitted to Cassini's spectra of Titan), methane's (Borysow and Frommhold 1987), nitrogen and hydrogen's (Borysow and Frommhold 1986), where methane's and nitrogen's grey depths stood: over Titan's 93.7 K ground its air lets 3.4 percent of the ground's radiation out (3.8 on the Huygens profile) and sends down 3.92 W/m² (3.92 to 3.98), the ground losing 0.46 W/m² net (0.379, McKay et al. 1989). Titan's greenhouse is then 29 K where McKay et al. 1991 find 21: its troposphere stands on the adiabat from its ground, colder aloft than Titan's, which is radiative above some kilometres (known). Its methane haze absorbs the light high over the air and its stratosphere sends a share of it back to space, McKay et al. 1991's anti-greenhouse, never warming the ground or the air under it: the haze 8.128 deep in the light per unit of its index, taking 1.5 of Titan's 3.6 W/m² over the lit hemisphere as its stratosphere does (Robinson and Catling 2012; the Huygens probe's radiometers found 40 percent of the light absorbed above 80 km), and 0.703 of what it takes sent back to space, between the half a stratosphere thin in the infrared sends and all of it, set by Titan's 93.7 K: its anti-greenhouse 17 K where McKay's is 9, as its greenhouse is the stronger (`atmosphere.sunlight_returned`; the energy balance's star's light the less by it, alike at every place until the sun's height splits the light). Venus's sulfuric clouds set again by its 737 K, 0.345 per kg/m² where 0.348; Mars at 216.5 K where 215.5, its column's 0.05 kg/m² of vapour, too much for Mars, whose humidity is its cold traps' (known, F293), making thin clouds. Past water's runaway plateau the outgoing radiation is held at its highest (Simpson 1927; Nakajima et al. 1992), where its vapour's growth stalled it within 0.1 percent at some 355 K (found by the balance's invariant that it rises with the warmth). Titan's year stands at 94.0 K, its ground 0.35 K warmer at its equator than its poles, as before, its methane rain 811 kg/m² a year where 1,172 under the light its haze now returns: the ground's own budget is the next milestone. The Earth-like's year stands at 284.3 K where 285.9, its pole's sea ice 5.4 m thick by the end of its winter where 0.5 to 4.4 are measured (known: its poles let out what the Arctic's air does over its ground, and its ice takes none of its air's warmth until the ground's own budget); TRAPPIST-1e at 254 K where 252, its sea open over 0.77 of its dayside where THAI's models leave almost half (known: the stated clouds warm its wet dayside without the brightness its clouds give it, 5G).
- **F39 Viscous relaxation of an ice shell.** A load of wavelength λ relaxes on τ = 4π η / (ρ g λ) with η the viscosity of the ice at depth 0.16 λ into the shell, along the conductive profile T(z) = T_s (T_m / T_s)^(z/d), η = 1e14 Pa s x exp((60 kJ/mol / R)(1/T − 1/T_m)) x 10^(12 f) for a crust with non-ice share f; loads shorter than 4 times the elastic thickness ride on the lid and do not relax, and the share that relaxes rises to 1 at twice that. The band low-passes are area averages (a sampled pyramid let one cell stand for a patch). Where the shell is thinned (F43) the topography relaxes towards the thinned shell's equilibrium surface rather than treating the depression as a load, and a renewed terrain drops the wavelengths shorter than its renewal length and relaxes the rest with its neighbours. The loop applies the survival per wavelength band at every step with the shell as the thermal history has it; replayed craters relax by the same law over their lives.
- **F40 Sublimation lag and frost.** Ice sublimes at the latitude's mean temperature T = (S (1 − A) cos φ / π σ)^(1/4) at the Hertz-Knudsen rate of its vapour pressure (Murphy and Koop 2005); the non-ice share of the regolith stays as lag, which chokes its growth to the square root of time and is opaque past 2,000 kg/m²; each cell sits at its own temperature, the body's Bond albedo scaled by the cell's reflectance against the mature surface's, so a fresh bright surface is cold and keeps its brightness until gardening takes it (Callisto's young craters) while a dark lag warms and grows; clean excavated ice is gardened back in over 0.5 Gyr; the ice the equator lost condenses where the mean temperature is below 105 K, covering up to 45% there, in proportion to the square of the regolith's cleanliness (a carbonaceous regolith gardens the frost in, so Ceres and Callisto show none and Ganymede six tenths).
- **F38 Rift population.** On each plume rise the extension E = tectonics.rift_extension x (rise height / nominal height) is spent along a transect 0.7 x 2π a long as Σ heave_i x L_i, the troughs drawn from N(>L) ∝ L^−2 between 50 km and 1.5 rise diameters with depth 1.5e−3 L (log-normal scatter 0.4, capped at the uplift), width 30 D and heave 1.2 D; half of them join an existing trough chosen by length as an en-echelon neighbour 0.8 to 3.5 widths to its side. Troughs wider than two coarse cells are carved through the loop over 1 Gyr, the rest cut at the finish; the rifting follows the dome on a stagnant lid and the resurfacing event on an episodic lid.
- **F37 Steady-flux production.** After the heavy bombardment the lunar production function falls two cumulative slopes steeper above 150 km and makes nothing over 600 km; each crater draws from the early or steady population by the early share r e^(−t/τ) / (1 + r e^(−t/τ)) of the flux at its time (F31).
- **F34 Threshold friction speed.** u*_t = 0.1 sqrt(ρ_particle g d / ρ_air) for sand-size grains (Iversen and White 1982, large-particle limit); d = 200 µm default.
